Massive holomorphicity of near-critical dimers and sine-Gordon model
Nathanaël Berestycki, Scott Mason, Lucas Rey

Abstract
In this paper, we consider the near-critical dimer model in the setup of isoradial superpositions with Temperleyan boundary conditions. We show that the centered height function converges as the mesh size tends to zero to a limiting field which agrees with the (electromagnetically tilted) sine-Gordon model, whose derivative correlations are described by Grassmann variables (or equivalently determinants involving a massive Dirac operator). This answers a longstanding question in the field. A crucial part of the work is to develop a notion of discrete massive holomorphic functions and the tools to study such functions, in particular finding an exact discrete form of the massive Cauchy–Riemann equations, which is satisfied by the inverse Kasteleyn matrix. In comparison with previous studies, a key novelty of this part of our work is that the mass is not only allowed to be non-constant but can be complex-valued.



1
Introduction; main results

1.1
Background and overview

The dimer model, introduced by Kasteleyn
[
20
]
and independently Fisher and Temperley
[
39
]
in the 1960s, is one of the most classical models of statistical mechanics. Given a finite, planar bipartite graph
G=(V,E)
, a dimer configuration
\mathbf{m}\subset E
is a perfect matching of
G
, i.e., a subset of edges such that each vertex
v\in V
is incident to exactly one edge
e\in\mathbf{m}
. We let
\mathcal{D}(G)
denote the set of dimer configurations on
G
. If edges of the graph
G
are furthermore weighted (let
c_{e}>0
denote the weight or conductance of the undirected edge
e
), then we form a probability measure on the set of dimer configurations by setting

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{m})=\frac{1}{Z}\prod_{e\in\mathbf{m}}c_{e},

where the constant
Z=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{D}(G)}\prod_{e\in\mathbf{m}}c_{e}
, the
partition function
, is chosen so that
\mathbb{P}
is a probability measure. We will always assume that
Z>0
(i.e., the set of dimer configurations
\mathcal{D}(G)
is nonempty).

When
G
is a portion of the square lattice, or more generally when it is an isoradial graph (or more precisely, an isoradial superposition, see definitions below) it is well known that there is a natural choice of weights where the dimer model is
critical
: e.g., dimer-dimer correlations decay polynomially with the distance, and the associated dimer height function becomes conformally invariant in the scaling limit (and in an appropriate gauge), in fact converges to a multiple of the Gaussian free field with appropriate boundary conditions in the scaling limit where the mesh size
\varepsilon\to 0
. This occurs e.g. on the square lattice when the weights are uniform (
c_{e}\equiv 1
) and the graph has
Temperleyan boundary conditions
, which will be defined below more precisely; this was the content of Kenyon’s groundbreaking results
[
22
,
23
]
. On isoradial superpositions when the weights are inherited from Kenyon’s critical weights
[
21
]
this was obtained in the full plane in
[
16
]
(generalised to domains with Temperleyan boundary conditions in
[
26
]
under additional assumptions on the boundary, and substantially more generally in
[
7
]
).

In this paper we are concerned with the
near-critical dimer model
, in which the edge weights deviate from the above critical weights by an amount which converges to 0 as
\varepsilon\to 0
at a suitably chosen speed. The goal of this paper will be to prove that in this case it is still possible to obtain a nontrivial scaling limit. Characteristic features of this scaling limit are that on scales smaller than macroscopic it is indistinguishable from the above critical scaling limit: in particular, at such scales dimer-dimer correlations will still decay polynomially fast with the distance, and the height function is locally a Gaussian free field. However, at macroscopic scales and beyond, dimer-dimer correlations decay exponentially fast, and the height function is no longer Gaussian in the scaling limit. In other words it is given by a
massive field theory
. The main goal of this paper is to identify precisely the limiting height function, which we show is given by the so-called
sine-Gordon model with electromagnetic field
, a model from Quantum Field Theory (QFT) which is integrable but non-conformal. Questions of this nature go back in the physics literature at least to the work of Lukanov
[
28
]
who considered the six-vertex model close to the critical point and suggested that in a suitable limit the scaling is identical to that of the sine-Gordon model.

Let us briefly introduce the model we will be working with in the case of the square lattice (the model will be described in a more general framework of isoradial superpositions below). Given a bounded, simply connected domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{C}
and a graph
G_{\varepsilon}\subset\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}^{2}
which is a Temperleyan approximation of
\Omega
, the basic parameter of the near-critical deformation we consider will be a
vector field

\alpha:\bar{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
which is assumed to be smooth up to and including the boundary. We will assume that this vector derives from a potential:

\alpha=\nabla V,\text{ where }V:\bar{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}

(1.1)

Given such a potential
V
, we define near-critical weights in
G_{\varepsilon}
in the following manner. Consider the standard black-white chessboard decomposition of
\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Around every other black vertex
b
(See Figure
1
below), if
w
is a neighbouring white vertex (thus
|w-b|=\varepsilon
), set

\displaystyle c_{e}

\displaystyle=e^{2(V(w)-V(b))}=1+2\langle\nabla V,w-b\rangle+o(\varepsilon)

(1.2)

where
e
is the undirected edge
e=(bw)
.

In the case where
\alpha(z)\equiv\alpha_{0}
is constant throughout
\Omega
(and when
\Omega
is actually equal to the whole plane) such a model was introduced by Chhita in
[
14
]
, who showed among other things that the limiting height function cannot be Gaussian since its moments do not satisfy the Wick relation. The same model was further considered in
[
30
]
who observed that the two-point correlation of the derivative of the height function match those of the sine-Gordon model in the full plane. Roughly simultaneously and independently, the above model was introduced (in a domain
\Omega
and with nonconstant vector field
\alpha
) in
[
6
]
. There, this model was studied from the perspective of Temperley’s bijection. In particular it was shown there that under the assumption that
V
is
log-convex
, paths in the Temperleyan tree have a nontrivial scaling limit, which in the case where
\alpha\equiv\alpha_{0}
is just constant coincides with Makarov and Smirnov’s
Massive SLE
2
(
[
29
,
13
]
– in fact this massive SLE had been introduced independently and slightly before by Bauer, Bernard and Kytöla in
[
2
]
; see also
[
1
]
). A precise conjecture was also formulated there for the scaling limit of the height function: namely, the authors conjectured that in the scaling limit, the height function is given by a multiple (namely,
\sqrt{2}
) times a field whose law is

\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}(\mathrm{d}\phi)=\exp\left(z_{0}\int_{\Omega}\left\langle e^{\mathbf{i}\frac{1}{\chi}\phi(x)},\alpha(x)\right\rangle\mathrm{d}x\right)\mathbb{P}^{\text{GFF}}(\mathrm{d}\phi),

(1.3)

where
\mathbf{i}=\sqrt{-1}
(here and everywhere in the paper),
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
is an unspecified constant,
\chi=1/\sqrt{2}
is the
Imaginary Geometry
constant associated with
\kappa=2
, and we identify complex numbers with vectors in multiple ways (thus
\langle z,w\rangle=\Re(z\bar{w})
is the standard Euclidean inner product), and
\mathbb{P}^{\mathrm{GFF}}
is the law of a Gaussian free field (see
[
10
]
for a general introduction). We note for future reference that in (
1.3
) (as well as in (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) below) the height function is implicitly defined with respect to a reference flow is the winding flow of, say,
[
8
,
9
]
and whose total mass is
2\pi
; in other words this height function is
2\pi
larger than the one we will be working with in this paper. In fact, this conjecture forgot to take into account a factor of
\mathbf{i}
in
\alpha
(or, equivalently, an additive factor of
\pi/2
in the limiting height function), as we will explain.

In (
1.3
) it is also important to note that the authors were dealing with an uncentered height function (i.e., the height function was defined with respect to the so-called winding reference flow, see
[
7
,
8
,
9
]
) and the height of the Gaussian free fied is normalised according to the conventions of imaginary geometry, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}^{\text{GFF}}[\phi(x)\phi(y)]=-\log|x-y|+O(1)
. However it is more common in the literature concerning sine-Gordon to normalise the field differently: namely we use the law
\mathbb{P}^{\text{GFF}\#}
to denote a GFF in which
\mathbb{E}^{\text{GFF}\#}[\phi(x)\phi(y)]=-(2\pi)^{-1}\log|x-y|+O(1)
. Then the law (
1.3
) in the conjecture from
[
6
]
can be rewritten (up to multiplication by a factor
\sqrt{2}\times\sqrt{2\pi}=2\sqrt{\pi}
, and after correcting the conjecture to include the factor of
\mathbf{i}
) as the law

\mathbb{P}_{\text{SG}(\alpha)}(\mathrm{d}\phi)=\exp\left(z_{0}\int_{\Omega}\left\langle e^{\mathbf{i}\sqrt{4\pi}\phi(x)},{\mathbf{i}\alpha(x)}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}x\right)\mathbb{P}^{\text{GFF}\#}(\mathrm{d}\phi).

(1.4)

We note that, formally, in the case when
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the whole plane and
\alpha
is constant (which can then be taken to be purely imaginary by rotation invariance, so
\alpha\equiv-\mathbf{i}\alpha_{0}
, say), the law (
1.4
) coincides with the law

\mathbb{P}_{\text{SG}(\alpha)}(\mathrm{d}\phi)=\exp\left(z_{0}\int_{\Omega}\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\phi(x))\mathrm{d}x\right)\mathbb{P}^{\text{GFF}\#}(\mathrm{d}\phi),

(1.5)

where the value of
z_{0}
has changed to incorporate that of
\alpha_{0}
.
Once again, we point out that, although it is not explicitly written in
[
6
]
, in the above conjecture it is important to note that the GFF of the law
\mathbb{P}^{\text{GFF}\#}
does not have zero boundary conditions: instead, it has
winding
boundary conditions (see (2.7) in
[
7
]
).

This is, at least formally, the so-called
sine-Gordon field at the free fermion point
. In the general case where
\alpha
is non-constant, the law (
1.4
) can be viewed intuitively as that of a perturbed sine-Gordon field, in which the electromagnetic field
\alpha
(deriving from the potential
V
) tilts the field so that
e^{\mathbf{i}\sqrt{4\pi}\phi}
points in the same direction as
\alpha
.

The main goal of this paper will be to prove this conjecture under certain assumptions on the potential
V
. To state it, let us first point out that actually defining the sine-Gordon model from (
1.5
) is in itself highly nontrivial. In the full plane, this was achieved by the breakthrough work of Bauerschmidt and Webb
[
4
]
. In a bounded domain (in fact, in the unit disc) subject to certain boundary conditions, this is the subject of a work by Park, Virtanen and Webb
[
32
]
. In both cases, a prominent feature (both a property of the field and a tool used in its construction) is the so-called
Coleman transform
, which expresses the fermionic nature of the field (and will thus ultimately yield a massive extension of the Boson-Fermion correspondence for the dimer model). This leads to concrete expressions for the moments of the sine-Gordon field, of the type

\mathbb{E}_{\text{SG}(\alpha)}((\bar{\phi},f)^{n})=m_{n}(f),

(1.6)

where
\bar{\phi}=\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\text{SG}(\alpha)}(\phi)
,
f
is a test function, and
m_{n}
is the sum of
n
-fold integrals of certain kernels
\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
(which can be interpreted as the weak holomorphic and antiholomorphic derivative correlations); here
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
are pairwise disjoint points of the domain
\Omega
, while
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
indicates whether we consider the holomorphic or antiholomorphic derivative. These kernels can be expressed in terms of
Grassmann variables, or, equivalently, in terms of determinants involving a massive Dirac operator. (Concrete expressions for the correlation kernels
\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}
, and thus for
m_{k}(f)
, will be given below).

We state our main theorem below, in a slightly informal manner for now.
Let
\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}
denote the expectation for the dimer model in
\Omega
with weights from (
1.2
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Suppose
\Omega
is a bounded simply connected domain. Let
h_{\varepsilon}
denote the centered height function associated to the dimer model on the Temperleyan graph
G_{\varepsilon}\subset\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cap\Omega
, associated with weights as in (
1.2
). Suppose that the potential
V
is log-convex, and satisfies additional assumptions. Then for any smooth test function
f
with compact support in
\Omega
, for any
k\geq 1
,
\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}((h_{\varepsilon},f)^{n})\to m_{n}(f)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
m_{n}(f)
are the expressions in (
1.6
).
Furthermore the moments
m_{n}(f)
identify uniquely the corresponding law.

In the above result the height function is defined with respect to the reference flow
\omega
such that
\omega(wb)
is the probability that the edge
wb
belongs to a random dimer configuration.

See Theorem
1.19
for a more precise statement.
In fact, we obtain this result in the more general setting of
double
isoradial graphs (also known as isoradial superpositions) with appropriate edge weights, which will be detailed below.

We emphasise that the work of Park, Virtanen and Webb
[
32
]
gives a rigorous construction of the sine-Gordon model from (
1.5
) and proves rigorously (
1.6
) in the case of the unit disc. Thus, combining their work with our Theorem
1.1
, this identifies the (centred) sine-Gordon model at the free fermion point
\beta=4\pi
as the limit of the centered height function for near-critical dimers, thereby resolving Conjecture (
1.5
), under the assumptions in the statement on the potential
V
. On the other hand, our work can be read independently from
[
32
]
and proves the convergence of the height function to a field whose moments match with the
Coleman form
of the sine-Gordon model: concretely, Theorem
4.7
up to (
4.47
) does not use any input from
[
32
]
. It is only at the very end that
see Section
1.6
for more details.

Physically, the result in Theorem
1.1
corresponds to a massive extension of the
boson-fermion correspondence
embodied by Kenyon’s convergence result
[
22
,
23
]
(note that the GFF has Gaussian correlations which are thus bosonic by virtue of Wick’s formula, whereas the dimer correlations are determinantal/Pfaffian, hence fermionic).

Heuristics and connection to imaginary geometry.

Although few details are given in
[
6
]
about this conjecture, it is in fact very natural from an imaginary geometry perspective. We now briefly explain this heuristics. We consider the Radon-Nikodym derivative of the near-critical dimer model defined by (
1.2
) with respect to the critical case where
c_{e}\equiv 1
, and argue that it is approximately given by (
1.3
). Let
\Gamma
denote the class of black vertices around which the edge weights are not equal to one (as defined by the Temperleyan, biperiodic structure of the edge weights). Note that if
\mathbf{m}
is a given dimer configuration, then

\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}(\mathbf{m})\propto\left(\prod_{e\in\mathbf{m}}c_{e}\right)\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,0)}(\mathbf{m})\approx\exp\left(2\sum_{e=(b,w)\in\mathbf{m},b\in\Gamma}\langle\nabla V,w-b\rangle\right)\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,0)}(\mathbf{m})

from (
1.2
). (We used here the Temperleyan nature of the weights, in that edges
e=(b,w)
with
b\not\in\Gamma
have weight equal to one by definition). Let
\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{m})
denote the wired spanning tree on the graph induced by
\Gamma
associated to
\mathbf{m}
via Temperley’s bijection: i.e., for each
b\in\Gamma
, by definition,
\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{m})
includes the edge
(bb^{\prime})
, where
b^{\prime}\in\Gamma
is uniquely defined by the requirement that
(bw)\in\mathbf{m}
, with
w
the white vertex midway through
b
and
b^{\prime}
. We write
b^{\prime}=\sigma(b)
for this unique vertex, which is the successor of
b
in the arborescence
\mathcal{T}
. We can then rewrite

\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}(\mathbf{m})\approx\exp\left(\sum_{b\in\Gamma}\langle\nabla V,\overrightarrow{(b,\sigma(b))}\rangle\right)\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,0)}(\mathbf{m})

where
\overrightarrow{(b,\sigma(b))}
is the vector joining
b
to
\sigma(b)
. Dividing by the norm of this vector (necessarily equal to
2\varepsilon
) we get a unit vector which we write as
e^{\mathbf{i}\theta_{b}}
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}(\mathbf{m})

\displaystyle\approx\exp\left(2\varepsilon\sum_{b\in\Gamma}\langle\nabla V,e^{\mathbf{i}\theta_{b}}\rangle\right)\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,0)}(\mathbf{m})=\exp\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{b\in\Gamma}\langle\nabla V,e^{\mathbf{i}\theta_{b}}\rangle\right)\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,0)}(\mathbf{m})

(1.7)

It remains to observe that
e^{\mathbf{i}\theta_{b}}
, the direction of the unique outgoing arrow emanating from
b
in the arborescence
\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{m})
, is nothing else but
e^{\mathbf{i}h_{\varepsilon}(f)}
, where
h_{\varepsilon}
is the height function with respect to the winding reference flow (in units of
2\pi
), and
f
is a face adjacent to
b
, chosen by convention so that
b
is the top-left corner of
f
. This follows from the fact that in Temperley’s bijection, the height function measures the winding of the branches in the spanning tree
\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{m})
(see in particular Proposition 4.4 in
[
8
]
for this formulation of Temperley’s bijection). Approximating the Riemann sum in (
1.7
) by an integral, we deduce

\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}(\mathbf{m})\approx\exp\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\langle\nabla V(x),e^{\mathbf{i}h_{\varepsilon}(x)}\rangle\mathrm{d}x\right)\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,0)}(\mathbf{m}).

(1.8)

Recall that, under the uniform law
\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,0)}
, by Kenyon’s result
[
22
]
(see also
[
7
, Theorem 1.2]
, as here we are working with the non-centered field),
h_{\varepsilon}\to\tfrac{1}{\chi}{\phi}
, where
\phi
has the law of a Gaussian free field with winding boundary conditions,
+\pi/2
(this extra factor of
\pi/2
accounts for the fact that the height function measures the intrinsic winding of the path in the tree connecting the point to the reference boundary vertex; and this path has an extra turn of
\pi/2
when it hits the boundary; see, e.g., Theorem 3.1 or Theorem 5.1 in
[
7
]
, bearing in mind that on the square lattice, the local mean winding of the uniform spanning tree,
m^{\#\delta}
, tends to 0).

Furthermore, we have (by definition of the imaginary chaos, see e.g.
[
19
]
)

\varepsilon^{-\tfrac{1}{2\chi^{2}}}\int_{\Omega}\langle\nabla V(x),e^{\mathbf{i}\tfrac{1}{\chi}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)+\mathbf{i}\pi/2}\rangle\mathrm{d}x\to-\int_{\Omega}\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla V(x),e^{\mathbf{i}\tfrac{1}{\chi}\phi(x)}\rangle\mathrm{d}x.

(1.9)

Since
\chi=1/\sqrt{2}
(equivalently, since we are at the free fermion point
\beta=4\pi
), the power of
\varepsilon
in the factor in front of the integral above is simply
\varepsilon^{-1}
, which matches exactly the power of
\varepsilon
in (
1.8
). (Note that in reality, (
1.9
) only holds strictly below the free fermion point. At the free fermion point the integral itself does not make literal sense; this is related to the fact that the sine-Gordon model is not expected to be absolutely continuous with respect to the Gaussian free field at the free fermion point.)

Making the substitution, we finally find:

\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}(\mathbf{m})\approx\exp\left(-2\int_{\Omega}\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla V(x),e^{\mathbf{i}\tfrac{1}{\chi}\phi(x)}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}x\right)\mathbb{P}^{\text{GFF}}(\mathrm{d}\phi)

(1.10)

This is indeed a discrete form of (
1.3
) with
z_{0}=-2
.

Discrete and continuous massive holomorphicity.

While the reasoning leading to (
1.10
) is very suggestive and appealing, it appears difficult to turn it into a proof at this stage using currently available technology. On the other hand, we will approach this conjecture via a totally different perspective. Our approach generalizes the approach of
[
23
]
to the near-critical setting: we develop a theory of
massive holomorphicity
and use it to compute exactly the limit of the so-called inverse Kasteleyn matrix, which governs dimer-dimer correlations. The fact that the inverse Kasteleyn matrix is not discrete holomorphic but is instead
massive
holomorphic in the scaling limit is one of the main challenges in the analysis.
In turn, after explicit calculations, we will show that the moments of the height function integrated against a given test function are given by formulas which match those of the above sine-Gordon model, at least when the latter is expressed in terms of fermionic integrals (something which is possible due to the already mentioned Coleman transform, a feature of the fact that we consider the sine-Gordon model at its free fermion point).

Discrete holomorphicity is one of the most useful tools to study critical models.
In
[
29
]
, Makarov and Smirnov introduced discrete massive martingale observables and advocated the use of a
discrete massive holomorphicity theory
to study near-critical models.
In the context of the Ising model, this was first partially implemented in
[
18
]
, and a theory of convergence of discrete massive holomorphic observables towards their continuous counterpart was developed in
[
33
,
31
]
and used to study the near-critical FK-Ising model. Because our near-critical perturbation depends on a vector field
\alpha
rather than single real number (“mass”) we will need to formulate a new notion of massive holomorphicity (which reduces to previously studied notions when the vector field is constant and, viewed as a complex-valued function, is purely real-valued).

In the context of the dimer model, a first form of discrete massive holomorphicity theory was developed in
[
17
]
with the introduction of a massive discrete Dirac operator on double isoradial graphs with
Z
-invariant weights. In
[
36
]
this theory is extended to double isoradial graphs with more general weights, and is applied in the near-critical setting to generalize to double isoradial graphs the results of
[
6
]
on the convergence of the near-critical dimer height function.

1.2
Isoradial setup; discrete massive harmonic functions

Let
\Gamma^{\infty}=\Gamma^{\infty}_{\varepsilon}
denote an infinite isoradial graph with mesh
\varepsilon
. This means that it can be embedded in the plane in such a way that every face is inscribed in a circle of radius
\varepsilon
, and such that the circumcenter of each face lies in the closure of the face. Each pair of dual edges form the diagonals of a rhombus.

We will everywhere assume that this graph has the
bounded angle property
: there exists
\eta>0
such that all rhombi angles are in
[\eta,\pi/2-\eta]
.
One classical consequence of the bounded angle assumption (see for example Assumption 1 of
[
37
]
) is that for any
x,y\in\Gamma^{\infty}
we can choose a path
\gamma_{\#}:x\to y
in
\Gamma^{\infty}
of length
\leq\frac{C|y-x|}{\varepsilon}
where the constant
C
depends only on
\eta
.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\varepsilon}
be a finite simply connected subgraph of
\Gamma^{\infty}
, denote by
\overline{\Gamma}
the union of
\Gamma
and all adjacent vertices, by
\Gamma^{*}
the restricted planar dual of
\Gamma
, that is the dual graph from which the dual vertex corresponding to the outer face and all incident dual edges are removed. The set
B=\Gamma\cup\Gamma^{*}
corresponds to the black vertices of a Temperleyan superposition graph
G
(also known as medial graph). That is, we remove one vertex on the boundary of
\Gamma^{*}
, called
b^{*}
.

We denote by
W=B^{*}
the set of white vertices, corresponding to centres of rhombi in the isoradial graph
\Gamma
. Note that in general (e.g. if
\Gamma
is not bipartite) there is only one class of white vertices. When
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\infty}
, we will write
W=W^{\infty}
and
B=B^{\infty}
.

Assumption 1.2
.

We always assume that in the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, the graph
\Gamma
with Temperleyan corner
b^{*}
approximates a fixed open set
\Omega
with boundary point
\beta\in\partial\Omega
, by which we mean

(i)

\Gamma\subset\Omega

(ii)

for every compact subset
\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega
,
\Gamma\cap\mathcal{C}=\Gamma^{\infty}\cap\mathcal{C}

(iii)

for all
\omega\in\partial\Omega
, there exists
x_{\varepsilon}\in\Gamma
such that
x_{\varepsilon}\to\omega

(iv)

b^{*}=b^{*}(\varepsilon)\to\beta^{*}\in\partial\Omega
.

In fact, assumption (iii) easily follows from assumption (ii).

Edge weights.

To define the edge weights on the superposition graph
G
we proceed as follows.

We fix a vector field
\alpha:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, smooth on
\bar{\Omega}
.
As mentioned previously we will assume that
\alpha=\nabla V
derives from a potential
V:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
which is assumed
C^{2}
in
\bar{\Omega}
.

Let
x^{-},x^{+}
be two adjacent vertices of
\Gamma^{\infty}
, and let
y^{-}
and
y^{+}
be the adjacent faces so that
x^{-}y^{-}x^{+}y^{+}
forms a rhombus in the superposition whose center is called
w
.
Let
\theta_{w}=\theta_{x^{-}x^{+}}=\theta
denote the half angle of
\widehat{y^{-}x^{-}y^{+}}
.
Let

c^{0}_{x^{-},x^{+}}=\tan(\theta),

be the
critical weights
on
\Gamma^{\infty}
. We now introduce the
near-critical weights
which we will use throughout the paper. We set

c^{\alpha}_{x^{-},x^{+}}=\exp\Big(V(x^{+})-V(x^{-})\Big)\tan(\theta).

(1.11)

Remark 1.3
.

This differs slightly from the convention in (
1.2
), where (in these notations) we considered
c^{\alpha}_{x^{-},x^{+}}=\exp\Big(\Big(V(w)-V(x^{+})\Big)\tan(\theta)
instead of (
1.11
). However this difference is irrelevant: it can be checked from the results in
[
6
]
that the height functions associated to both weight sequences have the same limit. This also explains why we included a factor of 2 in the exponent of (
1.2
).

Associated with these conductances on
\overline{\Gamma}
we define edge weights on the superposition graph
G
of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\star}
, where
c_{x^{-},w}=c^{\alpha}_{x^{-},x^{+}}
;
c_{x^{+},w}=c^{\alpha}_{x^{+},x^{-}}
, whereas
c_{w,y^{-}}=c_{w,y^{+}}=1
.
See Figure
1
.
On the boundary, that is when
x^{-}\sim x^{+},x^{-}\in\Gamma,x^{+}\in\overline{\Gamma}\setminus\Gamma
, we simply remove the edge
wx^{+}
.
Similarly, if
y\sim b^{*},y\in\Gamma^{\star}
, we simply remove the edge
wb^{*}
.

\begin{overpic}[dimer_weight.pdf]
\put(10.0,25.0){$x^{-}$}
\put(83.0,25.0){$x^{+}$}
\put(50.0,0.0){{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$y^{-}$}}
\put(50.0,45.0){{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$y^{+}$}}
\put(15.0,46.0){$\frac{e^{V(x^{+})}}{e^{V(x^{-})}}c^{0}_{x-,x^{+}}$}
\put(70.0,-2.0){$\frac{e^{V(x^{-})}}{e^{V(x^{+})}}c^{0}_{x^{+},x^{-}}$}
\put(25.0,4.0){$1$}
\put(70.0,44.0){$1$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 1:
Edge weights. (The weights indicated on this picture correspond to the orientation of a given edge from the black vertex towards the white vertex in the middle of the rhombus).

We consider the dimer model on
G
with the above off-critical edge weights. Ultimately we aim to describe the scaling limit of the height function as the mesh size
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Interpretation as drift.

We provide a simple lemma, which shows that the vector field
\alpha
may be viewed as the limiting drift in the diffusion which describes the scaling limit of the random walk on
\bar{\Gamma}
.

Lemma 1.4
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a domain and let
\alpha:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a vector field which is smooth on
\bar{\Omega}
. Then the random walk on
\overline{\Gamma}
with conductances
c^{\alpha}
, killed when exiting
\Gamma
converges to the solution of the above SDE, killed when exiting
\Omega
.

We also define a
mass function

M(x):=\Delta V(x)+\|\nabla V(x)\|^{2}.

(1.12)

This function will play an important role in the rest of this paper. Under the
drift-mass equivalence
observed in
[
6
]
and generalised in
[
36
]
to the isoradial setup, the function
M
will play the role of a mass. This is the main reason for enforcing the following assumption (the log-convexity assumption of
V
ensures precisely that
M(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in\bar{\Omega}
):

Assumption 1.5

(Log-convexity)
.

V
is log-convex throughout
\Omega
: that is,

M(x)=\Delta V(x)+\|\nabla V(x)\|^{2}>0;\quad x\in\bar{\Omega}.

(1.13)

If
V
is positive then this assumption is indeed equivalent to stating that
\log V
is convex on
\Omega
.

1.3
Notion of massive holomorphicity in the continuum

Our first goal will be to state a theorem on the scaling limit for the inverse Kasteleyn matrix
K^{-1}
(see Section
2.2
for the choice of gauge function and corresponding Kasteleyn matrix; see Theorem
1.13
below for the statement on
K^{-1}
). This necessitates the introduction of a notion of massive holomorphic function and some basic notions related to this.

Definition 1.6
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be an open set.

A function
h:U\to\mathbb{R}
is
massive harmonic
(or
M
-massive harmonic) if for all
x\in\Omega
,

[\Delta h](x)=M(x)h(x)

(1.14)

with the function
M
from (
1.12
).

A function
f:U\to\mathbb{C}
is
\alpha
-massive holomorphic
, resp.
-\bar{\alpha}
-massive holomorphic
, if it satisfies the identity:

\overline{\partial}f=\frac{1}{2}\alpha\overline{f}\quad,\text{resp.}\quad\overline{\partial}f=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\alpha}\overline{f}

(1.15)

where we identify here
\alpha=\nabla\varphi
with the complex number
\partial_{x}\varphi+\mathbf{i}\partial_{y}\varphi
(as usual) and the above product on the right hand side is the multiplication of two complex numbers.

Remark 1.7
.

This differs from previously considered notions of massive holomorphicity (e.g.
[
29
,
33
]
) where the corresponding equations were

\Delta h=M^{2}h\quad;\quad\partial\bar{f}=iMf,

for a
constant

M\in\mathbb{R}
(with
M^{2}
often called the
mass
).

The notion introduced in Definition
1.6
is therefore a novel and more general formulation of the notion of massive holomorphicity; note in particular that the “mass” term
M(x)
in (
1.14
) is not simply the square (or even the squared norm) of
\alpha
in (
1.15
).
Ultimately, the novelty comes from the fact that the near-criticality in the dimer model is described by a vector field
\alpha
in the domain, instead of a single real number.

It is worth noting that, in the full plane and with the vector field
\alpha
being constant, the two notions
do
coincide, since (after rotation) we can assume without loss of generality that
\alpha
points in the real positive direction. This will be analogous to the fact that the generalized sine-Gordon field coincides with the (constant-mass) sine-Gordon field in the full plane.

Written explicitly in real coordinates (
1.15
) becomes the
(
\alpha
-), resp. (
-\bar{\alpha}
-), massive Cauchy-Riemann equation

\begin{cases}\partial_{x}u-\alpha_{x}u&=\partial_{y}v+\alpha_{y}v\\
\partial_{y}u-\alpha_{y}u&=-(\partial_{x}v+\alpha_{x}v)\end{cases}\quad\text{, resp.}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{x}u+\alpha_{x}u&=\partial_{y}v+\alpha_{y}v\\
\partial_{y}u-\alpha_{y}u&=-(\partial_{x}v-\alpha_{x}v)\end{cases}

(1.16)

Recalling that
\alpha=\nabla V
, this can be rewritten as

\begin{cases}\partial_{x}(ue^{-V})e^{V}&=e^{-V}\partial_{y}(ve^{V})\\
\partial_{y}(ue^{-V})e^{V}&=-e^{-V}\partial_{x}(ve^{V}).\end{cases}\quad\text{, resp.}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{x}(ue^{V})&=\partial_{y}(ve^{V})\\
\partial_{y}(ue^{-V})&=-\partial_{x}(ve^{-V}).\end{cases}

The
\alpha
-massive Cauchy-Riemann equation can be rewritten more geometrically

e^{V}\nabla(ue^{-V})=e^{-V}(-\mathbf{i})\nabla(ve^{V}),

(1.17)

where the gradient is identified with a complex number. This relation is the massive analogue of the fact that in the critical case, the gradients of
u
and
v
can be obtained from one another by applying a 90 degrees rotation.

In the critical case, the real part of a holomorphic function is harmonic. This extends to the massive case, where the mass
M
is precisely the function introduced in (
1.12
). Furthermore, given a real-valued, massive harmonic function
u
, there is at most one function
v
such that
f=u+\mathbf{i}v
, which by analogy with the critical case we call the
massive harmonic conjugate
of
u
. (More precisely, two massive holomorphic functions
f
and
g
with the same real part coincide). We state these two properties in the lemma below.

Lemma 1.8
.

Suppose
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
is open and connected (not necessarily simply connected).
If
f=u+\mathbf{i}v
is
\alpha
-massive holomorphic in
\Omega
, then
u
is a
M
-massive harmonic function in
\Omega
. Furthermore, the
\alpha
-harmonic conjugate of
u
is at most unique up to an additive constant. That is, suppose
f
and
g
are two massive holomorphic functions with
\Re(f)=\Re(g)
. Then
f-g
is a constant.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\Delta u+\mathbf{i}\Delta v=\Delta f

\displaystyle=4\partial\overline{\partial}f

\displaystyle=2\partial(\alpha\bar{f})

\displaystyle=2\left[(\partial\alpha)\bar{f}+\alpha(\partial\bar{f})\right]

\displaystyle=2\left[2(\partial\overline{\partial}V)\bar{f}+\frac{1}{2}\alpha\bar{\alpha}f\right]

\displaystyle=\Delta V\bar{f}+|\nabla V|^{2}f

\displaystyle=\Delta V(u-\mathbf{i}v)+|\nabla V|^{2}(u+\mathbf{i}v)

\displaystyle=(\Delta V+|\nabla V|^{2})u+\mathbf{i}(-\Delta V+|\nabla V|^{2}))v

\displaystyle=Mu+\mathbf{i}(-\Delta V+|\nabla V|^{2}))v

Identifying the real and imaginary parts, we obtain that
u
is always a
M
-massive harmonic function, while
v
is a
M
-massive harmonic function if and only if
\Delta V=0
(which happens in particular when the drift
\alpha
is constant).

The fact that
v
is uniquely defined is a direct consequence of (
1.17
) since the gradient of
u
determines the gradient of
v
.
∎

Remark 1.9
.

Let
u:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be a massive harmonic function. Suppose that

w\mapsto e^{2V(w)}\nabla(u(w)e^{-V(w)})\text{ derives from a potential.}

(1.18)

Then, integrating this quantity from a designated marked point in
\Omega
to an arbitrary
z\in\Omega
produces a function
z\in\Omega\mapsto v(z)\in\mathbb{R}
(indeed, by assumption this does not depend on the choice of path). It is then straightforward to verify that
u+\mathbf{i}v
is massive holomorphic.

On the other hand, it is
a priori
not clear at all why (
1.18
) should hold. In the critical/massless case, this happens as soon as
\Omega
is simply connected. Could this also hold in the massive case? We do not know the answer to this question. As a result we do not know any criterion (other than (
1.18
) itself) for the existence of a harmonic conjugate. Fortunately, this is not needed in the rest of the paper: in Theorem
1.13
, we appeal to a specific harmonic conjugate and need to prove as part of the argument that this function is well defined.

We will also need the following definition.

Definition 1.10
.

Let
f=u+\mathbf{i}v,f^{*}=u^{*}+\mathbf{i}v^{*}:U\to\mathbb{C}
, be two complex-valued function whose real and imaginary parts
u,u^{*}
and
v,v^{*}
are massive harmonic functions. We
do not
assume that
f
and
f^{*}
are massive holomorphic. We say that
f^{*}
is the
\alpha
-conjugate
of
f
if
u^{*}
, resp.
v^{*}
, is the
\alpha
-conjugate of
u
, resp.
v
.

This definition can be written conveniently in matrix form.
For a complex number
z=a+\mathbf{i}b
we denote by
z^{\intercal}
the line vector
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\end{pmatrix}
.
We denote by
\mathrm{Rot}_{-\pi/2}
the rotation matrix of angle
-\pi/2
.
Then,
f
and
f^{*}
are
\alpha
-conjugates if and only if the following equality between
2
by
2
matrices holds:

e^{V}\nabla(fe^{-V})^{\intercal}=e^{-V}\mathrm{Rot}_{-\pi/2}\cdot\nabla(f^{*}e^{V})^{\intercal}.

(1.19)

Note that given
f=u+\mathbf{i}v:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
with
u,v
real massive harmonic, the existence of an
\alpha
-conjugate is not obvious.
However, if
\Omega
is connected and
x\in\partial U
is fixed, there is at most one
\alpha
-conjugate
f^{*}
with
f^{*}(x)=0
, and the values of
f^{*}
can be recovered by integrating (
1.19
).

1.4
Scaling limit for the inverse Kasteleyn matrix

The scaling limit of the inverse Kasteleyn matrix will be given in terms of a suitable massive Green function, which we now introduce. (In fact, the scaling limit itself will involve a number of transformations of this Green function). Recall the mass function
M(x)=\|\nabla V(x)\|^{2}+\Delta V(x)
defined in (
1.12
).

Definition 1.11
.

The
(continuous) massive Green function
in
\Omega
, with mass
M(\omega)
at position
\omega\in\Omega
, and with Dirichlet boundary condition is

G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{t}^{\Omega}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\mathbb{E}_{\omega_{1}\overset{t}{\to}\omega_{2}}\left(\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t}M(B_{s})\mathrm{d}s\right)\right)\mathrm{d}t

(1.20)

where
p_{t}^{\Omega}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
is the transition probability of ordinary Brownian motion killed upon reaching
\partial\Omega
, and
\mathbb{P}_{\omega_{1}\overset{t}{\to}\omega_{2}}
is the law of Brownian bridge from
\omega_{1}
to
\omega_{2}
of duration
t
, while
\mathbb{E}_{\omega_{1}\overset{t}{\to}\omega_{2}}
is the corresponding expectation.

Note that the expectation in the definition of the Green function (
1.20
) is well-defined due to the log-convexity assumption (Assumption
1.5
), which makes
M
a nonnegative function.

The following properties of the massive Green function are easily established.

Lemma 1.12
.

The massive Green function is symmetric:
G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=G^{m}(\omega_{2},\omega_{1})
for all
\omega_{1}\neq\omega_{2}\in\Omega
.
Furthermore,
G^{m}
can be characterised as follows: for each fixed
\omega_{1}\in\Omega
, the massive Green function is the unique solution in
L^{2}(\Omega)\cap\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{\omega_{1}\},\mathbb{R})
of the boundary value problem:

\Delta_{2}G^{m}(\omega_{1},\cdot)=M(\cdot)G^{m}(\omega_{1},\cdot)+\delta_{\omega_{1}}(\cdot),

(1.21)

in the sense of distributions, and with boundary conditions
G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=0
for
\omega_{2}\in\partial\Omega
.

Proof.

We include a short proof here for completeness. The symmetry follows at once from the time-reversibility of the law of Brownian bridge.
The uniqueness of the boundary value problem satisfied by
G^{m}
follows from the maximum principle for the massive Laplacian
\Delta-MI
, which holds under the positivity Assumption
1.5
.
Indeed, if
g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{2}(\Omega)\cap\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{\chi_{1}\},\mathbb{R})
are two solutions of Equation (
1.21
) in the sense of distributions, then
h=g_{1}-g_{2}\in L^{2}(\Omega)\cap\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{\omega_{1}\},\mathbb{R})
is a solution in the sense of distributions of

\Delta h=Mh

with zero boundary conditions.
By elliptic regularity (see
[
27
, Chapter 10.1 (5)]
,
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega},\mathbb{R})
, hence by the maximum principle
h=0
so
g_{1}=g_{2}
.
∎

We now introduce a function of two variables
\kappa
which plays a prominent role in our results below, and for which we give the three equivalent definitions. For this it is useful to make use of
Wirtinger derivatives
(i.e., holomorphic and antiholomorphic derivatives),

\partial=dx+\mathbf{i}dy=\frac{1}{2}\overline{\nabla},\text{\ and \ }\overline{\partial}=dx-\mathbf{i}dy=\frac{1}{2}\nabla.

Thus in terms of Wirtinger derivatives, if we think of
\alpha
as a complex-valued function rather than a vector field, since
\alpha=\nabla V
we see that

\alpha=2\overline{\partial}V.

(1.22)

With this in hand we now introduce the function
\kappa
:

\displaystyle\kappa(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=\overline{\nabla_{2}}G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})-\overline{\alpha}(\omega_{2})G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

(1.23)

\displaystyle=\overline{\nabla_{2}}G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})-\overline{\nabla}V(\omega_{2})G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=2\left(\partial_{2}G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})-\partial V(\omega_{2})G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\right)

(The expressions
\nabla_{2},\partial_{2},\ldots
indicate that the differentiation is taking place with respect to the second variable). As the last expression suggests,
\kappa
may be seen as a kind of
massive gradient
of the massive Green function.

Note that the real part and imaginary parts of
\kappa
are massive harmonic in the first variable away from
\omega_{2}
, since derivatives in the first and second variables commute. As for the behavior with respect to the second variable, using that
\Delta=4\partial\overline{\partial}
, we obtain that
\kappa
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{2}\kappa(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=2\overline{\partial}_{2}\partial_{2}G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})-\overline{\alpha}(\omega_{2})\overline{\partial}_{2}G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})-2\overline{\partial}\partial V(\omega_{2})G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

(1.24)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{2}G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})-\overline{\alpha}(\omega_{2})\overline{\partial}_{2}G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})-\frac{1}{2}\Delta V(\omega_{2})G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:green:distribution},\eqref{eq:pos}}{=}\frac{1}{2}M(\omega_{2})G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\omega_{1}}-\overline{\alpha}(\omega_{2})\overline{\partial}_{2}G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})-\frac{1}{2}G^{m}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})(M(\omega_{2})-|\alpha(\omega_{2})|^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\omega_{1}}-\frac{\overline{\alpha}(\omega_{2})}{2}\overline{\kappa}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

Because of (
1.15
), this identity means that
\kappa(\omega_{1},\cdot)
is
(-\bar{\alpha})
-massive holomorphic (in the second variable) away from
\omega_{1}
.

We are now ready to state our result concerning the scaling limit of the inverse Kasteleyn matrix associated to the near-critical weights defined in (
1.11
) (we recall the choice of gauge function and thus Kasteleyn matrix is specified in Section
2.2
.) This theorem holds for any (sequence of) isoradial superposition lattices
G_{\varepsilon}
(where
\varepsilon>0
denotes the common radius of all faces), requiring only the bounded angle assumption and straight boundary assumption that will be introduced formally in Section
2.3
. We also introduce the following notation: for a set
S\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
\mathcal{D}(S)
denotes the set of its diagonal elements, that is
\mathcal{D}(S)=\{(z,z)\in S,z\in\mathbb{C}\}
.
More generally, for
S\subset\mathbb{C}^{k}
,
\mathcal{D}(S)
denotes the set of elements having at least two coinciding coordinates.

Theorem 1.13
.

Assume that
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary centered at the Temperleyan corner
\partial\Omega\cap B(b^{*},\eta)=L
, as in Section
2.3
.
Let
\kappa
be as in (
1.23
). Then there exists a unique
\alpha
-conjugate function
\kappa^{*}(\cdot,\omega_{2})
in
\Omega\setminus\{\omega_{2}\}
satisfying
\kappa^{*}(\beta^{*},\omega_{2})=0
at the Temperleyan corner.

Furthermore, let
x_{1}=x_{1}(\varepsilon)\in\Gamma
,
y_{1}=y_{1}(\varepsilon)
and
w_{2}=w_{2}(\varepsilon)\in W
,
w_{2}=w_{2}(\varepsilon)
.
Let
x_{2}^{-}x_{2}^{+}
denote the edge of
\Gamma
corresponding to
w_{2}
, with
K_{w,x_{2}^{+}}>0
,
K_{wx_{2}^{-}}<0
.
Uniformly for
(x_{1},w_{2})
, resp.
(y_{1},w_{2})
, in compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
,

\displaystyle K^{-1}(x_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{w_{2}})}\langle\overline{\kappa}(x_{1},w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+o(\varepsilon)

(1.25)

\displaystyle K^{-1}(y_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{i}\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{w_{2}})}\langle\overline{\kappa^{*}}(y_{1},w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+o(\varepsilon)

Moreover, we have the following bounds: uniformly for
(x_{1},w_{2})
in compact subsets of
(\Omega\cup L)^{2}
and
(y_{1},w_{2})
in compact subsets of
(\Omega\cup L)\times(\Omega\cup L\setminus\{\beta^{*}\})
,

K^{-1}(x_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|}\right)\quad;\quad K^{-1}(y_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|y_{1}-w_{2}|}\right).

(1.26)

Note that the existence of the
\alpha
-conjugate
\kappa^{*}
is a part of the statement. The proof of this theorem will be given in Section
3.3
.

1.5
Scaling limit for moments of height function

Using the asymptotic estimate of the inverse Kasteleyn matrix obtained in Theorem
1.13
, we can compute the asymptotic moments of the height field.
This kind of computation is by now very well understood in the critical (non-massive) setting, see
[
21
]
for the square lattice case,
[
16
]
for the isoradial case (full plane), and
[
26
]
for the Temperleyan isoradial case.

Let
h_{\varepsilon}
be the height function associated with a random dimer configuration (on a graph
G_{\varepsilon}
subject to the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.13
), using the reference flow
\omega
defined by setting
\omega(wb)
to be the probability that the edge
wb
belongs to a random dimer configuration, and
\omega(wb)=-\omega(bw)
. Recall that
h_{\varepsilon}
is a random real-valued function defined on the faces of the superposition (medial) graph
G
. Recall the definition of
\kappa,\kappa^{*}
from Theorem
1.13
, and let

F_{0}=\frac{\kappa^{*}-\mathbf{i}\kappa}{2}\quad;\quad F_{1}=-\frac{\kappa^{*}+\mathbf{i}\kappa}{2}.

(1.27)

For a complex number
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we write
z^{(0)}=z,z^{(1)}=\overline{z}
. Then, the following holds.

Theorem 1.14
.

Assume that
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary centered at the Temperleyan corner
\partial\Omega\cap B(b^{*},\eta)=L
, as in Section
2.3
.
Let
n\geq 2
and
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\dots,\zeta_{n}
denote faces of the superposition graph
G
. Then, uniformly for
(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{n})
in compact subsets of
\Omega^{n}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{n})
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]\overset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}\sum_{s_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\int_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{n}}\det_{i\neq j}\Big[F_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(z_{i},z_{j})\Big]\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}.

(1.28)

where
s_{i}+s_{j}
is computed mod 2,
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
are any disjoint smooth paths in
\Omega\cup L
, starting at
n
distinct points on
L
and ending at the
\zeta_{i}
.
Moreover, uniformly for
(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{n})
in compact subsets of
\Omega^{n}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]=O\left(\sum_{\sigma}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+|\log(|\zeta_{i}-\zeta_{\sigma(i)}|)|)\right),

(1.29)

where the sum is over all permutations of
\{1,\dots,n\}
with no fixed points.

We prove this theorem in Section
3.4
.

Remark 1.15

(Coherence with Kenyon’s
[
22
,
23
]
and Li’s
[
26
]
results)
.

As a sanity check we now explain how these results are coherent with what is known in the critical case, since the translation is not entirely obvious.
If we let
G_{i}(w,z)=\mathbf{i}F_{i}(w,z)
, by multi-linearity of the determinant and being careful with the conjugates, rewrites as (
1.28
)

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]\overset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}(-\mathbf{i})^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\int_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{n}}\det_{i\neq j}\big[G_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(z_{i},z_{j})\big]\prod_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{s_{i}}\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}.

(1.30)

Note that the variables are exchanged between our setting and Kenyon and Li’s setting: in our setting,
K^{-1}(w,b)=\kappa(w,b)
while in their setting
K^{-1}(w,b)=\kappa(b,w)
(see for example Lemma 6 of Li’s paper).
Hence, if we let
H_{0}(w,z)=G_{0}(z,w)
,
H_{1}(w,z)=G_{1}(z,w)
, re-indexing Equation (
1.30
) we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]\overset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}(-\mathbf{i})^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\int_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{n}}\det_{i\neq j}\big[H_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{i})}(z_{i},z_{j})\big]\prod_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{s_{i}}\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}.

In other words, if we define
F_{+}(w,z)=H_{0}(w,z)=\mathbf{i}F_{0}(z,w)
and
F_{-}(w,z)=\overline{H_{1}}(w,z)=-\mathbf{i}\bar{F}_{1}(z,w)
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]\overset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}(-\mathbf{i})^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\int_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{n}}\det_{i\neq j}\big[F_{s_{i},s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})\big]\prod_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{s_{i}}\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}.

with the convention that

F_{0,0}=F_{+}\quad;\quad F_{1,0}=F_{-}\quad;\quad F_{0,1}=\bar{F}_{-}\quad;\quad F_{1,1}=\bar{F}_{+}.

This matches the result of
[
26
]
, see the expression before Lemma 15 (this is not so straightforward to check since her expression is very explicit).
This also matches Proposition 20 in Kenyon
[
22
]
, up to what seems to be a typo: the
(-\mathbf{i})^{n}
is present at the last line of the proof, but not in the statement of Proposition 20.
This also matches Proposition 2.2 in
[
23
]
. The
(-\mathbf{i})^{n}
does not appear there, but it is compensated by the fact that
F_{-}
is changed into
-F_{-}
compared to
[
22
]
, accounting for the
(-\mathbf{i})^{n}
in the determinant by multi-linearity (note that the odd terms vanish [for example because an expectation of height functions is always real], and for even terms changing
F_{1}
into
-F_{1}
indeed produces an overall factor
(-1)^{n}
).

In the work of
[
26
]
(which concerns
the critical, i.e. non-massive case), the analogue of this theorem is proved in two steps: the first step is to compute the asymptotic of
K^{-1}
, as we did in Theorem
1.13
.
The second step is to recover the height function from
K^{-1}
by summing along paths and to recover integrals.
This second step can be applied with little modification to our context, see Section
3.4
.

To prove that the limit in Theorem
1.14
is the sine-Gordon model with external electromagnetic field, we will need to identify
F_{0},F_{1}
as the solution of a certain boundary value problem.
This is the content of the following proposition.

Proposition 1.16
.

The functions
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
satisfy

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{w}F_{0}(w,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha(w)}{2}F_{1}(w,z)+\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\delta_{w}(z)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{z}F_{0}(w,z)=\frac{\bar{\alpha}(z)}{2}\bar{F}_{1}(w,z)-\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\delta_{w}(z)

(1.31)

\displaystyle\partial_{w}F_{1}(w,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{\alpha}(w)}{2}F_{0}(w,z)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{z}F_{1}(w,z)=\frac{\bar{\alpha}(z)}{2}\bar{F}_{0}(w,z).

(1.32)

Their diagonal behavior is given by

F_{0}(w,z)=\frac{\mathbf{i}}{4\pi(w-z)}+O(\log|w-z|)\quad;\quad F_{1}(w,z)=O(\log|w-z|).

(1.33)

Furthermore, for
z\in\partial\Omega
, let us denote by
\tau(w)
the tangent vector to
\Omega
at
w
such that
\Omega
is on the left.
The functions
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
satisfy the following boundary conditions:

\displaystyle F_{1}(w,z)

\displaystyle=-F_{0}(w,z)\quad;\quad w\in\partial\Omega

(1.34)

\displaystyle F_{1}(w,z)

\displaystyle=\bar{\tau}(z)^{2}\bar{F}_{0}(w,z)\quad;\quad z\in\partial\Omega.

(1.35)

This result is proved in Section
4.1
.

Remark 1.17
.

As a sanity check, let us compare once again with the massless case of Kenyon
[
22
]
and Li
[
26
]
.
Recall the definition of
F_{+},F_{-}
from Remark
1.15
.
According to Proposition 15 in
[
23
]
or Lemma 15 in
[
26
]
in the massless case, if
\varphi
is a conformal map from
\Omega
to the upper half-plane,

F_{+}(w,z)=-\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(w)}{4\pi(\varphi(w)-\varphi(z))}\quad;\quad F_{-}(w,z)=\frac{\overline{\varphi^{\prime}}(w)}{4\pi(\bar{\varphi}(w)-\varphi(z))}.

(note that there is a factor
8
between the normalization of Kenyon and the normalization of Li, which is the same as ours). By our Remark
1.15
, this implies that in the massless case,

F_{0}(w,z)=-\mathbf{i}F_{+}(z,w)=-\frac{\mathbf{i}\varphi^{\prime}(z)}{4\pi(\varphi(w)-\varphi(z))}\quad;\quad F_{1}(w,z)=-\mathbf{i}\bar{F}_{-}(z,w)=\frac{\mathbf{i}\varphi^{\prime}(z)}{4\pi(\bar{\varphi}(w)-\varphi(z))}

For
w\in\partial\Omega
,
\varphi(w)\in\mathbb{R}
so
F_{1}(w,z)=-F_{0}(w,z)
.
For
z\in\partial\Omega
,
\varphi(z)\in\mathbb{R}
so

\bar{F}_{0}(w,z)=\frac{\overline{\varphi^{\prime}}(z)}{\varphi^{\prime}(z)}F_{1}(w,z).

and since
\arg(\varphi^{\prime}(z))=\arg(\bar{\tau}(z))
at a boundary point, this is consistent with the boundary condition of Proposition
1.16
.

The boundary value problem of Proposition
1.16
will play a crucial role in the identification with the sine Gordon field, which will be discussed below.
The Dirac boundary value problem of Proposition
1.16
is to find a pair of functions
F=(F_{0},F_{1}):\Omega^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that

\begin{cases}\bar{\partial}_{w}F_{0}(w,z)=\frac{\alpha(w)}{2}F_{1}(w,z)+\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\delta_{w}(z)\quad;\quad\partial_{w}F_{1}(w,z)=\frac{\bar{\alpha}(w)}{2}F_{0}(w,z)&\text{ on }\Omega^{2}\\
F_{1}(w,z)=-F_{0}(w,z);&\text{ if }w\in\partial\Omega\\
\end{cases}

(1.36)

We call this boundary value problem
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
.
By Proposition
1.16
, the functions
(F_{0},F_{1})
provided by Theorem
1.14
solve
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
.
Given a vector field
\alpha:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{C}
in
\Omega
, this problem can be conveniently rewritten in terms of the Dirac operator with complex mass

\not{\partial}_{\alpha}:=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&2\bar{\partial}_{w}\\
2\partial_{w}&\overline{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}.

The first two lines can equivalently be rewritten as

\not{\partial}_{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{i}F_{1}&\mathbf{i}\overline{F_{0}}\\
-\mathbf{i}F_{0}&-\mathbf{i}\overline{F_{1}}\end{pmatrix}=I\text{ on }\Omega^{2}

1.6
Identification with sine-Gordon model

Recently,
[
32
]
have constructed the sine-Gordon correlation functions in a setting which intersects with our own. In particular, they prove formulas for the mixed moments of Wirtinger derivatives (
\partial\phi,\bar{\partial}\phi
) in terms of Pfaffians of two-point massive Ising fermion correlations.
Here we introduce some of their key results and
state a theorem which shows that the correlations of the (Wirtinger derivatives) of the limiting height function agree with those of the sine-Gordon, as constructed in
[
32
]
.

[
32
]
consider the case where
\Omega
is the unit disc
{\mathbb{D}}
and regularise the underlying Dirichlet GFF via a heat kernel regularisation. That is, they take the covariance of the Gaussian process on
\Omega
to be

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon}(x,y):=\int_{\varepsilon^{2}}^{\infty}p_{\Omega}(s,x,y)\mathrm{d}s

(1.37)

where
p_{\Omega}(s,x,y)
is the Dirichlet heat kernel on
\Omega
, i.e., the transition density of a Brownian motion (with speed two, so its infinitesimal generator is the Laplacian
\Delta
instead of
(1/2)\Delta
) killed upon exiting
\Omega
. We let
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{GFF}^{\#}(\epsilon)}
denote the law of this Gaussian field
\phi_{\epsilon}
, and following their notation, use
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}^{\#}(\epsilon)}
to denote expectation under it. They define Wick-ordered trigonometric functions
:\!\cos(\gamma\phi_{\epsilon})\!:
and
:\!\sin(\gamma\phi_{\epsilon})\!:
which contains an explicit additional normalising constant
c_{\alpha}
(defined in terms of the Euler–Mascheroni constant) compared to the standard Wick-ordered notions; see
[
32
, (1.3.6) and (2.2.2)]
. This normalising constant
c_{\alpha}
is non-universal and depends on the choice of regularisation.
They then introduce
a further regularisation
\typecolon\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\phi_{\varepsilon})\typecolon,\typecolon\sin(\sqrt{4\pi}\phi_{\epsilon})\typecolon
for the case
\gamma=\sqrt{4\pi}
, see
[
32
, (1.3.7)]
. Let
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
(this
\rho
plays the same role as our
\alpha
, up to a constant multiple, but is in particular assumed here to be
real-valued
).
The authors of
[
32
]
define the regularised sine-Gordon expectation by

\displaystyle\langle F(\phi_{\epsilon})\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho|\epsilon,\Omega)}:=\frac{\Big\langle F(\phi_{\epsilon})\exp\Big(\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{d}A(v)\rho(v)\typecolon\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\phi_{\epsilon}(v))\typecolon\Big)\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}^{\#}(\epsilon)}}{\Big\langle\exp\Big(\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{d}A(v)\rho(v)\typecolon\cos(\sqrt{4\pi}\phi_{\epsilon}(v))\typecolon\Big)\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{GFF}^{\#}(\epsilon)}},

(1.38)

where
\mathrm{d}A(v)
is the area (Lebesgue) measure. That is, for a fixed
\epsilon
, they define a probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho|\epsilon,\Omega)}
on fields (in fact, smooth fields in
C^{1}(\Omega)
) via its Radon-Nikodym derivative with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{GFF}^{\#}(\epsilon)}
.

Fix some test functions
f_{1},..,f_{p},g_{1},...,g_{q}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
with pairwise disjoint supports.
[
32
]
define their correlation functional by taking
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limits of the regularised versions. A key aspect of their result (contained within Theorem 1.1 of
[
32
]
) is to show that these limits exist, thereby showing the existence of a measure associated to the sine-Gordon model.

Theorem 1.18

(Part of Theorem 1.1 in
[
32
]
)
.

\displaystyle\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{p}\partial\phi(f_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{q}\bar{\partial}\phi(g_{j^{\prime}})\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho|\Omega)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int(\prod_{j=1}^{p}(-\partial f_{j}(z_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{q}(-\bar{\partial}g_{j^{\prime}}(w_{j^{\prime}}))\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{p}\phi_{\epsilon}(z_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{q}\phi_{\epsilon}(w_{j^{\prime}})\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho|\epsilon,\Omega)}\mathrm{d}z_{j}\mathrm{d}\bar{w}_{j^{\prime}}.

(1.39)

A more precise version of this result (still restricted to the setting we need) will be stated in Theorem
4.9
. An equivalent and more compact way of formulating the above result is as follows. Let
n\geq 1
and pick “signs”
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\in\{0,1\}
, as well as functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that
\partial^{(s_{j})}f_{j}
have pairwise disjoint support. Then

\displaystyle\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{j})}\phi(f_{j})\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho|\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int\prod_{j=1}^{n}(-\partial^{(s_{j})}f_{j}(z_{j}))\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\phi_{\epsilon}(z_{j})\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho|\epsilon,\Omega)}\mathrm{d}z_{j}^{(s_{j})}.

Here,
\partial^{(0)}
and
\partial^{(1)}
refer to the Wirtinger derivatives
\partial_{z}
and
\bar{\partial}_{z}
respectively. This limit can be written in the form

\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{j})}\phi(f_{j})\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho|\Omega)}=\int\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho)}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}^{(s_{j})},

where
{\mathbf{s}}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. The kernel
\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho)}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
above can thus be viewed (somewhat informally) as the correlation kernel of the sine-Gordon mixed Wirtinger derivatives, i.e.,

\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho)}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{j})}_{z_{j}}\phi(z_{j})\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho|\Omega)}.

(1.40)

[
32
]
give an explicit expression for
\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho)}
in terms of Grassmann variables or, equivalently, Pfaffians of certain operators, which we will also introduce and describe later in Section
4.6
.

We now explain how to proceed with the identification of the limiting height field as the sine-Gordon model at the free fermion point with suitable electromagnetic field, as conjectured in
[
6
]
and as briefly explained in the overview of this introduction. Roughly, the idea will be to say that the expression of the moments of the height function in Theorem
1.14
gives us access to the mixed Wirtinger derivative correlations and that these correlations are
exactly
equal to
\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho)}
for a suitable choice of
\rho
, up to a scaling factor
\lambda^{n}
which accounts for different choices of normalisations.

In order to state this rigorously, we must however extend the preceding results in different directions:

•

We must remove the condition that
\rho
is compactly supported in Theorem
1.18
, since ultimately
\rho
is a multiple of
\alpha=\nabla V
where
V
is assumed log-convex (this assumption is incompatible with the fact that
\alpha
has compact support).

•

We must extend the validity of Theorem
1.14
to domains not having a straight edge, in particular to the unit disc, so as to match the setting of Theorem
1.18
.

•

We must impose conditions on
V
which ensure assumptions needed both for Theorem
1.14
and Theorem
1.18
to hold.

To deal with the first issue, we consider a smooth truncation
\rho_{N}
so that
\rho_{N}
has compact support in
\Omega
for each finite
N\geq 1
, and
\rho_{N}
coincides with
\rho
on compact
K_{n}\subset\Omega
with
K_{n}\uparrow\Omega
. We then explain in Corollary
4.10
that

\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho)}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}):=\lim_{N\to\infty}\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho_{N})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})

(1.41)

exists pointwise for each pairwise distinct
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\Omega
and each
\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

Concerning the second issue, we will show in Corollary
4.5
that the validity of Theorem
1.14
can be extended to domains
\Omega
that are “smooth” in the sense that they are the image of a bounded domain
U
with a straight edge by a bijective map
T:U\to\Omega
such that
T
is analytic in a neighbourhood of
U
. This includes the case of
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
being the unit disc. Therefore, for such a domain, if we let
\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}
denote the law of the limiting dimer height function in
\Omega
with electromagnetic field
\alpha=\nabla V
(which is already known to exist thanks to
[
6
]
), then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}\left[(\phi,f)^{n}\right]=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\int_{\gamma_{1}}\ldots\int_{\gamma_{n}}v^{(\mathbf{s})}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}

(1.42)

for a test function
f
, for some explicitly characterized mixed Wirtinger derivative correlation function

v^{(\mathbf{s})}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{z_{i}}^{(s_{i})}\phi(z_{i})].

(1.43)

Finally, concerning the third issue we will show in Section
4.6
that when
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
, for some very explicit conditions on
\alpha
, all required assumptions are satisfied. A concrete example where these conditions are satisfied is as follows. Fix a function
f\in C^{\infty}((0,\infty))
such that
f^{\prime}<0
and
(e^{f})^{\prime\prime}>0
(e.g.,
f(x)
could be of the form
f(x)=ax+b
with
a<0
or
f(x)=c(x+1)^{2}+b
with
c<-1/2
).

Let
V_{\mathbb{H}}(z)=f(\Im(z))
, and let
\alpha_{\mathbb{H}}=\nabla V_{\mathbb{H}}
. Finally, set

\alpha_{\mathbb{D}}(w)=\bar{g}^{\prime}(w)\alpha_{\mathbb{H}}(g(w)),

(1.44)

where
g:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{H}
is a conformal isomorphism.

We can now state the theorem relating the height function to the sine-Gordon model.

Theorem 1.19
.

Suppose
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
,
\alpha=\alpha_{\mathbb{D}}
is as in (
1.44
). For
\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
v^{(\mathbf{s})}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}
denote the mixed holomorphic and antiholomorphic derivative correlations of the limiting height function in
(\Omega,\alpha)
defined in (
1.43
), and let
\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho)}
be the analogous sine-Gordon correlation kernel associated with
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
, defined in (
1.41
). Then

v^{(\mathbf{s})}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}=\lambda^{n}\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho)}

(1.45)

with

\lambda=-(4\sqrt{\pi})^{-1},\quad\rho=-\frac{\tilde{\alpha}}{8\mathbf{i}\pi},\text{ where }\tilde{\alpha}(z)=e^{\mathbf{i}\arg g^{\prime}(z)}\alpha(z),

(1.46)

and
g:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{D}
is a conformal isomorphism,
\arg g^{\prime}
being any continuous determination of the argument.

Therefore, from the point of view of correlations (and thus also of moments), the limiting height function
\phi
with law
\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}
agrees with
\lambda
times a sine-Gordon field SG
(\rho|\Omega)
with
\rho
as above. This factor
\lambda
accounts for different choices of normalisations.
(Note that, due to the presence of a normalising constant
c_{\alpha}
involving the Euler–Mascheroni constant in the definition of
\typecolon\cos(\sqrt{4\pi})\phi\typecolon
, and the fact that
\rho=-\tilde{\alpha}/8\mathbf{i}\pi
, the sine-Gordon penalisation is merely proportional to
\mathbf{i}\tilde{\alpha}
.)

Remark 1.20
.

Another possible choice of
\alpha
so that Theorem
1.19
holds (instead of (
1.44
)) would be to take
V_{\mathbb{D}}(z)=f(\Im(g(z))
where
f
is as above, and
\alpha(z)=\nabla V_{\mathbb{D}}(z),z\in\mathbb{D}
.

In light of Theorem
1.19
it is natural to wonder if the moments identify uniquely the distribution of the height functions. To this end we state a theorem which shows that this is indeed the case as soon as the domain satisfies some very mild regularity conditions. While it may be more common to consider the moment generating function associated to a given moment sequence and verify that this has positive radius of convergence, we find it more convenient to consider the log-Laplace transform, whose generating function is given in terms of the cumulants (rather than the moments). More precisely, if
X
is a random variable and
\kappa_{n}=\langle X,\ldots,X\rangle
denotes its
n
th cumulant, then
\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\mathbf{i}tX})=\sum_{n}(\mathbf{i}t)^{n}\kappa_{n}/n!
.

We find that the log-Laplace transform of the height function integrated against a test function
f
has positive radius of convergence, hence the cumulants (and thus the moments) uniquely identify the law. As a byproduct of this approach, we obtain a Fredholm regularised determinant (of fourth order) formula for the log-Laplace transform of the sine-Gordon model, which may be of independent interest. See Section
4.7
for definitions and more details.

Theorem 1.21
.

Assume that
\Omega
is a Jordan domain and that
\partial\Omega
is Dini-smooth, and assume that the conclusion (
1.28
) of Theorem
1.14
holds in this domain (thus,
\Omega
could be a domain with a straight edge as in Theorem
1.14
on top of being Dini-smooth, or it could be smooth in the sense that it is the image of a domain with a straight edge by a conformal isomorphism that is analytic in a neighbourhood of this domain).

There exists an integral operator

\displaystyle K:L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,1])\oplus L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,1])\to L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,1])\oplus L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,1])

(1.47)

which lies in the fourth Schatten class
S_{4}
, such that

\langle(\phi,f);\ldots;(\phi,f)\rangle_{(\Omega,\alpha)}=(n-1)!\Tr((fK)^{n}).

As a result, the power series defined by the cumulants has a positive radius of convergence, so that the moments of
(\phi,f)^{n}
uniquely determine the law of
(\phi,f)
. Furthermore, for
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\mu|>0
sufficiently small, we have the following regularized determinant formula for the moment generating function

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}[e^{\mu(\phi,f)}]

\displaystyle=\det_{4}(I-\mu fK)e^{\sum_{n=1}^{3}\frac{(-\mu)^{n}}{n!}\big\langle(\phi,f);...;(\phi,f)\big\rangle_{(\Omega,\alpha)}}.

(1.48)

See
[
35
, Chapter 3.1]
for the definition of a Dini smooth Jordan domain. In particular, any Jordan domain whose boundary has a
C^{1,\alpha}
parametrisation for some
0<\alpha<1
is Dini-smooth (see
[
35
, (9), Chapter 3.3]
).
This theorem will be proved in Section
4.7
.

[
32
]
.

2
Analysis of massive discrete differentials

The goal of this section is to develop the tools we will need in order to prove Theorems
1.13
and
1.14
. These can be viewed as a toolbox for discrete massive harmonic functions and discrete massive differentials which is interesting in itself, generalizing some of the tools of
[
12
]
to the non-critical setting.
This section is structured as follows:

•

we first give an equivalent formulation of the dimer model to relate the Kasteleyn matrix to a discrete massive Laplacian, in the spirit of
[
6
,
36
]
.
This enables us to express the inverse Kasteleyn matrix in terms of derivatives of the discrete massive Green function, which are discrete massive harmonic in one variable and discrete massive differentials in the other.

•

We develop a general theory of discrete massive harmonic functions and a discrete holomorphicity theory for discrete massive differentials, in the spirit of
[
12
]
: we find analogues of the Harnack and Beurling estimates for discrete massive harmonic functions, and we also give a discrete Cauchy formula and regularity estimates for discrete massive differentials

•

We apply those results to obtain asymptotic estimates of the derivatives of the discrete massive Green function.

In the next section, we will use these results to:

•

Obtain asymptotic estimates for the inverse Kasteleyn matrix, that is prove Theorem
1.13
.

•

Prove how Theorem
1.13
implies Theorem
1.14
, that is how to recover the asymptotic of the heights from the asymptotic of the inverse Kasteleyn matrix.

2.1
Discrete massive Laplacian

For
x\in\Gamma^{\infty}
, define its weight

\mu(x)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\sim x^{\prime}}\sin(2\theta_{xx^{\prime}}).

(2.1)

In comparison with Equation (2.1) of
[
12
]
, we do not include the
\varepsilon^{2}
factor in the renormalization.
Define the discrete (critical) Laplacian
\Delta_{\#}
on
\Gamma^{\infty}
by setting for
f:\Gamma^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}

\Delta_{\#}f(x)=\sum_{x\sim x^{\prime},x^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\infty}}c^{0}_{x,x^{\prime}}(f(x^{\prime})-f(x));x\in\Gamma^{\infty}.

(2.2)

Note the sign convention:
\Delta_{\#}
is a negative operator which converges in the scaling limit to the continuum Laplacian after diffusive scaling. Also note that contrary to
[
12
]
, we do not renormalize the discrete Laplacian by
\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x)
.

Define a
discrete mass function

m(x):=e^{-V(x)}[\Delta_{\#}e^{V}](x),\quad x\in\Gamma.

(2.3)

By definition,
e^{V}
is then a
discrete massive harmonic function
on
\Gamma
:

[(\Delta_{\#}-mI)e^{V}](x)=0;\quad x\in\Gamma.

(2.4)

Now, note that on
\Gamma^{\infty}
, since the discrete Laplacian approximates the continuum Laplacian as
\varepsilon>0
(see for example Lemma 2.2 of
[
12
]
),

\displaystyle\Delta_{\#}e^{V}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\mu\Delta e^{V}+O(\varepsilon^{3})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\mu(\Delta V+\|\nabla V\|^{2})e^{V}+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

(2.5)

Hence, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, recalling the definition of the continuous mass function
M
from (
1.12
),

m(x)=\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x)M(x)+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

(2.6)

Observe that using the positivity Assumption
1.5
, this ensures that
m
is non-negative for
\varepsilon
small enough.

Remark 2.1
.

This is the only place where we need that
M
is positive and not only non-negative.
This is a technical assumption and can probably be removed.

We will also need to discuss the boundary conditions of the Laplacian, when restricted to
\Gamma
. For
f:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
, extends
f
to a function
f_{0}:\Gamma^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
by letting
f_{0}
be
0
outside
\Gamma
, that is:

f_{0}(x)=f(x)~\forall x\in\Gamma\quad;\quad f_{0}(x)=0~\forall x\in\Gamma^{\infty}\setminus\Gamma.

Then, the discrete Laplacian with
Dirichlet boundary conditions

\Delta_{\#}^{d}
on
\Gamma
is defined by setting, for
f:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
,

\Delta_{\#}^{d}f(x):=\Delta_{\#}f_{0}(x);x\in\Gamma.

(2.7)

We emphasise that
\Delta_{\#}
is defined on the entire graph
\Gamma^{\infty}
, whereas
\Delta_{\#}^{d}
will always mean the discrete Laplacian on
\Gamma
, acting on functions with zero boundary conditions in the above sense.

2.2
Gauge equivalence, Kasteleyn matrix.

We set up an appropriate equivalent formulation of the off-critical dimer model using the massive Laplacian, analogous to
[
6
]
and to
[
36
]
.
As noted in
[
36
]
, the dimer weights of Figure
1
on the superposition graph
G
are actually gauge equivalent to the family of weights specified by Figure
2
.

\begin{overpic}[dimer_weight.pdf]
\put(51.0,26.0){$w$}
\put(10.0,25.0){$x^{-}$}
\put(83.0,25.0){$x^{+}$}
\put(50.0,0.0){{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$y^{-}$}}
\put(50.0,45.0){{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$y^{+}$}}
\put(13.0,46.0){$\sqrt{\frac{e^{V(x^{+})}}{e^{V(x^{-})}}c^{0}_{x^{-},x^{+}}}$}
\put(81.0,4.0){$\sqrt{\frac{e^{V(x^{-})}}{e^{V(x^{+})}}c^{0}_{x^{-},x^{+}}}$}
\put(3.0,0.0){$\frac{e^{V(y^{-})}}{\sqrt{e^{V(x^{+})+V(x^{-})}c^{0}_{x^{-},x^{+}}}}$}
\put(70.0,44.0){$\frac{e^{V(y^{+})}}{\sqrt{e^{V(x^{+})+V(x^{-})}c^{0}_{x^{-},x^{+}}}}$}
\put(32.0,26.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$-1$}}
\put(60.0,26.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$1$}}
\put(51.0,15.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$-i$}}
\put(45.0,32.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$i$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 2:
Gauge change. An example of Kasteleyn phases is given in red.

In reality, compared to
[
36
]
we have performed an additional gauge change on the
B_{1}
vertices (i.e.,
y^{-},y^{+}
on the figure). Namely, the weights on
wy^{-}
and
wy^{+}
were both
1/\sqrt{c^{0}_{x^{-},x^{+}}e^{V(x^{+})+V(x^{-})}}
, and we have now multiplied those respectively by
e^{V(y^{-})}
and
e^{V(y^{-})}
.

Remark 2.2
.

There might be other interesting choices of gauge change. What we want here is that the Kasteleyn matrix below corresponds to a drift in the vertical direction.

We now specify a collection of Kasteleyn phases
\zeta
.
Around each white vertex
w\in\Gamma^{*}
we pick one primal vertex
x^{-}
(there are two possible choices, and we pick one among these two arbitrarily), and associate a phase to the edge
(wx^{-})
given by
\zeta_{w,x^{-}}=1
. Then rotating cyclically (counterclockwise) all four edges are given phases
1,\mathbf{i},-1,-\mathbf{i}
.
These phases have the property that around each face
x,w_{1},y,w_{2}
of
G
,

\zeta_{w_{1},x}\overline{\zeta_{w_{1},y}}\zeta_{w_{2},y}\overline{\zeta_{w_{2},x}}=-1.

(2.8)

Recall that
B=\Gamma\cup\Gamma^{\star}
.
We define the
Kasteleyn operator/matrix

K:W\times B
by setting
K(w,b)=\tilde{c}_{w,b}\times
corresponding phase, where
\tilde{c}_{w,b}
is the above gauge-changed weights.
Note that
K^{*}K:B\times B\to\mathbb{C}
. In fact,

Lemma 2.3
.

K^{*}K
is naturally block diagonal where the blocks correspond respectively to
B_{0}=\Gamma
and
B_{1}=\Gamma^{*}
:

K^{*}K=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\Delta_{\#}^{d}+mI&0\\
0&\star\end{array}\right),

(2.9)

where
m(x)\geq 0
is the discrete mass function defined in (
2.3
).

Proof.

This is a special case of Proposition 4.7 in
[
36
]
, but we include the proof here since it is very short. We first check that
K^{*}K
is block diagonal. Let
x\in\Gamma,y\in\Gamma^{*}
. If
x
and
y
are not adjacent in
G
to a common vertex in
B^{*}
, then for all
w\in B^{*}
either
K^{*}_{x,w}=0
or
K_{w,y}=0
so
(K^{*}K)_{x,y}=0.
Otherwise,
x
and
y
are the opposite black vertices of a rhombus
xw_{1}yw_{2}
, and

(K^{*}K)_{x,y}=K^{*}_{x,w_{1}}K_{w_{1},y}+K^{*}_{x,w_{2}}K_{w_{2},x}=\sum_{i\in\{1,2\}}\tilde{c}_{w_{i},x}\tilde{c}_{w_{i},y}\overline{\zeta_{w_{i},x}}\zeta_{w_{i},y}=0

(2.10)

because by definition of the Kasteleyn phases
\overline{\zeta_{w_{1},x}}\zeta_{w_{1},y}=-\overline{\zeta_{w_{2},x}}\zeta_{w_{2},y}
and by definition of the weights

\forall i\in\{1,2\},\quad\tilde{c}_{w_{i},x}\tilde{c}_{w_{i},y}=e^{2V(y)-V(x)}

(2.11)

Hence
K^{*}K
vanishes on
\Gamma\times\Gamma^{*}
, and for the same reason it vanishes on
\Gamma^{*}\times\Gamma
.

We now check that
K^{*}K=I
on
\Gamma\times\Gamma
. If
x_{1}\neq x_{2}\in\Gamma
are not adjacent in
\Gamma
, then for all
w\in B^{*}
either
K^{*}_{x_{1},w}=0
or
K_{w,x_{2}}=0
so
(K^{*}K)_{x_{1},x_{2}}=0
. If
x_{1}\sim x_{2}
, denoting by
w\in B^{\star}
the middle of the edge
x_{1}x_{2}
,

(K^{*}K)_{x_{1}x_{2}}=\overline{\zeta_{w,x_{1}}}\zeta_{w,x_{2}}\tilde{c}_{w,x_{1}}\tilde{c}_{w,x_{2}}=-c_{x_{1},x_{2}}.

(2.12)

If
x\in\Gamma
, denoting by
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
its neighbours in
\Gamma
and by
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
the middle of the corresponding edges, recalling the definition of edge weights
\tilde{c}_{x,w}
in Figure
2
,

\displaystyle(K^{*}K)_{xx}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{\zeta_{w_{i},x}}\zeta_{w_{i},x}\tilde{c}_{w_{i},x}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c^{0}_{x,x_{i}}\frac{e^{V(x_{i})}}{e^{V(x)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c^{0}_{x,x_{i}}\frac{e^{V(x_{i})}-e^{V(x)}}{e^{V(x)}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}c^{0}_{x,x_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{\#}e^{V(x)}}{e^{V(x)}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}c^{0}_{x,x_{i}}.

\displaystyle=m(x)-(\Delta_{\#})_{x,x}.

If one of the
x_{i}\in\overline{\Gamma}\setminus\Gamma
, this is equivalent to setting
\tilde{c}_{x,x_{i}}=0
that is
\Delta_{\#}^{d}
has Dirichlet boundary conditions.
∎

2.3
The “straight boundary” assumption.

To avoid boundary issues, we will need an additional assumption on
\Omega
and
\Gamma
. Let
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma
denote the vertices of
y\in\Gamma^{\infty}\setminus\Gamma
having a neighbor in
\Gamma
.

Definition 2.4
.

We say that
\Omega
and
\Gamma

have a straight boundary
(near
\beta^{*}\in\partial\Omega
) if there exists
\eta>0
(independent of
\varepsilon
) such that

•

\partial\Omega\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)=L
is a straight segment in
\mathbb{C}

•

\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma\subset L

•

\Gamma^{\infty}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
is symmetric by reflection along
L
.

This is illustrated on Figure
3
.

Figure 3:
Straight boundary

This assumption is classical when dealing with Temperleyan graphs: see for example Section 5.3 of
[
21
]
or Section 3.4 of
[
12
]
.
Note that when we make this assumption,
\eta>0
is a fixed macroscopic length, in particular
\eta\gg\varepsilon
. For future reference, we denote
by
\Gamma_{\varphi}
the graph
\Gamma_{\varphi}:=\Gamma\cup\varphi(\Gamma\cap B)
and by
\Omega_{\varphi}=\Omega\cup\varphi(\Omega\cap B)
with
B=B(\beta^{*},\eta)
. Equivalently under our assumptions,
\Gamma_{\varphi}=\Gamma\cup(\Gamma^{\infty}\cap B)
and
\Omega_{\varphi}=\Omega\cup B
.

Remark 2.5
.

For convenience, we will also assume that
\partial\Omega\cap B(z,2\eta)
is a straight line. Note that this is not a stronger assumption, upon replacing
L
by a segment half the size.

When
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary,
L=\partial\Omega\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
, we denote by
\Gamma\cup B(\beta^{*},\eta)
the set obtained by adding to
\Gamma
the edges and vertices contained in
\Gamma^{\infty}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
. Let
\varphi
denote the reflection along
L
. The key observation is that if
H
is massive harmonic with Dirichlet boundary conditions on
\Gamma\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
, that is
(\Delta_{\#}^{d}-mI)H=0
on
\Gamma\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
, we can extend
H
to
\Gamma\cup B(\beta^{*},\eta)
as follows: if
x\in\Gamma\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
, then

H(\varphi(x)):=-H(x).

Note that for
x\in\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)\subset L
,
H(\varphi(x))=H(x)=0
. We abusively denote by
H
the extended function. It is easy to see that if the mass function is also symmetric in
B(\beta^{*},\eta)
, that is
m(x)=m(\varphi(x))
for all
x\in\Gamma\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
, then the extended function
H
is massive harmonic in
\Gamma^{\infty}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
(and vanishes on
L
). This is what we will call the
discrete Schwarz reflection principle
.

2.4
Discrete massive Green function, resolvent identity

Definition 2.6

(
[
12
]
, Definition 2.3)
.

The
non-massive free discrete Green function

G_{\#,\infty}:(\Gamma^{\infty})^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is the unique function satisfying for all fixed
x_{1}\in\Gamma^{\infty}
,

1.

\Delta_{\#}G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},\cdot)=\delta_{x_{1}=\cdot}

2.

G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{1})=(\log(\varepsilon)-\gamma_{Euler}-\log 2)/(2\pi)

3.

G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2})=o(|x_{1}-x_{2}|),|x_{1}-x_{2}|\to\infty.

Its asymptotic was obtained in Section 7 of
[
21
]
and recalled in Theorem 2.5 of
[
12
]
:

G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x_{1}-x_{2}|+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}\right);x_{1}\neq x_{2}.

(2.13)

uniformly in
x_{1},x_{2}
,
\varepsilon
