and the isoradial graph (under the bounded angle assumption). Another important property of the non-massive free discrete Green function is its
locality
: for
x_{1},x_{2}\in\Gamma^{\infty}
, and any path of primal vertices
\gamma
from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
,
G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2})
can be expressed as a function only of the angles of the rhombi adjacent to
\gamma
. This can be seen on the explicit expression of the Green function given in Theorem 7.1 of
[
21
]
.

Remark 2.7
.

This locality of the discrete Green function also holds for the
Z
-invariant massive Laplacian on isoradial graphs, which corresponds to the constant mass case
m(x)=constant
in our setting, see
[
11
]
. This is due to the specific combinatorial structure of the weights and to the existence of an explicit multiplicative discrete exponential function. This is not known to hold in our more general setting.

Definition 2.8
.

The
discrete massive Green function with Dirichlet boundary condition

G_{\#}^{m}:\Gamma^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is the unique function satisfying, for all fixed
x_{1}\in\Gamma
,
(\Delta_{\#}^{d}-mI)G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},\cdot)=\delta_{x_{1}}(\cdot)
.
Equivalently, in matrix notation,
G_{\#}^{m}=(\Delta_{\#}^{d}-mI)^{-1}
(both sides should be interpreted as
\Gamma\times\Gamma
matrices).

Note that by definition of the discrete Laplacian with Dirichlet boundary conditions (see Equation (
2.7
)),
G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},\cdot)
has “Dirichlet boundary conditions”: i.e.
G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},\cdot)
is the unique function
g
on
\Gamma
such that, extending it to be zero
\Gamma^{\infty}\setminus\Gamma
,
\Delta_{\#}g(x)=\delta_{x_{1}}(x)
for all
x\in\Gamma
. The uniqueness in this definition follows from the maximum principle for the discrete massive Laplacian
\Delta_{\#}-mI
, which holds under the positivity Assumption
1.5
(see Remark
2.1
).
In the case
m=0
,
G_{\#}:=G_{\#}^{0}
is the
non-massive discrete Green function with Dirichlet boundary conditions
and corresponds to Definition 2.6 of
[
12
]
.

The massive and non-massive discrete Green functions have a logarithmic singularity near the diagonal. In Appendix
A
, we recall several classical bounds, which will be useful everywhere in what follows.

Since the Laplacian
\Delta_{\#}^{d}
is a symmetric matrix, a direct consequence of Definition
2.8
is the symmetry of the discrete Green function with Dirichlet boundary conditions:

\forall x_{1},x_{2}\in\Gamma,~G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})=G_{\#}^{m}(x_{2},x_{1}).

This will be used without mention in what follows.

The massive and non-massive Green functions are related by the following identity, see Lemma 2.1 of
[
13
]
(and Proposition 4.10 of
[
6
]
for a continuous counterpart):

Proposition 2.9

(Resolvent identity)
.

For all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\Gamma
,

G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})=G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2})+\sum_{x\in\Gamma}m(x)G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x)G_{\#}(x,x_{2}).

Proof.

Let
x_{1}\in\Gamma
be fixed and denote the right-hand side by
x_{2}\to H(x_{1},x_{2})
.
It satisfies

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\#}^{d}-mI)H(x_{1},\cdot)=\delta_{x_{1}}(\cdot)-m(\cdot)G_{\#}(x_{1},\cdot)+\sum_{x\in\Gamma}m(x)G_{\#}(x_{1},x)\delta_{x}(\cdot)=\delta_{x_{1}}(\cdot),

hence by definition
H
is the massive Green function on
\Gamma
.
∎

Let
X^{m}
denote the killed random walk associated with the massive Laplacian
\Delta_{\#}-mI
. When
m=0
, we simply write
X=X^{m}
. Using the optional stopping theorem, we obtain the following probabilistic interpretation of massive harmonic functions. For any finite subset
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Gamma_{\infty}
, let
\tau
denote the first exit time of
\Gamma^{\prime}
by
X^{m}
. Then, if
H:\overline{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{R}
is massive harmonic on
\Gamma^{\prime}
,

H(x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}[H(X^{m}_{\tau})],~x\in\Gamma^{\prime}.

(2.14)

In this equality, when
X^{m}
dies before leaving
\Gamma^{\prime}
, the right-hand side should be interpreted as
H(X^{m}_{\tau})=0
.
Define

\pi^{m}(x)=m(x)+\sum_{y\sim x}c^{0}_{x,y},~x\in\Gamma.

The discrete massive Green function with Dirichlet boundary conditions can alternatively be defined as follows. Let
\tau
denote the first hitting time of
(\overline{\Gamma}\setminus\Gamma)\cup\{x_{1}\}
. Then,

G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x_{2}}[X^{m}_{\tau}=x_{1}]}{\pi^{m}(x_{1})}.

(2.15)

Indeed, computing the Laplacian of the right-hand side gives Definition
2.8
.
As a consequence of this random walk representation, we obtain the following result which will be used everywhere in the sequel without mention, and relies on the positivity Assumption
1.5
as well as (
2.6
).

Lemma 2.10
.

\forall x_{1},x_{2}\in\Gamma,~0\leq G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})\leq G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2}).

This will often be used in combination with the bound
A.1
.

2.5
Massive harmonic analysis on isoradial graphs

We now show how to adapt to the massive setting some of the basic tools in harmonic analysis on isoradial graphs developed in
[
12
]
.
For any
\Gamma\subset\Gamma^{\infty}
a subset of vertices, we denote by
\partial_{E}\Gamma^{\prime}
its edge boundary, that is

\partial_{E}\Gamma^{\prime}:=\{x^{-}x^{+},x^{-}\sim x^{+},x^{-}\in\Gamma^{\prime},x^{+}\in\Gamma^{\infty}\setminus\Gamma^{\prime}\}.

(2.16)

The following
discrete Green formula
holds: it is not specific to isoradial graphs, the proof is a simple computation involving only the reversibility of the conductances.

Lemma 2.11

(Equation (2.4) of
[
12
]
)
.

For
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Gamma^{\infty}
and
F,G:\Gamma^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\sum_{x\in\Gamma^{\prime}}[G(x)(\Delta_{\#}F)(x)-(\Delta_{\#}G)(x)F(x)]=\sum_{x^{-}x^{+}\in\partial_{E}\Gamma^{\prime}}c^{0}_{x^{-},x^{+}}[G(x^{-})F(x^{+})-G(x^{+})F(x^{-})].

(2.17)

Note that the factor
\mu(x)
does not appear on the left-hand side, contrary to Equation (2.4) of
[
12
]
, since we do not use the same renormalization for the Laplacian.

We now state a lemma which implies that massive harmonic functions are globally Lipschitz: it is a generalization to the massive setting of Corollary 2.9 in
[
12
]
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
r>0
, let
B(x,r)
denote the Euclidean ball of radius
r
centered at
x
. Let
B_{\#}(x,r)
denote its
discretization
, that is the largest connected component of
B(x,r)\cap\Gamma^{\infty}
.

Let us also denote by
\mathcal{M}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|M(x)|<\infty
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
R,r>0
be such that
B(x_{0},R+r)\subset\Omega
and
H:\Gamma^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
is massive harmonic on
B_{\#}(x_{0},R+r)
(i.e.,
\Delta_{\#}H(x)=m(x)H(x)
for all
x\in B_{\#}(x_{0},R+r)
).
Then
H
is globally Lipschitz on
B_{\#}(x_{0},R)
, in the following sense: for all
w\in B_{\#}(x_{0},R)
corresponding to the middle of the edge
x^{-}x^{+}
,

|H(x^{+})-H(x^{-})|\leq C\varepsilon\left(r+\frac{1}{r}\right)\max_{B_{\#}(x_{0},R+r)}|H|,

where
C=C(\mathcal{M})
depends only on
\mathcal{M}
and the bounded angles assumption, but not on
\varepsilon
,
r
and
x
.

We will often use the following weaker form: if
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is an open set,
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{O}
is a compact set,
H:\Gamma^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is harmonic and bounded (by a constant
C
) on
\mathcal{O}
, then
H
is Lipschitz on
\mathcal{C}
(with Lipschitz constant depending only on
C
and
\dist(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{O}^{c})
).

Recall that because of the bounded angles assumption, there exist paths in the graph
\Gamma^{\infty}
between points
x
and
y
of total length comparable to the Euclidean distance
|x-y|
, see the beginning of Section
1.2
. Hence the second (weaker) part of the statement indeed follows from the first.

Proof.

The proof is an adaptation of the proof of Proposition 2.7 in Appendix 2 of
[
12
]
.
We first prove an analogue of the mean property for massive harmonic functions.
Recall that
G_{\#,\infty}
denotes the free discrete Green function on
\Gamma^{\infty}
, see Definition
2.6
.
Let
w\in B_{\#}(x_{0},R)
corresponding to the middle of
x^{-}x^{+}
. Define
F:\Gamma^{\infty}\times\Gamma^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\forall x_{1},x_{2}\in\Gamma^{\infty},~F(x_{1},x_{2})=G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2})-\frac{\log(r)}{2\pi}+\frac{r^{2}-|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}{4\pi r^{2}}.

(2.18)

as in the proof of Proposition A.2 in
[
12
]
.
It can be checked that this satisfies

\Delta_{\#}F(x_{1},\cdot)=\delta_{x_{1}}(\cdot)-\varepsilon^{2}\mu(\cdot)/(\pi r^{2}),

see
[
12
, Lemma 2.2 (i)]
. Using Equation (
2.13
), it is also seen to satisfy

F(x_{1},x_{2})=O(\varepsilon^{2}/r^{2})
if
|x_{1}-x_{2}|=r+O(\varepsilon)
.
For
x_{1}\in\{x^{-},x^{+}\}
, the discrete Green formula Equation (
2.17
) applied on
B_{\#}(x^{\prime},r)
with the two functions
H
,
F(x^{\prime},\cdot)
gives

\displaystyle H(x_{1})-\sum_{x_{2}\in B_{\#}(x_{1},r)}\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x_{2})}{\pi r^{2}}+m(x_{2})F(x_{1},x_{2})\right)H(x_{2})

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{x_{2}^{-}x_{2}^{+}\in\partial B_{\#}(x_{1},r)}\tan(\theta_{x_{2}^{-}x_{2}^{+}})(H(x_{2}^{-})F(x_{1},x_{2}^{+})-H(x_{2}^{+})F(x_{1},x_{2}^{-})).

Since
F(x_{1},\cdot)=O(\varepsilon^{2}/r^{2})
near
\partial B_{\#}(x_{1},r)
, which contains
O(r/\varepsilon)
elements, and since
B(x_{1},r)\subset B(x,R+r)\subset\Omega
,

\left|H(x_{1})-\sum_{x_{2}\in B_{\#}(x_{1},r)}\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x_{2})}{\pi r^{2}}+m(x_{2})F(x_{1},x_{2})\right)H(x_{2})\right|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon}{r}\max_{x_{2}\in B_{\#}(x_{0},R+r)}|H(x_{2})|

(2.19)

for some absolute constant
C=C(\mathcal{M})
. Subtracting Equation (
2.19
) with
x_{1}=x^{-},x_{1}=x^{+}
and using the triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle|H(x^{+})-H(x^{-})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x_{2}\in B_{\#}(x^{-},r)\cap B_{\#}(x^{+},r)}m(x_{2})|F(x^{-},x_{2})-F(x^{+},x_{2})|\cdot|H(x_{2})|

(2.20)

\displaystyle+\sum_{x_{2}\in B_{\#}(x^{-},r)\Delta B_{\#}(x^{+},r)}\left|\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x_{2})}{\pi r^{2}}+m(x_{2})F(x_{1},x_{2})\right)H(x_{2})\right|+\frac{2C\varepsilon\max_{B_{\#}(x_{0},R+r)}|H|}{r},

where
A\Delta B
denotes the symmetric difference
(A\setminus B)\cup(B\setminus A)
, and where in the last sum
F(x_{1},x_{2})
is
F(x^{-},x_{2})
if
x\in B(x^{-},r)\setminus B(x^{+},r)
, and
F(x^{+},x_{2})
if
x\in B(x^{+},r)\setminus B(x^{-},r)
.
On the one hand, since
m(x_{2})=\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x_{2})M(x_{2})+O(\varepsilon^{3})
by Equation (
2.6
), and since on
B_{\#}(x^{-},r)\Delta B_{\#}(x^{+},r)
(which contains
O(r/\varepsilon)
elements,
F(x_{1},x_{2})=O(\varepsilon^{2}/r^{2})
, we obtain

\sum_{x_{2}\in B_{\#}(x^{-},r)\Delta B_{\#}(x^{+},r)}\left|\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x_{2})}{\pi r^{2}}+m(x_{2})F(x_{1},x_{2})\right)H(x_{2})\right|\leq\frac{C(\mathcal{M})\varepsilon\max_{B_{\#}(x_{0},R+r)}|H|}{r}

(2.21)

for some constant
C=C(\mathcal{M})
depending only on
\mathcal{M}
and the constant in the bounded angle assumption.
On the other hand, for all
x_{2}\in B_{\#}(x^{-},r)\cap B_{\#}(x^{+},r)
, using that

\displaystyle||x_{2}-x^{-}|-|x_{2}-x^{+}||\leq|x^{-}-x^{+}|=\varepsilon=O(\varepsilon),

we obtain for all
x_{2}\neq x^{-},x^{+}

\displaystyle|F(x^{-},x_{2})-F(x^{+},x_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq|G_{\#,\infty}(x^{-},x_{2})-G_{\#,\infty}(x^{+},x_{2})|+\frac{\left||x_{2}-x^{-}|^{2}-|x_{2}-x^{+}|^{2}\right|}{4\pi r^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\left(\frac{|x_{2}-x^{-}|}{|x_{2}-x^{+}|}\right)+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|x_{2}-x^{+}|^{2}}\right)+\frac{\left||x_{2}-x^{-}|^{2}-|x_{2}-x^{+}|^{2}\right|}{4\pi r^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\left(1+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|x_{2}-x^{+}|}\right)\right)+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|x_{2}-x^{+}|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\left|(|x_{2}-x^{+}|+O(\varepsilon))^{2}-|x_{2}-x^{+}|^{2}\right|}{4\pi r^{2}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|x_{2}-x^{+}|}\right)+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon|x_{2}-x^{+}|}{r^{2}}\right)

where the
O
is uniform in
x_{2},r
. Note that for
x_{2}=x_{1}\in\{x^{-},x^{+}\}
,

|G_{\#,\infty}(x^{-},x_{2})-G_{\#,\infty}(x^{+},x_{2})|=\left|\frac{\log(\varepsilon)-c}{2\pi}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x^{-}-x^{+}|+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|x^{-}-x^{+}|^{2}}\right)\right|=O(1).

Hence, we can write for all
x_{2}\in\Gamma^{\infty}
,

|F(x^{-},x_{2})-F(x^{+},x_{2})|=O\left(1\wedge\frac{\varepsilon}{|x_{2}-x^{+}|}\right)+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon|x_{2}-x^{+}|}{r^{2}}\right),

(2.22)

and the first term on the right-hand side of Equation (
2.20
) can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{2}\in B_{\#}(x^{-},r)\cap B_{\#}(x^{+},r)}m(x_{2})|H(x_{2})|\cdot|F(x^{-},x_{2})-F(x^{+},x_{2})|

(2.23)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\max_{B_{\#}(x_{0},R+r)}|H|\sup_{B(x_{0},R+r)}|M|\sum_{x_{2}\in B_{\#}(x^{-},r)\cap B_{\#}(x^{+},r)}\left[O\left(1\wedge\frac{\varepsilon}{|x_{2}-x^{+}|}\right)+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon|x_{2}-x^{+}|}{r^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}r\varepsilon\max_{B_{\#}(x_{0},R+r)}|H|\sup_{B(x_{0},R+r)}|M|

for some universal constant
C,C^{\prime}>0
.
Inserting Equations (
2.21
) and (
2.23
) in Equation (
2.20
) gives

|H(x^{+})-H(x^{-})|\leq C\varepsilon\left(r+\frac{1}{r}\right)\max_{B_{\#}(x_{0},R+r)}|H|.

(2.24)

for some constant
C=C(\mathcal{M})
depending only on
\mathcal{M}
and the bounded angle assumption, which is the desired result.
∎

We now state a lemma to say that massive harmonic functions with Dirichlet boundary conditions are small near the boundary: this is a
Beurling estimate
, and is very close in spirit and proof to Proposition 2.11 of
[
12
]
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Assume that for some
r>0
and
x\in\Gamma
,
H
satisfies
(-\Delta^{d}_{\#}+mI)H=0
on
B_{\#}(x,r)\cap\Gamma
.
Then,

|H(x)|\leq C\left(\frac{\dist(x,\overline{\Gamma}\setminus\Gamma)}{r}\right)^{\beta}\cdot\sup_{B_{\#}(x,r)}|H|.

(2.25)

for some universal constants
C=C(M),\beta>0
(depending only on the bounded angle assumption and the diameter of
\Omega
).
We emphasise that
\Delta^{d}_{\#}
denotes the Laplacian with Dirichlet boundary conditions on
\Gamma
, hence (implicitly)
H
is assumed to vanish outside
\Gamma
.

The proof of this lemma heavily relies on the positivity Assumption
1.5
.

Proof.

This proof is standard.
Recall that
X^{m}
denotes the killed random walk on
\Gamma^{\infty}
,
X
denotes the random walk with no killing (i.e.
m=0
).
Using Lemma 2.10 of
[
12
]
, see their Figure 3B, there exists a constant
p>0
such that for each
r>0
,
x\in\Gamma^{\infty}
, the probability starting from any
x^{\prime}\in B_{\#}(x,2r)\setminus B_{\#}(x,r)
that the random walk
X
does a full turn before leaving the annulus is bounded from below by some absolute constant
p>0
.
We want to show that the same holds for the
X^{m}
.
The real part
\Re(X)
is a martingale with quadratic variation bounded from below (see Equation (1.2) of
[
12
]
and around):
[X]_{t}\geq ct\varepsilon^{2}
for some constant
c>0
depending on the bounded angle assumption.
Hence, if
\tau
denotes the first time when
\Re(X)
is at distance at least
4r
from its starting point, applying the optional stopping theorem gives
\mathbb{E}[\tau]\leq 16r^{2}/(c\varepsilon^{2})
and by the Markov inequality, for all
T>0
,
\mathbb{P}[\tau\geq Tr^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}]\leq 16/(cT)
.
In particular, for
T=32/(cp)
, this probability is bounded from above by
p/2
.
Hence, by union bound, the probability that the random walk
X
starting from any
x^{\prime}\in B_{\#}(x,2r)\setminus B_{\#}(x,r)
does a full turn in time less than
Tr^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
is at least
p/2
.
Since the killing rate is
m=\varepsilon^{2}\mu M+O(\varepsilon^{3})=O(\varepsilon^{2})
, the probability that
X^{m}
is killed before time
Tr^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}
is bounded from below by some positive constant depending only on the diameter of
\Omega
.
Hence we obtain a new constant
p^{\prime}>0
depending only on
\sup_{B(x,r)\cap\Omega}|M|
and the diameter of
\Omega
, such that the probability that the killed random wall
X^{m}
does a full turn before leaving the annulus
B_{\#}(x,2r)\setminus B_{\#}(x,r)
or dying is bounded from below by
p^{\prime}
.

Let
H
,
r
be as in the statement of the lemma.
Recall that
H_{0}
denotes the function
H
extended by zero outside
\Gamma
.
If
\sigma
denotes the first escape time of
B_{\#}(x,r)\cap\Gamma
, then using Equation (
2.14
),

|H(x)|=|\mathbb{E}_{x}[H_{0}(X_{\sigma})]|\leq\mathbb{P}[X_{\sigma}\notin\Gamma]\cdot\sup_{B_{\#}(x,r)}|H|.

(2.26)

Recall that in this equation, if
X^{m}
dies before escaping
B_{\#}(x,r)\cap\Gamma
, then
H_{0}(X_{\sigma})=0
.
Denoting
d=\dist(x,\overline{\Gamma}\setminus\Gamma)
,
x
is surrounded by
n=\lfloor\log_{2}(r/d)\rfloor
annuli
B(x,2^{i+1}d)\setminus B(x,2^{i}d)\subset\Gamma\cap B(x,r)
.
In each of these annuli,
X^{m}
has a positive probability to make a full turn before dying or escaping the annulus: when this happens,
X^{m}
hits
\overline{\Gamma}\setminus\Gamma
before dying or escaping
B(x,r)
.
Hence

\mathbb{P}[X_{\sigma}\notin\Gamma]\leq(1-p^{\prime})^{n}\leq(1-p^{\prime})^{\log_{2}(r/d)-1}\leq(1-p^{\prime})^{-1}\left(\frac{d}{r}\right)^{-\log_{2}(1-p^{\prime})},

(2.27)

which concludes the proof.
∎

2.6
Discrete holomorphicity and discrete massive differentials

In addition to the discrete massive harmonic analysis tools developed in the beginning of the section, we must also develop a discrete holomorphicity theory to analyse discrete massive differentials.
This theory extends to the massive case the discrete holomorphicity theory of
[
12
]
.
Our first definitions and results are reminders of
[
12
]
.
For a rhombus
x^{-}y^{-}x^{+}y^{+}
with center
w\in W^{\infty}
, and angle
\theta
, we let

\displaystyle\mu_{W}(w)

\displaystyle=\text{Area}(x^{-}y^{-}x^{+}y^{+})=\varepsilon^{2}\sin(2\theta)

(2.28)

\displaystyle\mu_{wx^{\pm}}

\displaystyle=2\tan(\theta)\cdot(x^{\pm}-w)=\mathbf{i}\cdot(y^{\mp}-y^{\pm})

\displaystyle\mu_{wy^{\pm}}

\displaystyle=2\cot(\theta)\cdot(y^{\pm}-w)=\mathbf{i}\cdot(x^{\pm}-x^{\mp}).

For
b\in B^{\infty}
, we also set

\mu_{B}(b)=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{w\sim b}\mu_{W}(w)

(2.29)

where the sum is over the white vertices neighbouring
b
in the superposition (medial) graph. Note that for
x\in\Gamma
,
\mu_{B}(x)=\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x)/2
with
\mu
the weight defined in Equation (
2.1
).

Definition 2.14

(Definition 2.12 of
[
12
]
)
.

Let
w\in W^{\infty}
and
x^{-},y^{-},x^{+},y^{+}\in B^{\infty}
the vertices of the corresponding rhombus in clockwise order.
If
H
is defined on some subset of
B^{\infty}
including
x^{-},y^{-},x^{+},y^{+}
, let

\displaystyle(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{H(x^{+})-H(x^{-})}{x^{+}-x^{-}}+\frac{H(y^{+})-H(y^{-})}{y^{+}-y^{-}}\right]

(2.30)

\displaystyle[\overline{\partial}_{\#}H](w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{H(x^{+})-H(x^{-})}{\overline{x^{+}-x^{-}}}+\frac{H(y^{+})-H(y^{-})}{\overline{y^{+}-y^{-}}}\right]

When
H
is defined only on
\Gamma
, we also use this definition with the convention
H_{|\Gamma^{*}}=0
.

This definition can be rephrased as follows (see page 12 of
[
12
]
):

[\partial_{\#}H](w)=\frac{1}{4\mu_{W}(w)}\sum_{b\sim w}\overline{\mu_{wb}}H(b)\quad;\quad[\overline{\partial}_{\#}H](w)=\frac{1}{4\mu_{W}(w)}\sum_{b\sim w}\mu_{wb}H(b)

(2.31)

where the sum is taken over
b\in\{x^{-},y^{-},x^{+},y^{+}\}
that is the neighbours of
w
in the superposition graph. We also need to define a dual notion of holomorphicity for functions defined on white vertices.

Definition 2.15

(Definition 2.13 of
[
12
]
)
.

Let
b\in B^{\infty}
and
w_{i}\in W^{\infty}
be the neighbors of
b
in the superposition (medial) graph
G
with corresponding rhombi angles
\theta_{i}
. If a function
F
is defined on some subset of
W^{\infty}
including the
w_{i}
, define

[\overline{\partial}_{\#}F](b)=-\frac{1}{4\mu_{B}(b)}\sum_{i}\mu_{w_{i}b}F(w_{i})\quad;\quad[\partial_{\#}F](b)=-\frac{1}{4\mu_{B}(b)}\sum_{i}\overline{\mu_{w_{i}b}}F(w_{i}).

We call
F

discrete holomorphic
at
b
if
[\overline{\partial}_{\#}F](b)=0
.

Note that we slightly abuse notation by using the same notation as in Definition
2.14
, but since the definitions apply to different objects there is no risk of confusion.

Proposition 2.16

(Proposition 2.14 of
[
12
]
)
.

Let
H
be defined on a subset of
B^{\infty}
. Then

[\Delta_{\#}H](b)=8\mu_{B}(b)[\overline{\partial}_{\#}\partial_{\#}H](b)

at all
b\in B^{\infty}
where the right-hand side makes sense.
When
b\in(\Gamma^{\infty})^{*}
, the right-hand side is the (dual) discrete Laplacian on the graph
(\Gamma^{\infty})^{*}
with conductances
c^{0}_{y^{-}y^{+}}=1/c^{0}_{x^{-}x^{+}}
(for all rhombi
x^{-}y^{-}x^{+}y^{+}
).

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward computation, see the proof of Proposition 2.14 in
[
12
]
. The factor
\mu_{B}(b)
comes from our different normalization of the Laplacian.
∎

Definition 2.17
.

We say that a function
F:W^{\infty}\to\mathbb{C}
is a
massive discrete differential
(on a part of
W^{\infty}
) if it is the discrete derivative of a massive harmonic function
F=\partial_{\#}H
with
H:\Gamma^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
. In this case, by the proposition above and Equation (
2.6
),

[\overline{\partial}_{\#}F](x)=\frac{m(x)}{4\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x)}H(x)=\frac{M(x)+O(\varepsilon)}{4}H(x).

Note that this definition is more restrictive than the corresponding definition of discrete holomorphic functions in
[
12
]
, but this will be enough for our purposes.

Remark 2.18
.

In the massless case, this notion coincides with that of a discrete holomorphic function, as used for instance in
[
12
]
. This is natural from a continuous perspective: a continuous (non-massive) holomorphic function on a simply connected domain is always the gradient of a continuous (non-massive) harmonic function, which makes this definition natural.

Although the definition above seems like a natural generalization of this notion to the massive case, we will see that in fact discrete massive differentials do not in general converge in the scaling limit to massive holomorphic functions in the sense of Definition
1.6
. This is why we refer to functions satisfying the above definition as
massive discrete differentials
.

In our massive setting, the correct definition of a discrete massive holomorphic function would be any function in the kernel of the Kasteleyn matrix. We expect these functions to converge in the scaling limit towards continuous massive holomorphic functions in the sense of Definition
1.6
: for example we prove this for the inverse Kasteleyn matrix in Theorem
1.13
.

The basic building block of the discrete holomorphicity theory is the
discrete Cauchy kernel
, which is the discrete analogue of the Cauchy kernel
k(z,w)=1/[2\mathbf{i}\pi(z-w)]
in complex analysis. It was introduced by Kenyon in
[
21
]
for periodic isoradial graphs, but his arguments extend to the non-periodic isoradial case, see Appendix A.1 of
[
12
]
. For complex numbers
z,\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
, let

\mathrm{Proj}[z,\zeta]=\Re\left(z\frac{\overline{\zeta}}{|\zeta|}\right)\frac{\zeta}{|\zeta|}

(2.32)

denote the orthogonal projection of
z
onto the line
\zeta\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 2.19

(Theorems 4.1, 4.2, 4.3 of
[
21
]
, Theorem 2.21 of
[
12
]
)
.

Let
w_{2}\in W^{\infty}
. There exists a unique function
k=\mathcal{K}(\cdot,w_{2}):B^{\infty}\to\mathbb{C}
called the Cauchy kernel such that

•

[\overline{\partial}_{\#}k](w_{1})=0
for all
w_{1}\neq w_{2}
and
[\overline{\partial}_{\#}k](w_{2})=1/\mu_{W}(w_{2})

•

|k(b_{1})|\to 0
as
|b_{1}-w_{2}|\to\infty
.

The Cauchy kernel has the following asymptotics:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(x_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\mathrm{Proj}\left[\frac{1}{x_{1}-w_{2}};\overline{x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}}\right]+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}}\right)\quad;\quad x_{1}\in\Gamma^{\infty}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(y_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\mathrm{Proj}\left[\frac{1}{y_{1}-w_{2}};\overline{y_{2}^{+}-y_{2}^{-}}\right]+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|y_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}}\right)\quad;\quad y_{1}\in(\Gamma^{\infty})^{*}

where
x_{2}^{+}y_{2}^{+}x_{2}^{-}y_{2}^{-}
denotes the rhombus with center
w_{2}
and the
O
holds uniformly in
\varepsilon,x_{1},y_{1},w_{2}
and the isoradial graph
\Gamma^{\infty}
.

The discrete Cauchy formula of Proposition 2.22 of
[
12
]
can be adapted to the massive setting as follows. Let
\Lambda\subset\Gamma^{\infty}
denote a simply connected subgraph and
\Lambda^{\star}
denote its restricted dual (see Figure
4
and below).
Let
x_{0}w_{0}x_{1}w_{1}\dots x_{n}w_{n}
denote the closed counterclockwise polyline boundary of the simply connected domain obtained by gluing all faces of
\Lambda
(see Figure
4
), with vertices alternating between
\Lambda
and
W
.
Denote

\partial\Lambda=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\}\quad;\quad\partial W(\Lambda)=\{w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{n}\},

respectively the black and white vertices on the thick red outer line of Figure
4
.

\begin{overpic}[fig_chelkak_smirnov.pdf]
\put(6.0,48.0){$x_{i}$}
\put(4.0,16.0){$x_{i+1}$}
\put(7.0,31.0){$w_{i}$}
\put(22.0,32.0){$y(w_{i})$}
\put(77.0,33.0){$y_{k+1}$}
\put(76.0,21.0){$y_{k}$}
\put(87.0,26.0){$\tilde{w}_{k}$}
\put(100.0,19.0){$x(\tilde{w}_{k})$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4:
A simply connected subgraph
\Lambda
of
\Gamma^{\infty}
, in black. Its restricted dual
\Lambda^{\star}
in grey.
The polylines
x_{0}w_{0}\dots x_{n}w_{n}
and
y_{0}\tilde{w}_{0}\dots y_{m}\tilde{w}_{m}
are respectively the outer thick red line and inner thick blue line.

Note the difference with the notation
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Lambda
for the outer vertex boundary, which we introduced in Section
2.3
.
For all
i
,
x_{i},x_{i+1}
are the opposite vertices of a rhombus with center
w_{i}
.
Let us denote by
y(w_{i})\in\Lambda^{\star}
the unique vertex of this rhombus lying strictly inside the polyline (see Figure
4
).
For
F:W\to\mathbb{C},G:\Lambda^{\star}\to\mathbb{C}
, we can define a contour integral

\displaystyle\oint_{\partial\Lambda}F(w)G(y)d_{\#}w

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=0}^{n}F(w_{i})G(y(w_{i}))\cdot(x_{i+1}-x_{i}),

(2.33)

with
x_{n+1}=x_{0}
. Similarly, let
y_{0}\tilde{w}_{0}y_{1}\tilde{w}_{1}\dots y_{m}\tilde{w}_{m}
denote the closed polyline path, enumerated in counter-clockwise order, passing through the centers of all faces touching
x_{0}x_{1}\dots x_{n}
from inside (see also Figure
4
).
Denote

\partial\Lambda^{\star}=\{y_{0},\dots,y_{m}\}\quad;\quad\partial W(\Lambda^{\star})=\{\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{w}_{1},\dots,\tilde{w}_{m}\},

respectively the grey and white vertices on the thick blue inner line of Figure
4
.
For all
k
,
y_{k},y_{k+1}
are the opposite vertices of a rhombus with center
\tilde{k}_{i}
.
Let us denote by
x(\tilde{w}_{k})\in\partial\Lambda
the unique black vertex of the rhombus containing
\tilde{w}_{k}
lying on the polyline
x_{0}w_{0}\dots x_{n}w_{n}
.
For
F:W\to\mathbb{C},G:\Lambda\to\mathbb{C}
, we define a contour integral

\displaystyle\oint_{\partial\Lambda^{\star}}F(w)G(x)d_{\#}w

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=0}^{m}F(\tilde{w}_{i})G(x(\tilde{w}_{i}))\cdot(y_{i+1}-y_{i}).

(2.34)

Finally, let
W(\Lambda)
, resp.
B(\Lambda)
, denote the set of white, resp. black, vertices inside (or on the boundary of) the closed polyline
x_{0}\dots w_{n}
(note that
B(\Lambda)
contains both primal and dual black vertices). Define

\mathrm{Int}\Lambda=\Lambda\setminus\partial\Lambda\quad;\quad\mathrm{Int}W(\Lambda)=W(\Lambda)\setminus\partial W(\Lambda),\quad;\quad\mathrm{Int}B(\Lambda)=B(\Lambda)\setminus\partial\Lambda.

(2.35)

Note that
\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda)\subset\Gamma^{\infty}
contains all
primal
black vertices (strictly) inside the polyline
x_{0},\ldots,w_{n}
, whereas
\mathrm{Int}B(\Lambda)\subset\Gamma^{\infty}\cup(\Gamma^{\infty})^{*}
contains
all
black vertices (whether primal or dual) strictly inside that polyline. Thus,
\mathrm{Int}B(\Lambda)=\mathrm{Int}\Lambda\cup\Lambda^{\star}
; where recall that
\Lambda^{\star}
denotes the restricted dual.

Up to now everything was as in
[
12
]
, but we now start discussing how this theory needs to be adapted in our massive context. We are ready to adapt the discrete Cauchy formula Proposition 2.22 of
[
12
]
to the massive setting.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
\Lambda\subset\Gamma^{\infty}
be a simply connected subgraph and
H:\Gamma^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
be a real discrete massive harmonic function on
\mathrm{Int}\Lambda
.
Denote by
F:=\partial_{\#}H:W^{\infty}\to\mathbb{C}
.
Recall that this is what we call a discrete massive differential.
Then, for any
w_{*}\in\mathrm{Int}W(\Lambda)
,

F(w_{*})=\frac{1}{4\mathbf{i}}\left[\oint_{\partial\Lambda}F(w)\mathcal{K}(y,w_{*})dw+\oint_{\partial\Lambda^{\star}}F(w)\mathcal{K}(x,w_{*})dw\right]-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda)}\mathcal{K}(x,w_{*})m(x)H(x).

Proof.

The proof is the same as in
[
12
]
, taking into account the massive terms. By the definitions of the discrete Cauchy kernel and of
\overline{\partial}_{\#}
(see the alternative definition Equation (
2.31
)),

\displaystyle 4F(w_{*})

\displaystyle=\underset{w\in\mathrm{Int}W(\Lambda),b\sim w,b\in B(\Lambda)}{\sum\sum}F(w)\mu_{wb}\mathcal{K}(b,w_{*})

\displaystyle=\underset{b\in B(\Lambda),w\sim b,w\in\mathrm{Int}W(\Lambda)}{\sum\sum}\mathcal{K}(b,w_{*})\mu_{wb}F(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda,w\sim x,w\in\partial W(\Lambda^{\star})}\mathcal{K}(x,w_{0})\mu_{wx}F(w)-\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda^{\star},w\sim y,w\in\partial W(\Lambda)}\mathcal{K}(y,w_{*})\mu_{wy}F(w)

\displaystyle+\underset{b\in\mathrm{Int}B(\Lambda),w\sim b,w\in W(\Lambda)}{\sum\sum}\mathcal{K}(b,w_{0})\mu_{wb}F(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda,w\sim x,w\in\partial W(\Lambda^{\star})}\mathcal{K}(x,w_{*})\mu_{wx}F(w)-\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda^{\star},w\sim y,w\in\partial W(\Lambda)}\mathcal{K}(y,w_{*})\mu_{wy}F(w)

\displaystyle-4\sum_{b\in\mathrm{Int}B(\Lambda)}\mu_{B}(b)\mathcal{K}(b,w_{*})[\overline{\partial}_{\#}F](b).

Note that the first two sums are exactly the integrals appearing in the statement of the proposition by Equation (
2.28
), up to a factor
\mathbf{i}
. For instance,

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda,w\sim x,w\in\partial W(\Lambda^{\star})}\mathcal{K}(x,w_{*})\mu_{wx}F(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\partial W(\Lambda^{*})}\mathcal{K}(x(w),w_{*})\mu_{w,x(w)}F(w)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{i}\sum_{i=0}^{m}F(\tilde{w}_{i})\mathcal{K}(x(\tilde{w}_{i}),w_{*})(y_{i+1}-y_{i})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{i}\oint_{\partial\Lambda^{\star}}F(w)\mathcal{K}(x,w_{*})dw.

Likewise,

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda^{\star},w\sim y,w\in\partial W(\Lambda)}\mathcal{K}(y,w_{*})\mu_{wy}F(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\partial W(\Lambda)}F(w)\mathcal{K}(y(w),w_{*})\mu_{w,y(w)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}F(w_{i})\mathcal{K}(y(w_{i}),w_{*})\mathbf{i}(x_{i+1}-x_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{i}\oint_{\partial\Lambda}F(w)\mathcal{K}(y,w_{*})dw

We identify the last term. For
x\in\mathrm{Int}\Lambda
,

4\mu_{B}(x)[\overline{\partial}_{\#}F](x)=4\mu_{B}(x)[\overline{\partial}_{\#}\partial_{\#}H](x)=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{\#}H)(x)=\frac{1}{2}m(x)H(x)

since
H
is massive harmonic on
\mathrm{Int}\Lambda
. For
y\in\Lambda^{\star}
, by definition

[\overline{\partial}_{\#}F](y)=[\overline{\partial}_{\#}\partial_{\#}H](x)=\frac{1}{8\mu_{B}(y)}[\Delta_{\#}H](y)=0

since in the definition of
F=\partial_{\#}H
we used the convention
H_{|(\Gamma^{\infty})^{*}}=0
(see Definition
2.14
).
∎

As in Corollary 2.23 of
[
12
]
, we provide an asymptotic form of the discrete Cauchy formula using Theorem
2.19
.

Corollary 2.21
.

Let
\Lambda\subset\Gamma^{\infty}
be a simply connected subgraph and
H:\Gamma^{\infty}\to\mathbb{C}
be a discrete massive harmonic function on
\mathrm{Int}\Lambda
. Denote by
F:=\partial_{\#}H:W^{\infty}\to\mathbb{C}
. For any
w_{0}\in W(\Lambda)
, denote by
x_{0}^{+}y_{0}^{+}x_{0}^{-}y_{0}^{-}
the corresponding rhombus, in counterclockwise order. Then,

\displaystyle F(w_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Proj}\left[\frac{1}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\left(\oint_{\partial\Gamma}\frac{F(w)}{w-w_{0}}d_{\#}w+\oint_{\partial\Lambda^{\star}}\frac{F(w)}{w-w_{0}}d_{\#}w\right)-\sum_{x\in\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda)}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x)M(x)H(x)}{\pi(x-w_{0})};\overline{x_{0}^{+}-x_{0}^{-}}\right]

\displaystyle+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon\mathcal{F}\mathcal{L}}{d^{2}}\right)+O\left(\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}\varepsilon\log\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)

where
d=\dist(w_{0},\partial\Lambda^{\star})
,
\mathcal{H}=\max_{x\in\Lambda}|H(x)|
,
\mathcal{F}=\max_{w\in\partial W(\Lambda)\cup\partial B(\Lambda)}|F(w)|
and
\mathcal{L}=O(\varepsilon(n+m))
is the sum of the length of the polylines
\partial\Lambda
and
\partial\Lambda^{*}
,
\mathcal{M}=\sup_{x\in\Lambda}|M(x)|
,
\mathcal{D}=\diam(\Lambda)
(the diameter of the simply connected set obtained by gluing all faces).

Remark 2.22
.

The analogous result of
[
12
]
establishes separate asymptotics for the two different projections
\mathcal{B}F
and
\mathcal{W}F
(which we did not define). In our case, since
F=\partial_{\#}H
and
H
is real on
\Lambda
and vanishes on
\Lambda^{\star}
,
F
coincides with
\mathcal{B}F
by Remark 2.18 of
[
12
]
.

Proof.

Again, the proof is exactly the same as the proof of Corollary 2.23 of
[
12
]
, with an additional mass term. Let
w\in\partial W(\Lambda^{\star})
correspond to the rhombus
x_{1}y_{1}x_{2}y_{2}
. Note that the edge
y_{1}y_{2}
of the dual graph
\Lambda^{\star}
belongs to the polyline
y_{0}\dots y_{m}
, hence
d_{\#}w=y_{2}-y_{1}
, and since furthermore
H|_{\Gamma^{*}}=0
,

\frac{F(w)d_{\#}w}{4\mathbf{i}}=\frac{(H(x_{2})-H(x_{1}))(y_{2}-y_{1})}{8\mathbf{i}(x_{2}-x_{1})}\in\mathbb{R}

because
y_{2}-y_{1}
and
x_{2}-x_{1}
are orthogonal. Hence by the asymptotics of Theorem
2.19
,

\mathcal{K}(y(w),w_{0})\cdot\frac{F(w)d_{\#}w}{4\mathbf{i}}=\mathrm{Proj}\left[\frac{F(w)d_{\#}w}{2\mathbf{i}\pi(y(w)-w_{0})};\overline{x_{0}^{+}-x_{0}^{-}}\right]+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon\mathcal{F}|d_{\#}w|}{d^{2}}\right).

Similarly, let
w\in\partial B(\Lambda)
correspond to the rhombus
x_{1}y_{1}x_{2}y_{2}
. Note that the edge
x_{1}x_{2}
of the primal graph belongs to the polyline
x_{0}\dots x_{n}
: hence
d_{\#}w=x_{2}-x_{1}
, and

\frac{F(w)d_{\#}w}{4\mathbf{i}}=\frac{H(x_{2})-H(x_{1})}{8\mathbf{i}}\in\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}.

Hence by the asymptotics of Theorem
2.19
, and using the fact that
\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Proj}(z;\zeta)=\mathrm{Proj}(\mathbf{i}z,\mathbf{i}\zeta)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(x(w),w_{0})\cdot\frac{F(w)d_{\#}w}{4\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Proj}\left[\frac{F(w)d_{\#}w}{2\mathbf{i}\pi(x(w)-w_{0})};\mathbf{i}\overline{y_{0}^{+}-y_{0}^{-}}\right]+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon\mathcal{F}|d_{\#}w|}{d^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Proj}\left[\frac{F(w)d_{\#}w}{2\mathbf{i}\pi(x(w)-w_{0})};\overline{x_{0}^{+}-x_{0}^{-}}\right]+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon\mathcal{F}|d_{\#}w|}{d^{2}}\right)

since
y_{0}^{+}-y_{0}^{-}
is orthogonal to
x_{0}^{+}-x_{0}^{-}
. Finally, for
x\in\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda)
, using Equation (
2.6
),

\mathcal{K}(x,w_{0})m(x)H(x)=\mathrm{Proj}\left[\frac{2\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x)M(x)H(x)}{\pi(x-w_{0})};\overline{x_{0}^{+}-x_{0}^{-}}\right]+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{3}\mathcal{H}\mathcal{M}}{|x-w_{0}|^{2}}\right).

Since

\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{|x-w_{0}|^{2}}=O\left(\varepsilon\log\left(\frac{\mathcal{D}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right),

we finally obtain the corollary.
∎

For
F:W^{\infty}\to\mathbb{C}
, we define an averaging operator

[A_{\#}F](z):=\frac{1}{4\mu_{B}(z)}\sum_{w\sim z,w\in W^{\infty}}\mu_{W}(w)F(w)\quad;\quad z\in B^{\infty}.

The point is that, when
F
is a massive discrete differential, it is actually not that well behaved at the discrete level itself; rather, it should be viewed as the projection onto a direction
x_{0}^{-},x_{0}^{+}
– which is lattice-dependent – of
A_{\#}F
, which
is
well behaved (e.g., it is
\beta
-Hölder for all
\beta<1
, as we will see below).

We now provide an analogue of Proposition 2.24 of
[
12
]
: for a massive harmonic function
H
on
\Gamma^{\infty}
we are able to define a
discrete gradient
of
H
(namely,
A_{\#}F
where
F=\partial_{\#}H
below) such that the discrete derivative in a given direction is close to the projection of the discrete gradient in this direction. We are also able to prove that this discrete gradient is well-behaved.

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{C}
,
R>r>0
and recall that
B_{\#}(u,R)\subset\Gamma^{\infty}
denotes the discretized euclidean ball. Assume that
H
is massive harmonic in
\mathrm{Int}B_{\#}(u,R)
, and denote by
F=\partial_{\#}H
. Then for all
x_{0}\in B(u,r)
, for all
x_{0}\sim w_{0}\in W^{\infty}
,

\left|F(w_{0})-\mathrm{Proj}\left[2[A_{\#}F](x_{0});\overline{w_{0}-x_{0}}\right]\right|=O\left(\frac{\mathcal{F}\varepsilon R}{(R-r)^{2}}\right)+O\left(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{H}\varepsilon\log\left(\frac{2R}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).

Moreover, for all
r>0
,
x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}\in B_{\#}(u,r)
,

\left|[A_{\#}F](x_{0}^{\prime})-[A_{\#}F](x_{0})\right|=O\left(\frac{|x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}|R\mathcal{F}}{(R-r)^{2}}\right)+O\left(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{H}|x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}|\left(1+\log\left(\frac{R}{|x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}|}\right)\right)\right)

In fact this phenomenon is already apparent in the Cauchy formula above. If we had alternatively defined
A_{\#}F(x_{0})
as the term inside the projection in the right-hand side of the Cauchy formula, that is

\frac{1}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\left(\oint_{\partial\Gamma}\frac{F(w)}{w-x_{0}}d_{\#}w+\oint_{\partial\Lambda^{\star}}\frac{F(w)}{w-x_{0}}d_{\#}w\right)-\sum_{x\in\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda)\setminus\{x_{0}\}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x)M(x)H(x)}{\pi(x-x_{0})},

this proposition would be a direct corollary of the discrete massive Cauchy formula. For later purposes, and following
[
12
]
, we prefer to use the definition of
A_{\#}
as the local average.
Also note that our result in the massive setting is weaker than the corresponding result in the non-massive case: the discrete gradient is not Lipschitz (because of the
\log\varepsilon
factor).

Proof.

Once again, the proof is similar to the proof of Proposition 2.24 in
[
12
]
. We apply to
\Lambda=B_{\#}(u,R)
the asymptotic Cauchy formula of Corollary
2.21
. Note that

\mathcal{L}\leq CR

for some absolute constant
C
, and that
\mathcal{D}=2R
. For all
w_{0}\sim x_{0}
,
|w_{0}-x_{0}|\leq\varepsilon
so for all
w\in\partial W(\Lambda)
,

\left|\frac{1}{w-w_{0}}-\frac{1}{w-x_{0}}\right|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon}{(R-r)^{2}}.

for some absolute constant
C
. Moreover, for all
x\in\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda)
,
|x_{0}-w_{0}|\leq\varepsilon
hence for all
x\in\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda)\setminus\{x_{0}\}
,

\left|\frac{1}{x-w_{0}}-\frac{1}{x-x_{0}}\right|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon}{|x-x_{0}|^{2}}.

and since
|x_{0}-w_{0}|\geq c\varepsilon
for some absolute constant
c>0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{x\in\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda)}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{x-w_{0}}-\sum_{x\in\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda)\setminus\{x_{0}\}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{x-x_{0}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\varepsilon+\sum_{x\in\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda))\setminus\{x_{0}\}}\frac{C\varepsilon^{2}}{|x-x_{0}|^{2}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{H}\varepsilon\log\left(\frac{2R}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).

The asymptotic Cauchy formula implies

F(w_{0})=\mathrm{Proj}[S(x_{0});\overline{w_{0}-x_{0}}]+{O\left(\frac{\mathcal{F}\varepsilon R}{(R-r)^{2}}\right)+}O\left(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{H}\varepsilon\log\left(\frac{2R}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)

(2.36)

with

S(x_{0})=\frac{1}{2\mathbf{i}\pi}\left(\oint_{\partial\Lambda}\frac{F(w)}{w-x_{0}}d_{\#}w+\oint_{\partial\Lambda^{*}}\frac{F(w)}{w-x_{0}}d_{\#}w\right)-\sum_{x\in\mathrm{Int}(\Lambda)\setminus\{x_{0}\}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x)M(x)H(x)}{\pi(x-x_{0})}.

Note that
S(x_{0})
does not depend on
w_{0}
, hence summing over
w_{0}\sim x_{0}
and using the identity

\frac{1}{4\mu_{B}(x_{0})}\sum_{w_{0}\sim x_{0}}\mu_{W}(w_{0})\mathrm{Proj}[S;\overline{w_{0}-x_{0}}]=\frac{S}{2}

valid for all
S\in\mathbb{C}
(see the proof of Proposition 2.24 of
[
12
]
for details), we obtain

[A_{\#}F](x_{0})=\frac{S(x_{0})}{2}+{O\left(\frac{\mathcal{F}\varepsilon R}{(R-r)^{2}}\right)+}O\left(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{H}\varepsilon\log\left(\frac{2R}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)

(2.37)

so in particular for
w_{0}\sim x_{0}
, Equation (
2.36
) implies that

\left|F(w_{0})-\mathrm{Proj}[2[A_{\#}F](x_{0});\overline{x_{0}-w_{0}}]\right|=O\left(\frac{\mathcal{F}\varepsilon}{R}\right)+O\left(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{H}\varepsilon\log\left(\frac{2R}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).

This proves the first point of the lemma.
To prove the second point, let
x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}\in B_{\#}(u,r)
. We can apply the first point in the ball
B_{\#}(u,R)
to
x_{0}
and
x_{0}^{\prime}
to obtain, for
x\in\{x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}\}
,

[A_{\#}F](x)=\frac{S(x)}{2}+O\left(\frac{\mathcal{F}\varepsilon R}{(R-r)^{2}}\right)+O\left(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{H}\varepsilon\log\left(\frac{2R}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)

(2.38)

Let
d=|x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}|
. On the one hand, for
w\in\partial W(B_{\#}(u,R))
,

\left|\frac{1}{w-x_{0}}-\frac{1}{w-x_{0}^{\prime}}\right|\leq\frac{d}{(R-r)^{2}}.

Hence,

\left|\oint_{\partial B_{\#}(u,R)}\frac{F(w)}{w-x_{0}}d_{\#}w-\oint_{\partial B_{\#}(u,R)}\frac{F(w)}{w-x_{0}^{\prime}}d_{\#}w\right|=O\left(\frac{dR\mathcal{F}}{(R-r)^{2}}\right),

and the same holds for the second integral.
On the other hand, for
x\in\mathrm{Int}(B_{\#}(u,R))\setminus\{x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}\}
,

\left|\frac{1}{x-x_{0}}-\frac{1}{x-x_{0}^{\prime}}\right|=\frac{d}{|x-x_{0}||x-x_{0}^{\prime}|}\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}2/|x-x_{0}|&\text{ when }x\in B(x_{0},d/2)\\
2/|x-x_{0}^{\prime}|&\text{ when }x\in B(x_{0}^{\prime},d/2)\\
3d/|x-x_{1}|^{2}&\text{ otherwise }.\end{array}\right.

Hence, splitting the sum in three, we obtain

\left|\sum_{x\in\mathrm{Int}(B_{\#}(u,R))\setminus\{x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}\}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{x-x_{0}}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{x-x_{0}^{\prime}}\right|\leq Cd\left(1+\log\left(\frac{R}{d}\right)\right)

for some absolute constant
C
. Finally, we get

\left|S(x_{0})-S(x_{0}^{\prime})\right|=O\left(\frac{dR\mathcal{F}}{(R-r)^{2}}\right)+O\left(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{H}d\left(1+\log\left(\frac{R}{d}\right)\right)\right).

Combining this with Equation (
2.38
), since
\varepsilon\leq d
, this concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

2.7
Convergence of the discrete massive Green function

We state a lemma to prove convergence of the discrete massive Green function with Dirichlet boundary conditions towards its continuous analog.
This lemma heavily relies on the Assumption
1.2
that the graph
\Gamma
approximates the domain
\Omega
.

Lemma 2.24
.

Suppose
x_{1}=x_{1}(\varepsilon),x_{2}=x_{2}(\varepsilon)\in\Gamma
.
Then, uniformly for
(x_{1},x_{2})
in compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
,

G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})=G^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})+o(1).

If
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary near
\beta^{*}
(say on the ball
B=B(\beta^{*},\eta)
, so that
L=\partial\Omega\cap B
is straight, recall that
\Gamma_{\varphi}=\Gamma\cup\varphi(\Gamma\cap B)
and
\Omega_{\varphi}=\Omega\cup B
, where
\varphi
is the reflection across the straight line
L
defined in Section
2.3
.
Recall that in this case,
G_{\#}^{m}
is naturally extended to
\Gamma_{\varphi}
.
Then, uniformly for
x_{1}\in\Gamma_{\varphi}
over compact subsets of
\Omega_{\varphi}
, and
x_{2}\in\Gamma_{\varphi}
,

G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})=O\left(1+|\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|\wedge|\varphi(x_{1})-x_{2}|)|\right)

where we set
\varphi(x_{1})=\infty
if
x_{1}\notin B
.

Proof.

The second point of the lemma is a direct consequence of Lemmas
2.10
and
A.1
.

We now prove the first point of the lemma.
By Lemma
A.1
, Lemma
2.12
and (ii) of Assumption
1.2
,
G_{\#}^{m}
is a (sequence of) Lipschitz function in each variable, uniformly bounded over
\varepsilon>0
and compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
, with uniform Lipschitz constants over
\varepsilon
and over compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
.
By Lemma
B.1
,
G_{\#}^{m}
is hence Lipschitz as a function of two variables, with uniform Lipschitz constant over
\varepsilon
and over compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
.
Hence by the Arzela-Ascoli theorem we can find a subsequence along which
G_{\#}^{m}\to g\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2}),\mathbb{R})
uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
.

To conclude the proof, it only remains to identify the limit
g
.
Let
\chi_{1}\in\Omega
. The rest of the proof consists in identifying
g(\chi_{1},\cdot)
uniquely.

Let us identify first the boundary conditions for
g(\chi_{1},\cdot)
.
Let
\chi_{2}\in\Omega
,
|\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}|=:2d>0
.
Let
x_{1}=x_{1}(\varepsilon),x_{2}=x_{2}(\varepsilon)\in\Gamma
be such that
x_{1}\to\chi_{1},x_{2}\to\chi_{2}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
By Lemma
A.1
and Lemma
2.13
, we obtain that for all
x_{2}\in\Gamma\cap B(\chi_{2},d)
,

|G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})|\leq C(1+|\log d|)\left(\frac{\dist(x_{2},\overline{\Gamma}\setminus\Gamma)}{d}\right)^{\beta}.

By (i) and (ii)
of Assumption
1.2
,
\dist(x_{2},\overline{\Gamma}\setminus\Gamma)\overset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}\dist(\chi_{2},\partial\Omega)
. Thus, in the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit, we obtain

|g(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})|\leq C(1+|\log|\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}|)\left(\frac{\dist(\chi_{2},\partial\Omega)}{|\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}|}\right)^{\beta}.

(2.39)

hence
g(\chi_{1},\cdot)
can be extended by
0
on
\partial\Omega
.
More precisely, if
\overline{g}(\chi_{1},\cdot)
coincides with
g(\chi_{1},\cdot)
on
\Omega
and
\overline{g}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})=0
for
\chi_{2}\in\partial\Omega
, then
\overline{g}(\chi_{1},\cdot)\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{\Omega}\setminus\{\chi_{1}\},\mathbb{R})
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
be a smooth test function compactly supported in
\Omega
.
We apply the discrete Green formula Equation (
2.17
) on
\Gamma
with the two functions
G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},\cdot)
and
f
to obtain

f(x_{1})+\sum_{x_{2}\in\Gamma\cap\supp f}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})\left(m(x_{2})-\Delta_{\#}f(x_{2})\right)=0.

(2.40)

Recall that
m(x_{2})=\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x_{2})M(x_{2})+O(\varepsilon^{3})
and note that the discrete Laplacian approximates the continuum Laplacian as
\varepsilon\to 0
, more precisely

\Delta_{\#}f(x_{2})=\varepsilon^{2}\mu(x_{2})\Delta f(x_{2})+O(\varepsilon^{3})

(see Lemma 2.2 of
[
12
]
).
Moreover, by the law of sines,
\mu(x_{2})
is half the sum of the areas of the rhombi around
x_{2}
.
Hence the sum is a Riemann sum for the function
g(\chi_{1},\cdot)(M-\Delta f)
on
\Omega
.
Since
g(\chi_{1},\cdot)
has a singularity at
x_{1}
, we must control the singularity to obtain convergence of the Riemann sum towards an integral.
Since

|G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})|\leq|G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2})|=O(1+\log|x_{1}-x_{2}|)

(2.41)

and since
\log
is integrable around the origin in two dimensions (or rather, using crude bounds for the sum below),
we deduce that for all
s>0
there exists
c(s)>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that

\left|\sum_{x_{2}\in\Gamma\cap\supp f\cap B(x_{1},s)}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})\left(m(x_{2})-\Delta_{\#}f(x_{2})\right)\right|\leq c(s)

with

c(s)\overset{s\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0.

This implies convergence of the Riemann sums. Taking the limit along the subsequence considered previously, we obtain

f+\int_{\Omega}g(\chi_{1},\cdot)(M-\Delta f)=0.

(2.42)

In other words,
g(\chi_{1},\cdot)
is a weak solution of the equation

\Delta g=Mg+\delta_{x}

(2.43)

with zero boundary condition on
\partial\Omega
.
By the uniqueness in Definition
1.11
(recall the elliptic regularity arguments such as
[
27
, Chapter 10.1, (5)]
), this implies that
g(\chi_{1},\cdot)=G^{m}(\chi_{1},\cdot)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

3
Scaling limit of inverse Kasteleyn matrix and height function

In this section, we establish the convergence of
(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
and
(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
and identify the limit, where we write
(\partial_{1})_{\#}
and
(\partial_{2})_{\#}
for the discrete derivatives with respect to the first or second variable respectively. In the non-massive case
m=0
, these results were obtained by Li in
[
26
]
. Note that an alternative proof of our convergence statements could be obtained by plugging her results in the resolvent identity. However, we preferred to develop a discrete holomorphicity theory for discrete massive differentials, which is interesting in itself and provides a more robust and self-contained proof.

3.1
Convergence of first derivative of discrete massive Green function

We state a lemma to prove convergence of the first order discrete derivative of the discrete massive Green function towards its continuous analog. Before stating the lemma, we need to introduce some notation.
We identify a complex number
z=a+\mathbf{i}b
with the vector
(a,b)
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and denote by
\langle\cdot;\cdot\rangle
the scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, that is,

\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle=\Re(z_{1}\overline{z_{2}}).

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
x_{1}=x_{1}(\varepsilon)\in\Gamma
and
w_{2}=w_{2}(\varepsilon)\in W
corresponding to the middle of the edge
x_{2}^{-}x_{2}^{+}
. Uniformly for
x_{1},w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
,

\displaystyle(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\overline{\nabla_{2}}G^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})+o_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)

\displaystyle(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla_{2}G^{m}(x_{1},w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+o_{\varepsilon\to 0}(\varepsilon)

where
\nabla_{2}
denotes the (ordinary) gradient in the second variable of the real-valued function
G^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})
; equivalently
\nabla_{2}G^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})=\bar{\partial}_{2}(G^{m}(x_{1},w_{2}))
when it is viewed as a complex-valued function.

Uniformly for
x_{1},w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega
,

(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|}\right).

(3.1)

When
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
\partial\Omega\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)=L
,
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
can be replaced by
\Omega_{\varphi}^{2}\setminus\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\Omega_{\varphi}^{2}\colon x_{1}=x_{2}\text{ or }x_{1}=\varphi(x_{2})\}
in the first statement and
\Omega
can be replaced by
\Omega_{\varphi}
in the second statement if we replace
|x_{1}-w_{2}|
by
|x_{1}-w_{2}|\wedge|\varphi(x_{1})-w_{2}|
.

Proof.

We start by proving the first point of the lemma. Define
F(x_{1},w_{2}):=[(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}](x_{1},w_{2})
and
(A_{2})_{\#}F(x_{1},x_{2}):=[(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}](x_{1},w_{2})
. The proof follows two steps:

•

Step 1: we prove that
(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
is a sequence of equi-continuous functions of two variables on compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
.

•

Step 2: we show that any sub-sequential limit of
(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
is
\frac{1}{4}\overline{\nabla_{2}}G^{m}

First step.

The main idea is to combine Lemma
2.12
and Proposition
2.23
with the bound Lemma
A.1
to prove that
(A_{2})_{\#}F
is Lipschitz in the first variable,
\beta
-Holder in the second variable for any
\beta<1
with constants independent of
\varepsilon
.
Then, we will be able to use the Arzela–Ascoli theorem.

We now give the details.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}\subset\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
be a compact subset. Here and below, if
\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega
is a set, its interior will be denoted by
\mathring{\mathcal{C}}
.
By Lemma
A.1
,

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}|G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})|<\infty.

(3.2)

Let
\mathcal{C}_{2}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
be a compact subset.
The above equation and Lemma
2.12
imply that

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(x_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{2}}|F(x_{1},w_{2})|<\infty.

(3.3)

Since
(A_{2})_{\#}F(x_{1},x_{2})
is by definition a linear combination with bounded coefficients of the
F(x_{1},w_{2})
with
x_{2}\sim w_{2}\in W
, this implies that

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{2}}|(A_{2})_{\#}F(x_{1},x_{2})|<\infty.

(3.4)

We are ready to apply Proposition
2.23
.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{3}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}_{2}}
be a compact subset.
Let
d=\dist(\mathcal{C}_{3},\mathcal{C}_{2}^{c})
.
For
x_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
, by the first point of Proposition
2.23
applied in
B_{\#}(w_{2},r)
with
r=R=d/2
,

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(x_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{3}}\frac{|F(x_{1},w_{2})-\mathrm{Proj}[2[(A_{2})_{\#}F](x_{1},x_{2}^{+});\overline{x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}}]|}{{\varepsilon(1+|\log(\varepsilon)|)}}<\infty.

(3.5)

This equation will only be useful at the very end of the proof.
The second point of Proposition
2.23
implies that

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(x_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{3},x_{2},x_{2}^{\prime}\in B(w_{2},d/2)}\frac{|[(A_{2})_{\#}F](x_{1},x_{2})-[(A_{2})_{\#}F](x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime})|}{|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|(1+|\log(|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|)|)}<\infty.

This implies that

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{3}}\frac{|[(A_{2})_{\#}F](x_{1},x_{2})-[(A_{2})_{\#}F](x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime})|}{|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|(1+|\log(|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|)|)}<\infty.

(3.6)

Indeed, if
|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|\leq d/4
, we can apply the bound above with any
w_{2}\sim x_{2}
, and if
|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|\geq d/4
we can simply use the bound Equation (
3.4
).
On the other hand,
(A_{2})_{\#}F(x_{1},x_{2})
is a discrete massive harmonic function of the
first
variable
x_{1}
(or rather, its real and imaginary parts are real bounded discrete massive harmonic, as a linear combination of such functions). It is furthermore uniformly bounded for
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
by Equation (
3.4
), hence Lemma
2.12
implies that

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{3}}\frac{|(A_{2})_{\#}F(x_{1},x_{2})-(A_{2})_{\#}F(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})|}{|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|}<\infty.

(3.7)

We are ready to conclude the first step.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{4}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}_{3}}
be a compact subset.
Combining Equations (
3.4
), (
3.6
) and (
3.7
) with Lemma
B.1
, we obtain

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{4}}\frac{|(A_{2})_{\#}F(x_{1},x_{2})-(A_{2})_{\#}F(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})|}{|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|(1+|\log(|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|)|)+|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|}<\infty.

Hence the sequence
(A_{2})_{\#}F
is equi-continuous on
\mathcal{C}_{4}
.
Note that since we made no assumption on the sequence
\mathcal{C}_{i}
except that
\mathcal{C}_{1}\subset\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
,
\mathcal{C}_{i+1}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}_{i-1}}
, we proved that this holds for any compact subset
\mathcal{C}_{4}\subset\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
.
By the Arzela–Ascoli theorem, upon taking a subsequence,
(A_{2})_{\#}F\to D\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2}),\mathbb{C})
uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
.
If
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
, the modification is straightforward, replacing Equation (
3.2
) by its improvement from Lemma
A.1
.

Second step.

To conclude the proof, it remains to identify the limit
D
.
Let
x_{1}=x_{1}(\varepsilon)\to\chi_{1}\in\Omega
, and denote by
H(\cdot)=G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},\cdot)
and
F(\cdot)=F(x_{1},\cdot)
.
Let
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\Omega\setminus\{\chi_{1}\}
.
Let
\gamma
be a smooth path between
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
in
\Omega\setminus\{\chi_{1}\}
of length
|\gamma|<\infty
.
Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
be a compact subset large enough such that
(x_{1},\gamma(t))\in\mathcal{C}
for all
t
.
Let
x,x^{\prime}\in W^{\infty}
such that
x=x(\varepsilon)\to\chi,x^{\prime}=x^{\prime}(\varepsilon)\to\chi^{\prime}
.
Let
\gamma_{\#}=w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
be a path in
W^{\infty}
from
x
to
x^{\prime}
, that is if we denote by
x_{i}^{-}x_{i}^{+}
the edge of
\Gamma
corresponding to
w_{i}
,
x_{0}^{-}=x,x_{n}^{+}=x^{\prime}
and
x_{i}^{+}=x_{i+1}^{-}
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
.
We assume further that the length of
\gamma_{\#}
is
n\leq C|\gamma|
, and that
\gamma_{\#}
remains at distance
O(\varepsilon)
from
\gamma
.
(this is always possible under the bounded angle assumption).
Then, using the fact that
\zeta\mathrm{Proj}(z,\bar{\zeta})=\Re(z\zeta)=\Re(\bar{z}\bar{\zeta})=\langle\bar{z};\zeta\rangle
,

\displaystyle H(x^{\prime})-H(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}H(x_{i}^{+})-H(x_{i}^{-})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-})F(w_{i})

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:AF:first:point}}{=}2\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-})\left(\mathrm{Proj}[2[A_{\#}F](x_{i});\overline{x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-}}]+O\left(\varepsilon(1+|\log\varepsilon|)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}2\left\langle\overline{[A_{\#}F](x_{i})};x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-}\right\rangle+O\left(\varepsilon(1+|\log\varepsilon|)\right)

where the constant in the
O
depends only
\mathcal{C}
and
|\gamma|
.
Since
A_{\#}F\to D(\chi_{1},\cdot)
uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega\setminus\{\chi_{1}\}
and
H\to G^{m}(\chi_{1},\cdot)
pointwise by Lemma
2.24
, we obtain

G^{m}(\chi_{1},\chi^{\prime})-G^{m}(\chi_{1},\chi)=4\int_{\gamma}\langle\overline{D(\chi_{1},\omega)};\mathrm{d}\omega\rangle.

In the last integral,
\int_{\gamma}\langle\overline{D(\chi_{1},\omega)};\mathrm{d}\omega\rangle
stands for
\int_{0}^{1}\langle\overline{D(\chi_{1},\gamma(t))};\gamma^{\prime}(t)\rangle\mathrm{d}t
.

Since this holds for all
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\Omega\setminus\{\chi_{1}\}
and all paths between them, this identifies the projections of
D(\chi_{1},\chi)
in all directions, and we thus obtain that for
(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})\in\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
,

D(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})=\frac{1}{4}\overline{\nabla_{2}}G^{m}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2}),

which proves the first point of the lemma.
The second point follows by Equation (
3.5
).

We now move towards a proof of (
3.1
).
Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega
be a compact subset (
\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega_{\varphi}
if
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
), where as before we write
\mathring{\mathcal{C}}
for its interior.
The resolvent identity of Proposition
2.9
can be written as

\displaystyle G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2})+\sum_{x\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{C}}m(x)G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x)G_{\#}(x,x_{2})+\sum_{x\in\Gamma\setminus\mathcal{C}}m(x)G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x)G_{\#}(x,x_{2})

\displaystyle=G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2})+\sum_{x\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{C}}m(x)G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x)G_{\#}(x,x_{2})+H(x_{1},x_{2}),

where
H
is defined by the last equation.
Observe that
H(x_{1},x_{2})
is uniformly bounded for
x_{1},x_{2}
in any compact subset of
\mathring{\mathcal{C}}
by Lemma
A.1
, hence so is
(\partial_{2})_{\#}H
by Lemma
2.12
.
Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}}
be a compact subset.
Differentiating with respect to the second variable and using Lemmas
2.10
,
A.1
and Lemma
A.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(x_{1},w_{2})+\sum_{x\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{C}}m(x)G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x)(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(x,w_{2})+(\partial_{2})_{\#}H(x_{1},w_{2})

(3.8)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|}\right)+\sum_{x\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{C}}m(x)O\left(\frac{1+|\log|x_{1}-x||}{|x-w_{2}|}\right)+O(1)

where the
O
is uniform for
x_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\cap\Omega
.
Note that when
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
and when
x_{1},x,w_{2}\in\Gamma
,
|\varphi(x_{1})-x|=O(|x_{1}-x|)
and
|\varphi(x)-w_{2}|=O(|x-w_{2}|)
by Remark
2.5
so this equation also holds in this case.
Let
2d=|x_{1}-w_{2}|
.
We split the sum in two:

S_{1}=\sum_{|y-x_{1}|\leq d}m(y)\frac{1+|\log|x_{1}-y||}{|y-w_{2}|}\leq\sum_{|y-x_{1}|\leq d}m(y)\frac{1+|\log|x_{1}-y||}{d}\leq\frac{C_{1}\mathcal{M}}{d},

because
\log
is integrable in two dimensions. On the other hand,

\displaystyle S_{2}=\sum_{|y-x_{1}|\geq d}m(y)\frac{1+|\log|x_{1}-y||}{|y-w_{2}|}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{|y-x_{1}|\geq d}m(y)\frac{1+|\log(|y-w_{2}|/3)|}{|y-w_{2}|}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y\in\Omega\cap\mathcal{C}}m(y)\frac{1+|\log(|y-w_{2}|/3)|}{|y-w_{2}|}\leq C_{2}\mathcal{M}

because if
|y-x_{1}|\geq d,|y-w_{2}|\geq d
, then
|y-w_{2}|\leq|y-x_{1}|+2d\leq 3|y-x_{1}|
and because
z\to(\log|z|)/|z-w_{2}|
is integrable in two dimensions.
This implies that

(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|}\right),

uniformly for
x_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\cap\Omega
.

When
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
, since
(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})
is extended to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
by the discrete reflection principle, this gives

(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|\wedge|\varphi(x_{1})-w_{2}|}\right).

uniformly for
x_{1},w_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

3.2
Convergence of second order discrete derivative of massive Green function

We state a lemma to prove convergence of the second order discrete derivative of the discrete massive Green function towards its continuous analog. Before stating the lemma, we need to introduce some more matrix notation.
Recall that we identify a complex number
z=a+\mathbf{i}b
with the vector
(a,b)
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We will denote by
\cdot
the matrix product (it should be clear from context whether we are talking about multiplication of matrices or multiplication of complex numbers). In particular, the scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is

\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle=\Re(z_{1}\overline{z_{2}})=(z_{1})^{\intercal}\cdot z_{2}.

Recall also the (conjugate) discrete gradients from the previous section, namely
\overline{\nabla_{1}}G^{m}
, and
\overline{\nabla_{2}}G^{m}
, that is for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\overline{\nabla_{i}}G^{m}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{i,x}G^{m}\\
-\partial_{i,y}G^{m}\end{pmatrix}

where the variables of
G^{m}
are implicit in the notation, and where in
\partial_{i,z}
the number
i\in\{1,2\}
stands for the variable which is derivated and the letter
z\in\{x,y\}
stands for the direction of the partial derivative. We also define

(\nabla_{1})(\nabla_{2})^{\intercal}G^{m}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1,x}\partial_{2,x}G^{m}&\partial_{1,x}\partial_{2,y}G^{m}\\
\partial_{1,y}\partial_{2,x}G^{m}&\partial_{1,y}\partial_{2,y}G^{m},\end{pmatrix}\quad;\quad(\overline{\nabla_{1}})(\overline{\nabla_{2}})^{\intercal}G^{m}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1,x}\partial_{2,x}G^{m}&-\partial_{1,x}\partial_{2,y}G^{m}\\
-\partial_{1,y}\partial_{2,x}G^{m}&\partial_{1,y}\partial_{2,y}G^{m}\end{pmatrix},

and

(\nabla_{2})(\nabla_{1})^{\intercal}G^{m}=\left((\nabla_{1})(\nabla_{2})^{\intercal}G^{m})\right)^{\intercal}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1,x}\partial_{2,x}G^{m}&\partial_{1,y}\partial_{2,x}G^{m}\\
\partial_{1,x}\partial_{2,y}G^{m}&\partial_{1,y}\partial_{2,y}G^{m}\end{pmatrix}

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
w_{1}=w_{1}(\varepsilon),w_{2}=w_{2}(\varepsilon)\in W
corresponding respectively to the middle of the primal edges
x_{1}^{-}x_{1}^{+}
,
x_{2}^{-}x_{2}^{+}
. Uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
,

\displaystyle(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{+},x_{2}^{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}(\overline{\nabla_{1}})(\overline{\nabla_{2}})^{\intercal}G^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})+o_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)

\displaystyle\left((\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})^{\intercal}\cdot\nabla_{1}(\nabla_{2})^{\intercal}G^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})\cdot(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})

\displaystyle+o_{\varepsilon\to 0}(\varepsilon^{2}).

The first equation means that the gradients of the real and imaginary parts of
(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
converge. More precisely, if we write the complex-valued function
(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
as
U+iV
(where
U,V
are real-valued), then the complex-valued function
(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}U
converges to the first column of the matrix on the right hand side (after identifying complex numbers with vectors, as usual). Likewise,
(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}V
converges to the second column of that matrix.

Moreover,

(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}}\right)

uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}
.
When
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
\Omega\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)=L
,
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
can be replaced by
\Omega_{\varphi}^{2}\setminus\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\Omega_{\varphi}^{2}\colon x_{1}=x_{2}\text{ or }x_{1}=\varphi(x_{2})\}
in the first statement and
\Omega
can be replaced by
\Omega_{\varphi}
in the second statement if we replace
|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}
by
|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}\wedge|\varphi(w_{1})-w_{2}|^{2}
.

Proof.

We start with the first point of the lemma. The proof idea is roughly the same as for Lemma
3.1
. We first prove using Lemmas
2.12
and
2.23
that
(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
is a sequence of equi-continuous functions in both variables and then identify uniquely the limit by the same argument as before since
(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
converges by Lemma
3.1
.

However, some new technical difficulties arise along the way, we highlight some of them:

•

(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
is a complex valued function, whose real part and imaginary parts are real discrete massive harmonic function. Since we did not develop the holomorphicity theory for complex-valued functions, we must treat them separately.

•

We need to use some new symmetry argument to show that
(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
is an equi-continuous sequence of functions of the second variable, since it is no more discrete massive harmonic.

•

We know from the second point of Proposition
2.23
that
(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
is approximately the projection of
(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
on
x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}
, and that
(\partial_{1})_{\#}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
is approximately the projection of
(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
on
x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-}
. This does not imply that we can approximate
(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
by a projection of
(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
.

The main arguments to solve these problems are based on the following simple observations. Let
\mathfrak{P}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{2}
denote either the real part
\Re
or the imaginary part
\Im
.

\displaystyle(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=(\partial_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle(\partial_{1})_{\#}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{1}(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}_{1}(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}).

(3.10)

These observations can all be checked by direct computation: for example the last one is equal to

\sum_{w_{1}\sim x_{1},w_{2}\sim x_{2}}\mathfrak{P}_{1}\left(\frac{\mu_{W}(w_{1})}{x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}}\right)\mathfrak{P}_{2}\left(\frac{\mu_{W}(w_{2})}{x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}}\right)\frac{G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{+},x_{2}^{+})-G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{+})-G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{+},x_{2})+G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})}{16\mu_{B}(x_{1})\mu_{B}(x_{2})}.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{3}\subset\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
be a compact subset.
Recall from Equation (
3.4
) that

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{3}}\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})<\infty.

(3.11)

Let
\mathcal{C}_{4}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}_{3}}
be a compact subset.
By Lemma
2.12
,

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{4}}|(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})|<\infty.

(3.12)

Let
\mathcal{C}_{5}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}_{4}}
be a compact subset.
Applying the first point of Proposition
2.23
as in the proof of Lemma
3.1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{\underset{\varepsilon>0}{(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{5}}}

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathfrak{P}_{1}(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})-\mathfrak{P}_{1}(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})|}{|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|(1+|\log(|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|)|)}

(3.13)

\displaystyle<\infty.

Since
G_{\#}^{m}
is symmetric in the two variables, and
\mathfrak{P}_{1}(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}
commute by Equation (
3.10
) and
\mathfrak{P}_{1},\mathfrak{P}_{2}
were chosen arbitrarily, we can interchange the variables:

\displaystyle\sup_{\underset{\varepsilon>0}{(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{5}}}

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathfrak{P}_{1}(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})-\mathfrak{P}_{1}(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime})|}{|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|(1+|\log(|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|)|)}

(3.14)

\displaystyle<\infty.

Combining Equations (
3.12
), (
3.13
) and (
3.14
) with Lemma
B.1
, this implies that if
\mathcal{C}_{6}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}_{5}}
is a compact subset,

\displaystyle\sup_{\underset{\varepsilon>0}{(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{6}}}

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathfrak{P}_{1}(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})-\mathfrak{P}_{1}(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}_{2}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})|}{|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|(1+|\log(|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|)|)+|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|(1+|\log(|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|)|)}

\displaystyle<\infty.

Hence, for
\mathfrak{P}\in\{\Re,\Im\}
, the sequence
(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
is equi-continuous on
\mathcal{C}_{6}
, and this holds for any compact subset
\mathcal{C}_{6}\subset\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
.
By the Arzela–Ascoli theorem, upon taking a subsequence,
(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{P}}
uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
.
Moreover, since
\mathfrak{P}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
and
(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
are bounded on
\mathcal{C}_{4}
by Equations (
3.11
) and (
3.12
), by the first point of Proposition
2.23

\displaystyle\sup_{\underset{\varepsilon>0}{(w_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{5}}}

\displaystyle\frac{\left|(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})-\mathrm{Proj}\left[2(A_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{+},x_{2});\overline{x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-}}\right]\right|}{\varepsilon(1+|\log(\varepsilon)|)}

(3.15)

\displaystyle<\infty.

Since by Lemma
3.1
,

\mathfrak{P}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{P}\overline{\nabla_{2}}G^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})+o_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1),

the argument of the second step of the proof of Lemma
3.1
applies and we obtain that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{P}}=\frac{1}{16}\overline{\nabla_{1}}\mathfrak{P}\overline{\nabla_{2}}G^{m}
.
This concludes the proof of the first point.
When
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
, the modification is straightforward by replacing Equation (
3.11
) by its modification, see the proof of the first point of Lemma
3.1
.

To obtain the second point, we first note that by linearity of
(\partial_{1})_{\#}
, Equation (
3.9
), for all
w_{1},x_{2}
,

\displaystyle(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(\partial_{1})_{\#}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})

(3.16)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(\partial_{1})_{\#}\Re(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})\\
(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(\partial_{1})_{\#}\Im(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2}).\end{pmatrix}

We use the vector form rather than the complex form for the right-hand side to emphasize that both components are real.
The right-hand side can be estimated by Equation (
3.15
).
For the left-hand side, we use once more Proposition
2.23
.
As before, applying Lemma
2.12
consecutively to the discrete real massive harmonic functions
G_{\#}^{m}(\cdot,x_{2})
and
(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},\cdot)
, we obtain that for all compact subset
\mathcal{C}_{1}\subset\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
,

\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(w_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{C}}|(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})|<\infty\quad;\quad\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(w_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}}|(\partial_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})|<\infty.

By the first point of Proposition
2.23
applied to the
real-valued
discrete massive harmonic function
(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{+})(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},\cdot)
, if
\mathcal{C}_{2}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
is a compact subset,

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon>0,(w_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon(1+|\log(\varepsilon)|)}\Bigg|(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(\partial_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})

(3.17)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Proj}\left[2(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2}^{+});\overline{x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}}\right]\Bigg|<\infty.

To obtain the second point of the lemma from Equations (
3.15
), (
3.16
), (
3.17
) and the first point of the lemma is only a matter of basic algebra. We do it below for completeness.

Let
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{5}
.
From Equation (
3.15
), the first point of the lemma, and the fact that

z_{1}\mathrm{Proj}[z_{2},\overline{z_{1}}]=\Re(z_{1}z_{2})=z_{1}^{\intercal}\cdot\overline{z_{2}}=\overline{z_{2}}^{\intercal}\cdot z_{1}\in\mathbb{R},

(3.18)

we obtain that for
\mathfrak{P}\in\{\Re,\Im\}

(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(\partial_{1})_{\#}\mathfrak{P}(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{8}\nabla_{1}^{\intercal}\mathfrak{P}\overline{\nabla_{2}}G^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})\cdot(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})+o(\varepsilon),

uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{5}
.
Note that both sides of this equation are real.
Equation (
3.16
) then becomes

\displaystyle(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(A_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\overline{\nabla_{2}}\nabla_{1}^{\intercal}G^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})\cdot(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})+o(\varepsilon),

uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{5}
.
From Equations (
3.17
) and (
3.18
), we finally obtain that

\displaystyle(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(\partial_{2})_{\#}(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})^{\intercal}\overline{\overline{\nabla_{2}}\nabla_{1}^{\intercal}G^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})\cdot(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})}+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})^{\intercal}\nabla_{2}\nabla_{1}^{\intercal}G^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})\cdot(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})+o(\varepsilon^{2})

uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{5}
, which concludes the proof.

We now tackle the last point of the lemma. Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega
(
\Omega_{\varphi}
if
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
) be a compact subset. As in the proof of Lemma
3.1
, we separate the boundary terms in the resolvent identity and differentiate in each variable to obtain

\displaystyle(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(w_{1},w_{2})+\sum_{x\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{C}}m(x)(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x)(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(x,w_{2})

\displaystyle+(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}H(w_{1},w_{2}).

Recall that
H(w_{1},w_{2})
is discrete massive harmonic in the first variable, discrete (non-massive) harmonic in the second variable.
Moreover, it is bounded uniformly for
x_{1},x_{2}
in compact subsets of
\mathring{\mathcal{C}}
.
Hence its derivative in one and two variables are also bounded uniformly in any compact subset of
\mathring{\mathcal{C}}
by Lemma
2.12
.
Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}}
be a compact subset.
By Lemmas
3.1
,
A.4
and
A.5
(and by symmetry of the discrete Green function),

\displaystyle(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}}\right)+\sum_{y\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{C}}m(y)O\left(\frac{1}{|x-w_{1}||x-w_{2}|}\right)+O(1)

uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\cap\Omega
.
Recall that as in the preceding lemma,
|\varphi(w_{1})-w_{2}|=O(|w_{1}-w_{2}|)
for
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Omega
so this holds also when
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a compact subset of
\Omega\cup B(\beta^{*},\eta)
.
Again, we can denote
2d=|w_{1}-w_{2}|
and split the sum in three: for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

S_{i}=\sum_{|y-w_{i}|\leq d}\frac{m(y)}{|x-w_{1}||x-w_{2}|}\leq\frac{1}{d}\sum_{|y-w_{i}|\leq d}\frac{m(y)}{|x-w_{i}|}\leq\frac{C_{1}\mathcal{M}}{d}

because
z\to 1/|z|
is integrable in two dimensions, and

S_{3}=\sum_{|y-w_{1}|\geq d,|y-w_{2}|\geq d}\frac{m(y)}{|x-w_{1}||x-w_{2}|}\leq\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{\Gamma\setminus\mathcal{C}}m(y)\leq\frac{C_{3}\mathcal{M}}{d^{2}}.

This implies that

(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}}\right),

uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\cap\Omega
.
When
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
, since
G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2})
and its derivatives are extended to
\Gamma_{\varphi}
by the discrete Schwarz principle, this gives

(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}\wedge|\varphi(w_{1})-w_{2}|^{2}}\right).

This concludes the proof.
∎

3.3
Convergence of the inverse Kasteleyn matrix (proof of Theorem
1.13
)

This section is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
1.13
. Roughly speaking, Lemma
2.3
relates the inverse Kasteleyn matrix
K^{-1}(x,w)
to the derivatives of the massive Green function when
x\in\Gamma
is a (primal) black vertex. The tools developed in the preceding subsections therefore give us the asymptotics of
K^{-1}
in this case, which yields the first point of Theorem
1.13
in this case (i.e., the first asymptotics of (
1.25
)).

The asymptotics of
K^{-1}(y,w)
when
y
is instead a black (dual) vertex is however more complicated. Let us first recall an important idea concerning the classical (non-massive) case, which goes back in some form to the seminal paper of Kenyon
[
22
]
. In this classical case, the idea is that
K^{-1}
satisfies
KK^{-1}=I
, so is discrete holomorphic. This means that the derivatives of
K^{-1}(\cdot,w)
in the
y
direction are equal to
\mathbf{i}
times those in the
x
direction of a given rhombus. In combination with the asymptotic analysis of the (derivatives of)
K^{-1}
in the
x
direction this gives us access to the asymptotics of the derivatives of
K^{-1}
in the
y
direction. Summing these asymptotics along a path emanating from the boundary (more specifically from the “special” Temperleyan corner
b^{*}
) then gives an integral formula for the asymptotics of
K^{-1}(y,w)
.

In our massive case, it is of course still the case that
KK^{-1}=I
. However this now means that
K^{-1}
is discrete
massive
holomorphic, so the relation between the
x
- and
y
-derivatives of
K^{-1}(\cdot,w)
on a given rhombus is more complex. Ultimately, we need to find a suitable transformation of
K^{-1}
for which an exact discrete form of the massive Cauchy–Riemann equation (
1.17
) holds. The resulting key identities are stated in (
3.23
), (
3.27
) and
(
3.28
).

Proof.

Suppose that
w_{2}\in W
corresponds to the middle of the rhombus
x_{2}^{-}y_{2}^{-}x_{2}^{+}y_{2}^{+}
with angle
\theta_{2}
and with
K_{w_{2},x_{2}^{+}}>0
,
K_{w_{2},x_{2}^{-}}<0
