as in the statement of the theorem.
From Lemma
2.3
, since by definition
G_{\#}^{m}=(\Delta_{\#}^{d}-mI)^{-1}
, we can write

\displaystyle K^{-1}(x_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=(G_{\#}^{m}K^{*})(x_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{+})\overline{K(w_{2},x_{2}^{+})}+G^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{-})\overline{K(w_{2},x_{2}^{-})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{2})}\left[\exp\left(\frac{V(x_{2}^{-})-V(x_{2}^{+})}{2}\right)G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{+})-\exp\left(\frac{V(x_{2}^{+})-V(x_{2}^{-})}{2}\right)G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{-})\right]

by definition of
K
. For any
w\in W
corresponding to the middle of the edge
x^{-}x^{+}
of
\Gamma
, we can perform an asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{V(x^{+})-V(x^{-})}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\nabla V(w),x^{+}-x^{-}\right\rangle+O(\varepsilon^{3})\right)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\nabla V(w),x^{+}-x^{-}\right\rangle+R(w,\varepsilon)+O(\varepsilon^{3})

uniformly for
w\in W
, with
R(w,\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{8}\left\langle\nabla V(w),x^{+}-x^{-}\right\rangle^{2}
depends only on
w\in W
(and not on the choice of
x^{-}
and
x^{+}
) and
R(w,\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon^{2})
uniformly for
w\in W
. Hence we can write, uniformly for
(x_{1},w_{2})
in compact subsets of
(\Omega\cup L)^{2}
,

\displaystyle K^{-1}(x_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{2})}\left[G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{+})\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla V(w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+R(w_{2},\varepsilon)+O(\varepsilon^{3})\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{-})\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla V(w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+R(w_{2},\varepsilon)+O(\varepsilon^{3})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{2})}\bigg[2(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})\left(1+R(w_{2},\varepsilon)+O(\varepsilon^{3})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-(G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{+})+G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{-}))\left(\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla V(w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+O(\varepsilon^{3})\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{2})}\left[2(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla V(w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+R(w_{2},\varepsilon)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\quad-G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{-})\langle\nabla V(w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle\bigg]+O(\varepsilon^{3}\log\varepsilon).

(3.19)

where at the last line we use Lemma
A.1
to bound
G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})=O(1+|\log|x_{1}-w_{2}||)=O(\log\varepsilon))
. We are ready to prove the first point of the theorem.
The discrete massive Green function and its discrete derivative
(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})
are uniformly bounded for
x_{1},w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega\cup L)^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}((\Omega\cup L)^{2})
by Lemmas
A.1
and
A.4
.
Hence, in Equation (
3.19
) if we consider only terms of order up to
O(\varepsilon^{2})
, we obtain

K^{-1}(x_{1},w_{2})=\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{2})}\left[2(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})-G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1},x_{2}^{-})\langle\nabla V(w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle)\right]+O(\varepsilon^{2})

(3.20)

Using Lemmas
2.24
and
3.1
, we obtain

K^{-1}(x_{1},w_{2})=\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{2})}\left\langle\nabla_{2}G^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})-G^{m}(x_{1},w_{2})\nabla V(w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\right\rangle+o(\varepsilon),

uniformly for
(x_{1},w_{2})
in compact subsets of
(\Omega\cup L)^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}((\Omega\cup L)^{2})
. Moreover, since
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary, we can plug the bounds of Lemmas
2.24
and
3.1
in Equation (
3.19
) to obtain that uniformly for
(x_{1},w_{2})
in compact subsets of
(\Omega\cup L)^{2}
,

K^{-1}(x_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|}\right).

(3.21)

We prove the second point of the theorem. For
w_{1}\neq w_{2}
corresponding to the middle of the rhombus
x_{1}^{-}y_{1}^{-}x_{1}^{+}y_{1}^{+}
with angle
\theta_{1}
and with
K_{w_{1},x_{1}^{+}}=1
, since by definition
KK^{-1}=I
, the
discrete massive Cauchy–Riemann equation
at
w_{1}
for the function
w_{1}\to K^{-1}_{w_{1},w_{2}}
(which is, by definition,
discrete massive meromorphic
in the sense of Remark
2.18
) is

0=(KK^{-1})_{w_{1},w_{2}}=K_{w_{1},x_{1}^{-}}K^{-1}_{x_{1}^{-},w_{2}}+K_{w_{1},x_{1}^{+}}K^{-1}_{x_{1}^{+},w_{2}}+K_{w_{1},y_{1}^{-}}K^{-1}_{y_{1}^{-},w_{2}}+K_{w_{1},y_{1}^{+}}K^{-1}_{y_{1}^{+},w_{2}}.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that, by definition of the Temperleyan corner
b^{*}
, this equation also holds for
w_{1}
corresponding to the middle of an edge
y_{1}^{-}b^{*}
with
y_{1}^{-}\in\Gamma^{\star}
if we let
K^{-1}_{b^{*},w_{2}}=0
. Intuitively, Temperleyan boundary condition imposes Dirichlet boundary on
\Gamma
, and Neumann boundary conditions on
\Gamma^{\star}
except at
b^{*}
where the boundary condition is also Dirichlet.

The natural strategy would be to use a compactness argument to obtain convergence of
K^{-1}(\cdot,w_{2})
and its derivatives on
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\star}
, and to take the limit in the discrete Cauchy–Riemann equation which would identify uniquely the limit on
\Gamma^{\star}
as the unique massive holomorphic conjugate of the limit on
\Gamma
(which we already identified in the first point of the theorem). However,
K^{-1}(\cdot,w_{2})
is not discrete massive holomorphic on
\Gamma^{\star}
: actually, we do not even have a definition of discrete massive holomorphicity on
\Gamma^{\star}
, so we need to use a different argument.

For
w_{1},w_{2}
in
W
, let

\displaystyle(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{1})}\left(\exp\left(\frac{V(x_{1}^{-})-V(x_{1}^{+})}{2}\right)K^{-1}_{x_{1}^{+},w_{2}}-\exp\left(\frac{V(x_{1}^{+})-V(x_{1}^{-})}{2}\right)K^{-1}_{x_{1}^{-},w_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle[\mathrm{d}_{y}K^{-1}](w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{1})}}\bigg[\exp\left(V(y_{1}^{+})-\frac{V(x_{1}^{+})+V(x_{1}^{-})}{2}\right)K^{-1}_{y_{1}^{+},w_{2}}

\displaystyle-\exp\left(V(y_{1}^{-})-\frac{V(x_{1}^{+})+V(x_{1}^{-})}{2}\right)K^{-1}_{y_{1}^{-},w_{2}}\bigg].

Then, the discrete massive Cauchy–Riemann equation (
3.21
) can be rewritten as

-[\mathrm{d}_{x}K^{-1}](w_{1},w_{2})=\mathbf{i}[\mathrm{d}_{y}K^{-1}](w_{1},w_{2}).

(3.22)

We first study the left-hand side.

\displaystyle\frac{[\mathrm{d}_{x}K^{-1}](w_{1},w_{2})}{\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{1})}}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla V(w_{1}),x_{1}^{-}-x_{1}^{+}\rangle+R(w_{1},\varepsilon)+O(\varepsilon^{3})\right)K^{-1}_{x_{1}^{+},w_{2}}

(3.23)

\displaystyle-\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla V(w_{1}),x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-}\rangle+R(w_{1},\varepsilon)+O(\varepsilon^{3})\right)K^{-1}_{x_{1}^{-},w_{2}}

Note that by Equation (
3.20
) and Lemmas
2.24
,
3.1
and
3.2
, uniformly for
w_{1}\neq w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega\cup L
,

|K^{-1}_{x_{1}^{\pm},w_{2}}|=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|w_{1}-w_{2}|}\right)\quad;\quad|K^{-1}_{x_{1}^{+},w_{2}}-K^{-1}_{x_{1}^{-},w_{2}}|=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}}\right).

(3.24)

Hence, uniformly for
w_{1}\neq w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega\cup L
,

[\mathrm{d}_{x}K^{-1}](w_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}}\right).

(3.25)

For points which are far away from each other, we can be more precise. Multiplying by
\tan(\theta_{2})^{-1/2}
and plugging in the asymptotic expansion of Equation (
3.19
) in Equation (
3.23
) (and using that
(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
and
(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}
are bounded by Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
to approximate
G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{+},x_{2}^{-})=G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{+},x_{2}^{-})+O(\varepsilon)
and
(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{+},w_{2})=(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{-},w_{2})+O(\varepsilon)
), we obtain that uniformly for
(w_{1},w_{2})
in compact subsets of
(\Omega\cup L)^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}((\Omega\cup L)^{2})
,

\displaystyle\frac{[\mathrm{d}_{x}K^{-1}](w_{1},w_{2})}{\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{1})\tan(\theta_{2})}}

\displaystyle=4(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},w_{2})(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})

\displaystyle-2(\partial_{1})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(w_{1},x_{2}^{-})(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})\langle\nabla V(w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle

\displaystyle-2\langle\nabla V(w_{1}),x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-}\rangle(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{-},w_{2})(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})

\displaystyle+\langle\nabla V(w_{1}),x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-}\rangle G_{\#}^{m}(x_{1}^{-},x_{2}^{-})\langle\nabla V(w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

Using Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
, and the fact that for two vectors
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
,
\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle=(z_{1})^{\intercal}\cdot z_{2}=(z_{2})^{\intercal}\cdot z_{1}
, we obtain that uniformly for
(w_{1},w_{2})
in compact subsets of
(\Omega\cup L)^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}((\Omega\cup L)^{2})
,

\displaystyle\frac{[\mathrm{d}_{x}K^{-1}](w_{1},w_{2})}{\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{1})\tan(\theta_{2})}}

\displaystyle=(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})^{\intercal}\cdot(\nabla_{1})(\nabla_{2})^{\intercal}G^{m}\cdot(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})-\langle\nabla_{1}G^{m},x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-}\rangle\langle\nabla V(\omega_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle

(3.26)

\displaystyle+\langle\nabla V(w_{1}),x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-}\rangle\left[-\langle\nabla_{2}G^{m},x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+G^{m}\langle\nabla V(w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle\right]+o(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=(x_{1}^{+}-x_{1}^{-})^{\intercal}\cdot\left[((\nabla_{1})(\nabla_{2})^{\intercal}G^{m}-\nabla_{1}G^{m}\cdot(\nabla V(w_{2}))^{\intercal}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad-\nabla V(w_{1})\cdot(\nabla_{2}G^{m})^{\intercal}+G^{m}\nabla V(w_{1})\cdot(\nabla V(w_{2}))^{\intercal}\right]\cdot(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})+o(\varepsilon^{2})

where some arguments are omitted in the notation when there is no risk of confusion.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
be a compact subset. We can find

\mathcal{C}_{1}\subset(\Omega\cup L)^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}((\Omega\cup L)^{2})
and
C>0
a constant such that for all
(y_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists a continuous smooth path
\gamma
between
y_{0}:=b^{*}
and
y_{1}
of bounded length
|\gamma|<C
such that
(\gamma(t),w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
for all
t
.
Let
\gamma_{\#}=\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{w}_{1},\dots,\tilde{w}_{n}
be a path in
W
from
y_{0}
to
y_{1}
, that is if we denote by
\tilde{x}_{k}^{-}\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}\tilde{x}_{k}^{+}\tilde{y}_{k}^{+}
the rhombus corresponding to
\tilde{w}_{k}
,
\tilde{y}_{0}^{-}=y_{0},\tilde{y}_{n}^{+}=y_{1}
and for all
0\leq k\leq n-1
,
\tilde{y}_{k}^{+}=\tilde{y}_{k+1}^{-}
.
By the bounded angle assumption, we can assume further that the length of
\gamma_{\#}
is
n\leq C|\gamma|\varepsilon^{-1}
and that
\gamma_{\#}
remains at distance
\leq C\varepsilon
from
\gamma
upon increasing
C
.
If we sum the
[\mathrm{d}_{y}K^{-1}](\tilde{w}_{k},w_{2})
along
\gamma_{\#}
, consecutive terms do not cancel out, so we need to modify them slightly. We can write

\displaystyle(\tilde{w}_{k},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp\left(V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-})-\frac{V(\tilde{x}_{k}^{+})+V(\tilde{x}_{k}^{-})}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\tan(\tilde{\theta}_{k})}}\left(\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k+1}^{-})-V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}))K_{\tilde{y}_{k+1}^{-},w_{2}}-K_{\tilde{y}_{k}^{-},w_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=q_{k}\left(\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k+1}^{-})-V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}))K_{\tilde{y}_{k+1}^{-},w_{2}}-K_{\tilde{y}_{k}^{-},w_{2}}\right),

where
q_{k}
is defined by the last equation. Note that

\frac{\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}))}{q_{k}}[\mathrm{d}_{y}K^{-1}](\tilde{w}_{k},w_{2})=\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k+1}^{-}))K_{\tilde{y}_{k+1}^{-},w_{2}}-\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}))K_{\tilde{y}_{k}^{-},w_{2}},

(3.27)

so summing along
\gamma_{\#}
, the intermediate terms cancel out and since
\exp(V(y_{0}))K^{-1}_{y_{0},w_{2}}=0
by Remark
3.3
, we obtain

\exp(V(y_{1}))K^{-1}_{y_{1},w_{2}}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}))}{q_{k}}[\mathrm{d}_{y}K^{-1}](\tilde{w}_{k},w_{2}).

(3.28)

Note that

\displaystyle q_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-})-V(\tilde{w}_{k})+O(\varepsilon^{2}))}{\sqrt{\tan(\tilde{\theta}_{k})}}=\frac{1-\frac{1}{2}\nabla V(\tilde{w}_{k})\cdot(\tilde{y}_{k}^{+}-\tilde{y}_{k}^{-})}{\sqrt{\tan(\tilde{\theta}_{k})}}+O(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1+O(\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{\tan(\tilde{\theta}_{k})}},

where the
O
is uniform over
\Omega
. Hence, using the discrete massive Cauchy–Riemann Equation (
3.22
) as well as the
a priori
bounds on
K^{-1}
from (
3.24
), and plugging in the asymptotic expansion of Equation (
3.26
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\exp(V(y_{1})K^{-1}_{y_{1},w_{2}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}))\frac{\sqrt{\tan(\tilde{\theta}_{k})}}{1+O(\varepsilon)}[\mathrm{d}_{y}K^{-1}](\tilde{w}_{k},w_{2})

(3.29)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{i}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}))(1+O(\varepsilon)){\sqrt{\tan(\tilde{\theta}_{k})}}[\mathrm{d}_{x}K^{-1}](\tilde{w}_{k},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{i}\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{2})}(1+O(\varepsilon))\Big\{\sum_{k=0}^{n}\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}))\tan(\tilde{\theta}_{k})(\tilde{x}_{k}^{+}-\tilde{x}_{k}^{-})^{\intercal}\cdot\bigg[(\nabla_{1})(\nabla_{2})^{\intercal}G^{m}

\displaystyle-\nabla_{1}G^{m}\cdot(\nabla V(w_{2}))^{\intercal}-\nabla V(\tilde{w}_{k})\cdot(\nabla_{2}G^{m})^{\intercal}+G^{m}\nabla V(\tilde{w}_{k})\cdot(\nabla V(w_{2}))^{\intercal}\bigg]

\displaystyle\cdot(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})+o(\varepsilon^{2})\Big\}

where the
o
is uniform for
(y_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}
. Finally, observe that for all rhombus
x^{-}y^{-}x^{+}y^{+}
with angle
\theta
, it holds that

y^{+}-y^{-}=\tan(\theta)\mathrm{Rot}_{\pi/2}\cdot(x^{+}-x^{-}).

where we identify a complex number with the associated vector in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and we let
\mathrm{Rot}_{\alpha}
denote the only matrix such that
\mathrm{Rot}_{\alpha}z
is the rotation of
z
by an angle
\alpha\in[0,2\pi)
for all vector
z
. Note that
\mathrm{Rot}_{\alpha}^{\intercal}=\mathrm{Rot}_{-\alpha}
. Hence, bearing in mind that the number of terms
n
in this sum is at most
n=|\gamma_{\#}|\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}
, and using the fact that for vectors
x,y
and a matrix
M
we have the identity
y^{\intercal}\cdot M\cdot x=\langle x,M^{\intercal}\cdot y\rangle
, we obtain

\begin{aligned} \frac{\exp(V(\upsilon_{1}))K^{-1}_{y_{1},w_{2}}}{\sqrt{\tan(\theta_{2})}(1+O(\varepsilon))}&=\mathbf{i}\Big\{\sum_{k=0}^{n}\exp(V(\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}))(\mathrm{Rot}_{-\pi/2}(\tilde{y}_{k}^{+}-\tilde{y}_{k}^{-}))^{\intercal}\cdot\left[(\nabla_{1})(\nabla_{2})^{\intercal}G^{m}-\nabla_{1}G^{m}\cdot(\nabla V(w_{2}))^{\intercal}\right.\\
&\quad\left.-\nabla V(\tilde{w}_{k})\cdot(\nabla_{2}G^{m})^{\intercal}+G^{m}\nabla V(\tilde{w}_{k})\cdot(\nabla V(w_{2}))^{\intercal}\right]\cdot(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})\Big\}+o(\varepsilon^{2})n\\
&=\mathbf{i}\int_{\gamma}\exp(V(\upsilon))\mathrm{d}\gamma(\upsilon)^{\intercal}\cdot\mathrm{Rot}_{\pi/2}\cdot\left[(\nabla_{1})(\nabla_{2})^{\intercal}G^{m}-\nabla_{1}G^{m}\cdot(\nabla V(\omega_{2}))^{\intercal}\right.\\
&\quad\left.-\nabla V(\upsilon)\cdot(\nabla_{2}G^{m})^{\intercal}+G^{m}\nabla V(\upsilon)\cdot(\nabla V(\omega_{2})))^{\intercal}\right]\cdot(x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-})+o(\varepsilon)\\
&=\mathbf{i}\Bigg\langle x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-},\int_{\gamma}\exp(V(\upsilon))\left[(\nabla_{2})(\nabla_{1})^{\intercal}G^{m}-\nabla V(\omega_{2})\cdot(\nabla_{1}G^{m})^{\intercal}\right.\\
&\quad\left.-\nabla_{2}G^{m}\cdot(\nabla V(\upsilon))^{\intercal}+G^{m}\nabla V(\omega_{2}))\cdot(\nabla V(\upsilon))^{\intercal}\right]\cdot\mathrm{Rot}_{-\pi/2}\cdot\mathrm{d}\gamma(\upsilon)\Bigg\rangle+o(\varepsilon)\end{aligned}.

uniformly for
(y_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}
. Hence, we have proved the second point of Equation (
1.25
), with the following definition of
\overline{\kappa^{*}}
: for all smooth path
\gamma
from
\beta^{*}
to
\omega_{1}
avoiding
\omega_{2}
,

\displaystyle\overline{\kappa^{*}}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=\exp(-V(\omega_{1}))\int_{\gamma}\exp(V(\omega))\left[(\nabla_{2})(\nabla_{1})^{\intercal}G^{m}(\omega,\omega_{2})-\nabla V(\omega_{2})\cdot(\nabla_{1}G^{m}(\omega,\omega_{2}))^{\intercal}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\nabla_{2}G^{m}(\omega,\omega_{2})\cdot(\nabla V(\omega))^{\intercal}+G^{m}\nabla V(\omega_{2}))\cdot(\nabla V(\omega))^{\intercal}\right]\cdot\mathrm{Rot}_{-\pi/2}\cdot\mathrm{d}\omega

The operators
\overline{\kappa}
and
\overline{\kappa^{*}}
are massive harmonic conjugates in the sense of Definition
1.10
, since

\displaystyle e^{V(\omega_{1})}(\nabla_{1})^{\intercal}(\overline{\kappa}e^{-V(\omega_{1})})

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{1})^{\intercal}\overline{\kappa}-\overline{\kappa}\cdot(\nabla)^{\intercal}V(\omega_{1})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{2}(\nabla_{1})^{\intercal}G^{m}-\nabla V(\omega_{2})\cdot(\nabla_{1})^{\intercal}G^{m}

\displaystyle-\nabla_{2}G^{m}\cdot(\nabla)^{\intercal}V(\omega_{1})+G^{m}\nabla V(\omega_{2})\cdot(\nabla V)^{\intercal}(\omega_{1})

\displaystyle=e^{-V(\omega)}(\nabla_{1})^{\intercal}(e^{V(\omega_{1})}\overline{\kappa^{*}})\cdot\mathrm{Rot}_{\pi/2}.

Hence, the operators
\kappa
and
\kappa^{*}
are also massive harmonic conjugates since their real parts, resp. imaginary parts, are massive harmonic conjugates .

It remains to prove the bound for nearby points. Let
\mathcal{C}\subset(\Omega\cup L)\times(\Omega\cup L\setminus\{\beta^{*}\})
be a compact subset. We can find

\mathcal{C}_{1}
a compact subset of
(\Omega\cup L)\times(\Omega\cup L\setminus\{\beta^{*}\})
such that for some
C>0
and all
(y_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}
, there is a continuous smooth path
\gamma
between
y_{0}:=b^{*}
and
y_{1}
such that
(\gamma(t),w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
for all
t
,
|\gamma|\leq C
and
\gamma
remains at distance at least
|y_{1}-w_{2}|/C
from
w_{2}
. Moreover, we can assume that for some
\nu>0
if
w_{2}
is within
\nu
of
y_{1}
, then the portion of the path starting from
y_{1}
is locally a straight line escaping
B(w_{2},2\nu)
.

Let
(y_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{C}
.
As before, by the bounded angle assumption we can find
\gamma_{\#}=(\tilde{w}_{0},\dots,\tilde{w}_{n})
of length
n\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}
remaining at distance at most
C\varepsilon
from
\gamma
. Using the same argument as before and the estimate Equation (
3.25
), Equation (
3.29
) implies that

K^{-1}_{y_{1},w_{2}}=O\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\tilde{w}_{k}-w_{2}|^{2}}\right)

Using that
\gamma
is a straight line near
y_{1}
and the fact that

\int_{r}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s^{2}}=O\left(\frac{1}{r}\right),

we obtain that

K^{-1}(y_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|b^{*}-w_{2}|\wedge|y_{1}-w_{2}|}\right),

uniformly for
(y_{1},w_{2})
in compact subsets of
(\Omega\cup L)^{2}
.
∎

3.4
Convergence of height moments (proof of Theorem
1.14
)

In this section, we briefly summarize and adapt the arguments of
[
26
]
to explain how Theorem
1.13
implies Theorem
1.14
.

Remark 3.4
.

The interested reader who will compare this with Li’s work might be interested by the following two remarks on notation:

•

Li calls “dual graph” and denotes by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
what we denote by
\Gamma
and call “primal graph”. Even if they bear different names, these two objects are defined exactly the same way in her work and ours.

•

for
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, Li calls “directional derivative” of
f
at
z
in the direction
\zeta
the complex number
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{f(z+\varepsilon\zeta)-f(z)}{\varepsilon\zeta}
. We will avoid this notation.

We define a massive Dirac operator, corresponding to the
\overline{\partial}
operator of
[
24
]
,
[
26
]
in the critical case.
For
w\in W
, if
x^{+}
denotes the neighbor of
w
in
\Gamma
with
K(w,x^{+})>0
, let
\psi(w)=2\sqrt{\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)}\frac{x^{+}-w}{|x^{+}-w|}
.
The operator
\overline{\partial}
is defined as the gauge change of
K
via
\psi
:

\overline{\partial}(w,b)=\psi(w)K(w,b),w\in W,b\in\Gamma\cup\Gamma^{\star}.

(3.30)

Let
f,f^{\prime}
be two faces of the superposition graph, separated by an edge
e=wb
of the superposition graph. Let
\xi(wb)=0
if
w
is on the left of
ff^{\prime}
,
\xi(wb)=1
otherwise. The height difference between
f
and
f^{\prime}
is

h_{\varepsilon}(f^{\prime})-h_{\varepsilon}(f)=(-1)^{\xi(wb)}(1_{e}-\mathbb{P}(e))

(3.31)

where
1_{e}
denotes the indicator that
e
belongs to the dimer configuration,
\mathbb{P}(e)=\mathbb{E}(1_{e})
.

With these notations, we can reformulate Kasteleyn theory for the edge probabilities (see
[
26
, Lemma 11]
) as follows: for edges
e_{1}=(w_{1}b_{1}),\dots,e_{k}=(w_{k}b_{k})
,

\mathbb{E}[(1_{e_{1}}-\mathbb{P}(e_{1}))\dots(1_{e_{k}}-\mathbb{P}(e_{k}))]=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\overline{\partial}(w_{i},b_{i})\det\big(\overline{\partial}^{-1}(w_{i},b_{j})1_{i\neq j}\big).

(3.32)

This holds as well in the massive case, since the proof uses only the local statistics formula and determinantal identities.

Let
k\geq 2
,
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k}\in L\setminus\{\beta^{*}\}
be points on the boundary of
\partial\Omega
.
Let
\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{k}
be faces of the superposition graph. Assume first that these faces
\zeta_{i}
lie in a given compact subset of
\Omega^{k}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{k})
.
Let
\gamma_{i}
be pairwise disjoint paths of faces of the superposition graph, starting from the nearest face to
\beta_{i}
below the straight boundary and ending at
\zeta_{i}
.
For
z_{i}
a face of the superposition graph along the path
\gamma_{i}
, we let
\Delta z_{i}
denote the increment between
z_{i}
and the next face.
Let
E_{i}
be the set of edges
w_{i}b_{i}
intersecting
\gamma_{i}
.

Li notes that if we fix
h_{\varepsilon}(\beta_{1})=0
, then
h_{\varepsilon}(\beta_{i})=0
for all
i
. This also holds here.
Writing
h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})=\sum_{z_{i}\in\gamma_{i}}(h_{\varepsilon}(z_{i}+\Delta z_{i})-h_{\varepsilon}(z_{i}))
, the joint moments of the height can be computed using Equations (
3.31
) and (
3.32
).
Expanding the determinant, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]=\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{w_{i}b_{i}\in E_{i}}(-1)^{\xi(w_{1}b_{1})+\dots+\xi(w_{k}b_{k})}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\overline{\partial}(w_{i},b_{i})\overline{\partial}^{-1}(b_{\sigma(i)},w_{i}).

(3.33)

It is a sum over all permutations with no fixed point, and all
k
-tuples of edges
(w_{1}b_{1},w_{k}b_{k})
with
w_{i}b_{i}\in E_{i}
.
Note that since
\overline{\partial}
is obtained from
K
by Equation (
3.30
):

\overline{\partial}^{-1}(b,w)=\psi^{-1}(w)K^{-1}(b,w).

Hence, Theorem
1.13
implies that uniformly for
(x_{1},w_{2})
, resp.
(y_{1},w_{2})
, in compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
,

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}^{-1}(x_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}}\langle\overline{\kappa}(x_{1},w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+o(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}^{-1}(y_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{i\varepsilon}{x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}}\langle\overline{\kappa^{*}}(y_{1},w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle+o(\varepsilon).

We will write

\overline{\partial}^{-1}(b,w)=\varepsilon i^{\eta(b)}f_{\eta(b)}(b,w)+o(\varepsilon)

uniformly for
(w,b)
in compact subsets of
\Omega^{2}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{2})
, with
\eta(b)=1_{b\in\Gamma^{\star}}
and

f_{0}(x_{1},w_{2})=\frac{\langle\overline{\kappa}(x_{1},w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle}{x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}}\quad;\quad f_{1}(y_{1},w_{2})=\frac{\langle\overline{\kappa^{*}}(x_{1},w_{2}),x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}\rangle}{x_{2}^{+}-x_{2}^{-}}

This extends Sections 3 and 4 of
[
26
]
, where she obtains an asymptotic expression for
\overline{\partial}^{-1}
.
Hence, when the path are disjoint, plugging this asymptotic in Equation (
3.33
), we can write

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]=\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{w_{i}b_{i}\in E_{i}}(-1)^{\xi(w_{1}b_{1})+\dots+\xi(w_{k}b_{k})}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varepsilon\overline{\partial}(w_{i},b_{i})i^{\eta(b_{\sigma(i)})}f_{\eta(b_{\sigma(i)})}(b_{\sigma(i)},w_{i})+o(\varepsilon^{k}).

Now, Li observes that if
\Delta z_{i}
is the increment of
\gamma_{i}
along the edge dual to
w_{i}b_{i}
, then

\mathbf{i}(-1)^{\xi(w_{i}b_{i})}\varepsilon\overline{\partial}(w_{i},b_{i})=2\Delta z_{i}+O(\varepsilon^{2}).

We find a factor
2
missing from her computations here, which cancels out later.
In the non-massive case, this is an equality, see her Lemma 12: there is no
O(\varepsilon^{2})
.
In the massive case, this holds up to
O(\varepsilon^{2})
(uniformly for
w,b\in\Omega
) since the massive Kasteleyn operator
K(w,b)
is equal to its critical value, up to
1+O(\varepsilon)
.
Hence

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]=\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{w_{i}b_{i}\in E_{i}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{k}2\Delta z_{i}(-i)\mathbf{i}^{\eta(b_{\sigma(i)})}f_{\eta(b_{\sigma(i)})}(b_{\sigma(i)},w_{i})+o(\varepsilon^{k}).

(3.34)

Now, Li observes (see her Lemma 13) that

\overline{x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-}}\Delta z_{i}=(x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-})(-1)^{\eta(b_{i})+1}\overline{\Delta z_{i}}.

This is true because if
\eta(b_{i})=0
, i.e.
b_{i}\in\Gamma
, then
x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-}
and
\Delta z_{i}
are orthogonal while if
\eta(b_{i})=0
, i.e.
b_{i}\in\Gamma^{\star}
, then
x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-}
and
\Delta z_{i}
are parallel.
Hence, if we let
\overline{\kappa^{(0)}}=\overline{\kappa}
,
\overline{\kappa^{(1)}}=\overline{\kappa^{*}}
we obtain

\displaystyle(-\mathbf{i})\mathbf{i}^{\eta(b_{j})}2\Delta z_{i}f_{\eta(b_{j})}(b_{j},w_{i})

\displaystyle=(-\mathbf{i})\mathbf{i}^{\eta(b_{j})}2\Delta z_{i}\frac{\langle\overline{\kappa^{(\eta(b_{j}))}}(b_{j},w_{i}),x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-}\rangle}{x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-}}

(3.35)

\displaystyle=(-\mathbf{i})\mathbf{i}^{\eta(b_{j})}\Delta z_{i}\frac{\kappa^{(\eta(b_{j}))}(b_{j},w_{i})(x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-})+\overline{\kappa^{(\eta(b_{j}))}}(b_{j},w_{i})\overline{(x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-})}}{x_{i}^{+}-x_{i}^{-}}

\displaystyle=(-\mathbf{i})\mathbf{i}^{\eta(b_{j})}\left(\kappa^{(\eta(b_{j})}(b_{j},w_{i})\Delta z_{i}+(-1)^{\eta(b_{i})+1}\overline{\kappa^{(\eta(b_{j})}}(b_{j},w_{i})\overline{\Delta z_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\big(F_{0}(b_{j},w_{i})+(-1)^{\eta(b_{j})}F_{1}(b_{j},w_{i})\big)\Delta z_{i}+

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\eta(b_{j})+\eta(b_{i})}\big(\overline{F_{0}(b_{j},w_{i})}+(-1)^{\eta(b_{j})}\overline{F_{1}(b_{j},w_{j})}\big)\overline{\Delta z_{i}},

with

F_{0}=\frac{\kappa^{*}-\mathbf{i}\kappa}{2}\quad;\quad F_{1}=-\frac{\kappa^{*}+\mathbf{i}\kappa}{2},

which should be compared with the statement of
[
26
, Lemma 13]
.
If we denote
z^{(0)}=z,z^{(1)}=\overline{z}
, developing Equation (
3.34
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{i},t_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\sum_{w_{i}b_{i}\in E_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}F^{(s_{i})}_{t_{i}}(b_{\sigma(i),w_{i}})\Delta z_{i}^{(s_{i})}(-1)^{t_{i}\eta(b_{\sigma(i)})+s_{i}\eta(b_{\sigma(i)})+s_{i}\eta(b_{i})}

(3.36)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{i},t_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\sum_{w_{i}b_{i}\in E_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}F^{(s_{i})}_{t_{i}}(b_{\sigma(i),w_{i}})\Delta z_{i}^{(s_{i})}(-1)^{\eta(b_{\sigma(i)})(t_{i}+s_{i}+s_{\sigma(i)})}

The indices
s_{i},t_{i}
denote which of the four terms we choose in Equation (
3.35
) for each term of the product in Equation (
3.34
).
The last equation is obtained by reindexing the last factor.
Li proves in her Lemma 14 that along any portion of the path
\gamma_{i}
,

\sum(-1)^{\eta(b_{i})}\Delta z_{i}=O(\varepsilon),

hence every term with
s_{i}+s_{\sigma(i)}+t_{i}=1\mod 2
for some
i
remains
O(\varepsilon)
when taking the sum along the path
\gamma_{i}
, since it is the product of an oscillating term with a smooth function. We obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]=\sum_{s_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\sum_{w_{i}b_{i}\in E_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}F^{(s_{i})}_{s_{i}+s_{\sigma(i)}}(b_{\sigma(i)},w_{i})\Delta z_{i}^{(s_{i})}+o(\varepsilon)

This is a Riemann-sum for a
k
-fold integral:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]\overset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{\sigma}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\int_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}F^{(s_{i})}_{s_{i}+s_{\sigma(i)}}(z_{\sigma(i)},z_{i})\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{i}\in\{0,1\}}\int_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{k}}\det_{i\neq j}\big[F_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(z_{i},z_{j})\big]\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}.

Note that the diagonal terms in the determinant are
0
since we sum over permutations
\sigma
with no fixed points.
Note that this holds uniformly for
(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{k})\in\Omega^{k}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega^{k})
, upon choosing paths
\gamma_{i}
of length
O(1/\varepsilon)
which remain at macroscopic distance from one another.
This proves Equation (
1.28
).

We now prove the bound Equation (
1.29
) which holds for any faces
(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{k})
in compact subsets of
\Omega^{k}
.
In the non-massive case, this is done in Lemma 16 of
[
26
]
. We prefer to use the argument of Appendix A.3 of
[
25
]
, where it is explained in more details.
Note that they can give a more precise bound since they have a more precise asymptotic estimate of
K^{-1}
. We will be satisfied with the following very rough bound.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega
be a compact subset,
(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{k})
be faces with
\zeta_{i}\subset\mathcal{C}
.
We choose paths
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}
from respectively
\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{k}
towards distinct points on the straight boundary, at distance of order
1
from each other.
We choose paths which are macroscopically far away except possibly near their extremities
\zeta_{i}
.
Near the extremities, we choose
\gamma_{i}
which remains within
O(\varepsilon)
of a straight line.
We choose a different asymptotic direction for each of the straight lines.
An example is provided in Figure 12 of
[
25
]
, in the case where the faces are partitioned into two sets,
5
of them near a face
f
and
5
of them near a face
f^{\prime}
at macroscopic distance from
f
.
Even though
[
25
]
is in the square lattice, we can do the same thing using the bounded angle assumption.
We can also assume that each path has length
N_{i}=|\gamma_{i}|=O(1/\varepsilon)
.

Number the edges of each path starting from the extremity
\zeta_{i}
:
e_{i}(1),e_{i}(2),\dots,e_{i}(N_{i})
.
Let
\delta_{ij}=|\zeta_{i}-\zeta_{j}|
.
Note that for such a choice of paths, we have

\dist(e_{i}(m_{i}),e_{j}(m_{j}))\geq c_{ij}(\delta_{ij}+\varepsilon(m_{i}+m_{j}))

for all
1\leq i,j\leq k
,
1\leq m_{i}\leq N_{i}
,
1\leq m_{j}\leq N_{j}
and some constant
c_{ij}>0
.
Since

\overline{\partial}(b_{i},w_{j})=O\left(\frac{1}{|b_{i}-w_{j}|}\right)

uniformly for
b_{i},w_{j}
in compact subsets of
\Omega\cup L
by Theorem
1.13
, and since
\overline{\partial}(w,b)=O(1)
uniformly for
w\sim b
, Equation (
3.33
) gives, for some constant
C>0
changing from line to line

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}h_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{1\leq m_{i}\leq N_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{i\sigma(i)}+\varepsilon(m_{i}+m_{\sigma(i)})}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\sigma}\sum_{1\leq m_{i}\leq N_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{i\sigma(i)}+\varepsilon m_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\sigma}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{1\leq m_{i}\leq N_{i}}\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{i\sigma(i)}+\varepsilon m_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\sigma}\prod_{i=1}^{k}(1+|\log(\delta_{i\sigma(i)})|)

where at the last line we use a classical bound for the harmonic series and the fact that
N_{i}=O(1/\varepsilon)
.

4
Identification with sine-Gordon model

4.1
Boundary value problem for correlation functions (Proof of Proposition
1.16
)

Before proving Proposition
1.16
, we first elaborate a bit on
G^{m},\kappa,\kappa^{*},F_{0},F_{1}
.
Recall that
G^{m}
is defined by Equation (
1.20
) or (
1.21
).
In the critical case
M=0
and in the upper half-plane
\Omega=\mathbb{H}
, it is well-known (see for example
[
23
]
) that

G_{\mathbb{H}}(w,z)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\log|z-w|^{2}-\log|z-\bar{w}|^{2}\right).

Still in the critical case
M=0
but for a general domain
\Omega
, the expression of the Green function is obtained by conformal invariance: if
g:\Omega\to\mathbb{H}
is a conformal map,

G(w,z)=G_{\mathbb{H}}(g(w),g(z))=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\log|g(z)-g(w)|^{2}-\log|g(z)-\bar{g}(w)|^{2}\right)

(4.1)

There is no such explicit expression of the massive Green function in the general case
M\neq 0
.
A direct consequence of Definition
1.11
is the bound

0\leq G^{m}(w,z)\leq G(w,z).

(4.2)

The massive and non-massive Green functions can be related through the
resolvent identity
(Proposition 4.10 of
[
6
]
, see also
[
29
,
13
]
for earlier use of this identity):

G^{m}(w,z)=G(w,z)+\int_{y\in\Omega}M(y)G^{m}(w,y)G(y,z)\mathrm{d}y.

(4.3)

This is proved by observing that the right-hand side is solution to the Dirichlet problem (
1.21
) defining the massive Green function.
Note that the integral in the right-hand side of Equation (
4.3
) is well-defined thanks to the bound (
4.2
).
The resolvent identity is very useful since it expresses the massive Green function as an explicit singular object (the critical Green function) + a non-singular non-explicit object which is easier to bound.

The operators
\kappa
and
\kappa^{*}
.

Recall that
\kappa
is defined by Equation (
1.23
)

\displaystyle\kappa(w,z)

\displaystyle=\overline{\nabla_{z}}G^{m}(w,z)-\overline{\alpha}(z)G^{m}(w,z)

(4.4)

and that
z\in\Omega
,
\kappa^{*}(\cdot,z)
is defined as the
\alpha
-conjugate of
\kappa(\cdot,z)
in the sense of Definition
1.10
.

In the critical case
\alpha=0
, we write
\kappa_{0},\kappa^{*}_{0}
.
By Equation (
4.1
),

\kappa_{0}(w,z)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\frac{g^{\prime}(z)}{g(z)-g(w)}-\frac{g^{\prime}(z)}{g(z)-\bar{g}(w)}\right).

(4.5)

Be careful that
\kappa_{0}^{*}
is defined as the conjugate in the sense of Definition
1.10
with
\alpha=0
.
It is easily checked that the conjugate (in this sense) of a holomorphic function
f(w)
is
-\mathbf{i}f(w)
, while the conjugate of an anti-holomorphic function
h(w)
is
\mathbf{i}h(w)
, hence

\kappa^{*}_{0}(w,z)=-\frac{\mathbf{i}}{4\pi}\left(\frac{g^{\prime}(z)}{g(z)-g(w)}+\frac{g^{\prime}(z)}{g(z)-\bar{g}(w)}\right).

They satisfy

\overline{\partial}\kappa_{0}(w,z)=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{w}(z)\quad;\quad\overline{\partial}\kappa^{*}_{0}(w,z)=-\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\delta_{w}(z).

(4.6)

In the general case, we write
\kappa=u+\mathbf{i}v
,
\kappa^{*}=u^{*}+\mathbf{i}v^{*}
, where
u^{*}(\cdot,z)
, resp.
v^{*}(\cdot,z)
, is the
\alpha
-conjugate of
u(\cdot,z)
, resp.
v(\cdot,z)
.
Let us be more precise. Let
z\in\Omega
.
For all
w\in\Omega
,

e^{V(w)}\nabla_{w}(u(w,z)e^{-V(w)})=e^{-V(w)}(-\mathbf{i})\nabla_{w}(u^{*}(w,z)e^{V(w)}),

(and the same holds for
v,v^{*}
).
For any
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Omega
, integrating along any path from
w_{1}
to
w_{2}
avoiding
z
,

u^{*}(w_{2},z)e^{V(w_{2})}-u^{*}(w_{1},z)e^{V(w_{1})}=\int_{w_{1}}^{w_{2}}e^{2V(w)}\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{w}(u(w,z)e^{-V(w)}),\mathrm{d}w\right\rangle.

(4.7)

(and the same holds for
v,v^{*})
.
In particular, since
\kappa^{*}
vanishes at
\beta^{*}
by definition (see Theorem
1.13
,

u^{*}(w,z)=e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{2V(\omega)}\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}(u(\omega,z)e^{-V(\omega)}),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle,

(4.8)

and the same holds for
v,v^{*}
.
The following consequence of the resolvent identity for the operators
\kappa,\kappa^{*}
will be crucial in the proof of Proposition
1.16
.

Lemma 4.1
.

For all
z,w\in\Omega
,

\kappa(w,z)=\kappa_{0}(w,z)+O(\log|z-w|)\quad;\quad\kappa^{*}(w,z)=\kappa^{*}_{0}(w,z)+O(\log|z-w|).

(4.9)

Proof.

Differentiating the resolvent identity, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{z}G^{m}(w,z)

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}G(w,z)+\int_{y\in\Omega}M(y)G^{m}(w,y)\partial_{z}G(y,z)\mathrm{d}y.

(4.10)

so

\displaystyle\kappa(w,z)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{0}(w,z)+\int_{y\in\Omega}M(y)G^{m}(w,y)\kappa_{0}(y,z)\mathrm{d}y-\bar{\alpha}(z)G^{m}(w,z).

(4.11)

Since
G^{m}(w,z)=O(\log|z-w|)
and
\partial_{z}G(y,z)=\kappa_{0}(y,z)=O(1/|z-y|)
(by the explicit expression of
\kappa_{0}
Equation (
4.5
)), we obtain that

\partial_{z}G^{m}(w,z)=\partial_{z}G(w,z)+O(1)

(4.12)

(the integral remains bounded) which implies

\kappa(w,z)=\kappa_{0}(w,z)+O(\log(|z-w|).

(4.13)

We establish the same for
\kappa_{0}^{*}
.
Taking the real part of Equation (
4.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle u(w,z)

\displaystyle=u_{0}(w,z)+\int_{y\in\Omega}M(y)G^{m}(w,y)u_{0}(y,z)\mathrm{d}y-\Re(\alpha)(z)G^{m}(w,z)

(4.14)

\displaystyle=:u_{0}(w,z)+u_{1}(w,z)+u_{2}(w,z).

We denote
\kappa_{0}=u_{0}+\mathbf{i}v_{0}
,
\kappa^{*}_{0}=u_{0}^{*}+\mathbf{i}v_{0}^{*}
.
Be careful that
u_{0}^{*},v_{0}^{*}
denote the
\alpha
-conjugate with
\alpha=0
(that is
\nabla u^{*}=\mathbf{i}\nabla u
) while
u^{*},v^{*}
denote the
\alpha
-conjugate with a general
\alpha
(that is
e^{-V}\nabla(u^{*}e^{V})=\mathbf{i}e^{V}\nabla(ue^{-V})
).
We show that
u^{*}=u_{0}^{*}+O(\log|z-w|)
.
By Equation (
4.8
),

\displaystyle u^{*}(w,z)

\displaystyle=e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{2V(\omega)}\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}(u(\omega,z)e^{-V(\omega)}),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle

(4.15)

\displaystyle=e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{2V(\omega)}\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}((u_{0}(\omega,z)+u_{1}(\omega,z)+u_{2}(\omega,z))e^{-V(\omega)}),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle

\displaystyle=:U_{0}(w,z)+U_{1}(w,z)+U_{2}(w,z).

The first term is

\displaystyle U_{0}(w,z)

\displaystyle=e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{2V(\omega)}\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}(u_{0}(\omega,z)e^{-V(\omega)}),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle

(4.16)

\displaystyle=e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{V(\omega)}\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}u_{0}(\omega,z),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle-e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{V(\omega)}u_{0}(w,z)\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}V(\omega),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}(1+O(w-\omega))\left\langle\nabla_{\omega}u_{0}(\omega,z),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle-e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{V(\omega)}u_{0}(w,z)\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}V(\omega),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle

\displaystyle=u_{0}^{*}(w,z)+\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}O(w-\omega)\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}(u_{0}(\omega,z)),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle-e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{V(\omega)}u_{0}(w,z)\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}V(\omega),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle

since
e^{V(w)-V(\omega)}=1+O(w-\omega)
and by definition of
u_{0}^{*}
.
Using the explicit form of
\kappa_{0}
from Equation (
4.5
) and the definition
u_{0}(w,z)=\Re(\kappa_{0}(w,z))
, we obtain that

u_{0}(w,z)=O(1/(z-w))\quad;\quad\nabla_{\omega}u_{0}(\omega,z)=O(1/(z-w)^{2}),

(4.17)

and after integrating this gives

U_{0}(w,z)=u_{0}^{*}(w,z)+O(\log|z-w|).

(4.18)

The second term is

\displaystyle U_{1}(w,z)

\displaystyle=e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{2V(\omega)}\left\langle\mathbf{i}\int_{y\in\Omega}M(y)u_{0}(y,z)\nabla_{\omega}G^{m}(\omega,y)\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle

(4.19)

By symmetry of the Green function and Equation (
4.12
),

\nabla_{\omega}G^{m}(\omega,y)=\nabla_{\omega}G(\omega,y)+O(1)=O\left(\frac{1}{\omega-y}\right)

Using this and Equation (
4.17
),

\int_{y\in\Omega}M(y)u_{0}(y,z)\nabla_{\omega}G^{m}(\omega,y)\mathrm{d}y=O\left(\frac{1}{\omega-z}\right)

And hence

U_{1}(w,z)=\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}\left\langle O\left(\frac{1}{\omega-z}\right),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle=O(\log|z-w|)).

(4.20)

Finally, using Equation (
4.17
) and the fact that
V
is a smooth function, we obtain

U_{2}(w,z)=\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}\left\langle O\left(\frac{1}{\omega-z}\right),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle=O(\log|z-w|).

(4.21)

Plugging Equations (
4.18
), (
4.20
) and (
4.21
) in Equation (
4.15
) gives

u^{*}(w,z)=u_{0}^{*}(w,z)+\log(|w-z|).

The same arguments hold for
v^{*}
, which implies

\kappa^{*}(w,z)=\kappa_{0}^{*}(w,z)+O(\log|z-w|)).

(4.22)

This concludes the proof of Lemma
4.1
.
∎

We are ready to prove Proposition
1.16
.

Proof of Proposition
1.16
.

We first study the off-diagonal behavior, that is we establish Proposition
1.16
for
z\neq w
.
We start with the first column.
Set
z\in\Omega
.
The fact that
\kappa(\cdot,z)
and
\kappa^{*}(\cdot,z)
are
\alpha
-conjugate for
z\neq w
can be reformulated as follows.
If we define
f(w)=u(w,z)+\mathbf{i}u^{*}(w,z)
and
g(w)=v(w,z)+\mathbf{i}v^{*}(w,z)
, they are
\alpha
-holomorphic away from
z
i.e. satisfy for all
w\neq z
,

\bar{\partial}_{w}f(w)=\frac{1}{2}\alpha(w)\bar{f}(w)\quad;\quad\bar{\partial}_{w}g(w)=\frac{1}{2}\alpha(w)\bar{g}(w).

(4.23)

Since

\displaystyle F_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\kappa^{*}-\mathbf{i}\kappa)=\frac{1}{2}(u^{*}+\mathbf{i}v^{*}-\mathbf{i}u+v)=\frac{1}{2}(v+\mathbf{i}v^{*}-\mathbf{i}(u+\mathbf{i}u^{*}))=\frac{1}{2}(g-\mathbf{i}f)

\displaystyle F_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(\kappa^{*}+\mathbf{i}\kappa)=-\frac{1}{2}(u^{*}+\mathbf{i}v^{*}+\mathbf{i}u-v)=-\frac{1}{2}(-(v-\mathbf{i}v^{*})+\mathbf{i}(u-\mathbf{i}u^{*}))=\frac{1}{2}(\bar{g}-\mathbf{i}\bar{f}),

Equation (
4.23
) implies that for
w\neq z
,

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{w}F_{0}(w,z)

\displaystyle=\bar{\partial}_{w}\frac{g(w)-\mathbf{i}f(w)}{2}=\frac{\alpha(w)}{2}\frac{\bar{g}(w)-\mathbf{i}\bar{f}(w)}{2}=\frac{\alpha(w)}{2}F_{1}(w,z)

(4.24)

\displaystyle\partial_{w}F_{1}(w,z)

\displaystyle=\bar{\partial}_{w}\frac{\bar{g}(w)-\mathbf{i}\bar{f}(w)}{2}=\frac{\bar{\alpha}(w)}{2}\frac{g(w)-\mathbf{i}f(w)}{2}=\frac{\bar{\alpha}(w)}{2}F_{0}(w,z).

Set
w\in\Omega
.
We claim that for
z\neq w
,

\bar{\partial}_{z}\kappa(w,z)=-\frac{\bar{\alpha}}{2}\bar{\kappa}(w,z)\quad;\quad\bar{\partial}_{z}\kappa^{*}(w,z)=-\frac{\bar{\alpha}}{2}\bar{\kappa}^{*}(w,z).

(4.25)

This is proved below.
Assuming Equation (
4.25
), for
z\neq w
,

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{z}F_{0}(w,z)=\bar{\partial}_{z}\frac{\kappa^{*}(w,z)-\mathbf{i}\kappa(w,z)}{2}=-\frac{\bar{\alpha}(z)}{2}\frac{\overline{\kappa^{*}}(w,z)-\mathbf{i}\overline{\kappa}(w,z)}{2}=\frac{\bar{\alpha}(z)}{2}\bar{F}_{1}(w,z)

(4.26)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{z}F_{1}(w,z)=-\bar{\partial}_{z}\frac{\kappa^{*}(w,z)+\mathbf{i}\kappa(w,z)}{2}=\frac{\bar{\alpha}(z)}{2}\frac{\overline{\kappa^{*}}(w,z)+\mathbf{i}\overline{\kappa}(w,z)}{2}=\frac{\bar{\alpha}(z)}{2}\bar{F}_{0}(w,z).

We check Equation (
4.25
).
The first part of this equation is Equation (
1.24
) with
z\neq w
.
This is equivalent to the
(-\bar{\alpha})
-Cauchy Riemann Equation (
1.16
)

\begin{cases}\partial_{z_{x}}(u(w,z)e^{V(z)})&=\partial_{z_{y}}(v(w,z)e^{V(z)})\\
\partial_{z_{y}}(u(w,z)e^{-V(z)})&=-\partial_{z_{x}}(v(w,z)e^{-V(z)})\end{cases}\quad;\quad w\neq z.

To check the second part of Equation (
4.25
), we must prove that this also hold for
\kappa^{*}
.
Recall that
u^{*},v^{*}
are obtained from
u,v
by Equation (
4.8
).
Since the derivatives in
z
and
w
commute

\displaystyle\partial_{z_{x}}(e^{V(z)}u^{*}(w,z))

\displaystyle=e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{2V(\omega)}\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}(e^{-V(\omega)}\partial_{z_{x}}(e^{V(z)}u(\omega,z)),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{-V(w)}\int_{\beta^{*}}^{w}e^{2V(\omega)}\left\langle\mathbf{i}\nabla_{\omega}(e^{-V(\omega)}\partial_{z_{y}}(e^{V(z)}v(\omega,z)),\mathrm{d}\omega\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\partial_{z_{y}}(e^{V(z)}v^{*}(w,z))

along any fixed path from
\beta^{*}
to
w
avoiding
z
.
The second part of the
\-\bar{\alpha}
-Cauchy-Riemann Equation works for the same reason, which gives the second part of Equation (
4.25
).

We finally consider the diagonal behavior.
Combining Lemma
4.1
with the explicit expression of
\kappa_{0}
and
\kappa^{*}_{0}
, we obtain that

F_{0}(w,z)=-\frac{\mathbf{i}}{4\pi}\frac{g^{\prime}(z)}{g(z)-g(w)}+O(\log|w-z|)\quad;\quad F_{1}(w,z)=O(\log|w-z|).

(4.27)

This directly implies the diagonal behavior

F_{0}(w,z)=\frac{\mathbf{i}}{4\pi(w-z)}+O(\log|w-z|)\quad;\quad F_{1}(w,z)=O(\log|w-z|)

(4.28)

which is exactly Equation (
1.33
).
It is standard to prove that the Dirac term in (
1.31
) follows from the off-diagonal equalities (
4.24
) and (
4.26
) and the diagonal behavior (
4.28
), by integration by parts and the residue theorem.
We detail it below for completeness.
Let
B(w,\varepsilon)
denote the Euclidean ball of radius
\varepsilon
around
w
, and for
z\in\partial B(w,\varepsilon)
let
n(z)
denote the outward unit normal vector.
Let
dV(z)
and
dl(z)
denote respectively the infinitesimal volume and perimeter.
Note that along
\partial B(w,\varepsilon)
,
n(z)dl(z)=dz
.
Let
f
be any smooth test function with zero boundary conditions in
\Omega
.
Integrating by parts
\kappa^{*}
and
f
(and integrating back after some algebraic manipulations) gives

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}F_{0}(w,z)\partial f(z)\mathrm{d}V(z)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega\setminus B(w,\varepsilon)}F_{0}(w,z)\partial f(z)\mathrm{d}V(z)

(4.29)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left[\int_{\partial B(w,\varepsilon)}F_{0}(w,z)f(z)n(z)\mathrm{d}l(z)-\int_{\Omega\setminus B(w,\varepsilon)}\overline{\partial}_{z}F_{0}(w,z)f(z)\mathrm{d}V(z)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left[\int_{\partial B(w,\varepsilon)}\left(-\frac{\mathbf{i}}{4\pi}\frac{g^{\prime}(z)}{g(z)-g(w)}+O(\log|w-z|)\right)f(z)n(z)\mathrm{d}l(z)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega\setminus B(w,\varepsilon)}\bar{\alpha}(z)\overline{\kappa^{*}}(w,z)f(z)\mathrm{d}V(z)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left[\frac{1}{4\mathbf{i}\pi}\int_{\partial B(w,\varepsilon)}\frac{g^{\prime}(z)}{g(z)-g(w)}f(z)(-\mathbf{i})\mathrm{d}z+O(\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon))\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\bar{\alpha}(z)\overline{\kappa^{*}}(w,z)f(z)\mathrm{d}V(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}f(w)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\bar{\alpha}(z)\overline{\kappa^{*}}(w,z)f(z)\mathrm{d}V(z)

which is the second part of Equation (
4.25
).
The same argument works for the other equations.

We now verify the statement concerning the boundary conditions in Proposition
1.16
. To obtain the boundary condition in the first variable, we simply notice that
\kappa(w,z)=0
when
w\in\partial\Omega
, which directly give the result.

We turn to the boundary conditions in the second variable.
By definition (Equation (
1.21
)),
G^{m}(w,z)=0
for
z\in\partial\Omega
.
Hence, since
G^{m}
vanishes in the tangent direction to the boundary, for
w\in\Omega,z\in\partial\Omega
,
\nabla_{z}G^{m}(w,z)=h_{z}(w)\eta(z)
where
h_{z}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
. Hence,

\kappa(w,z)=h_{z}(w)\bar{\eta}(z)\quad;\quad z\in\partial\Omega,w\in\Omega.

Recall that for
z\in\Omega
,
\kappa^{*}(\cdot,z)
is defined as the unique
\alpha
-conjugate of
\kappa(\cdot,z)
.
We claim that this also holds for
z\in\partial\Omega
(this will be proved below).
Since the
\alpha
-conjugate is defined coordinate by coordinate, if
h_{z}^{*}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
denotes the unique
\alpha
-conjugate of
h_{z}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, we simply have

\kappa^{*}(w,z)=h_{z}^{*}(w)\bar{\eta}(z)\quad;\quad z\in\partial\Omega,w\in\Omega.

Recall the definition of
F_{0},F_{1}
from Equation (
1.27
).
For
z\in\partial\Omega
, the above implies

F_{0}=\bar{\eta}(z)\frac{h_{z}^{*}(w)-\mathbf{i}h_{z}(w)}{2}\quad;\quad F_{1}=-\bar{\eta}(z)\frac{h_{z}^{*}(w)+\mathbf{i}h_{z}(w)}{2}.

Hence,

F_{1}(w,z)=-\frac{\bar{\eta}(z)}{\eta(z)}\bar{F}_{0}(w,z)=\frac{\bar{\tau}(z)}{\tau(z)}\bar{F}_{0}(w,z)=\bar{\tau}^{2}(z)\bar{F}_{0}(w,z).

We now check that
\kappa(\cdot,z)
and
\kappa^{*}(\cdot,z)
are also
\alpha
-conjugate when
z\in\partial\Omega
.
We already know that Equation (
4.7
) holds for
z\in\Omega
, that is for all
w_{1},w_{2}\neq z
,

u^{*}(w_{2},z)e^{V(w_{2})}-u^{*}(w_{1},z)e^{V(w_{1})}=\int_{w_{1}}^{w_{2}}e^{-2V(w)}\mathrm{Rot}_{\pi/2}\nabla_{v}(u(w,z)e^{-V(w)})\mathrm{d}w.

(4.30)

To prove that this remains true at
z\in\partial\Omega
, we can apply a dominated convergence theorem.
For this, it suffices to prove that
u(w,z)
and
\nabla_{w}u(w,z)
are continuous functions in
\bar{\Omega}\times\bar{\Omega}\setminus\mathcal{D}(\bar{\Omega}\times\bar{\Omega})
, bounded away from the diagonal.
By definition, if we denote by
\partial_{w_{1}},\partial_{w_{2}},\partial_{z_{1}},\partial_{z_{2}}
the partial derivatives with respect to the real and imaginary parts of
z
and
w
,

\displaystyle u(w,z)

\displaystyle=\Re\kappa(w,z)=\partial_{z_{1}}G^{m}(w,z)-\alpha_{1}(z)G^{m}(w,z),

(4.31)

\displaystyle v(w,z)

\displaystyle=\Im\kappa(w,z)=-\partial_{z_{2}}G^{m}(w,z)+\alpha_{y}(z)G^{m}(w,z).

(4.32)

Hence, we only need to prove that for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
,
G^{m}(w,z)
,
\partial_{z_{i}}G^{m}(w,z)
and
\partial_{w_{j}}\partial_{z_{i}}G^{m}(w,z)
are continuous in
(z,w)
and bounded away from the diagonal.

(i) For
G^{m}(w,z)
, this is true by definition, and furthermore

0\leq G^{m}(w,z)\leq G(w,z)=O(\log|z-w|).

(ii) The partial derivatives of the critical Green function in the half-plane are explicit:

\partial_{z_{x}}G_{\mathbb{H}}(w,z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\frac{z_{x}-w_{x}}{|z-w|^{2}}-\frac{z_{x}-w_{x}}{|z-\bar{w}|^{2}}\right)~;~\partial_{z_{x}}G_{\mathbb{H}}(w,z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\frac{z_{y}-w_{y}}{|z-w|^{2}}-\frac{z_{y}+w_{y}}{|z-\bar{w}|^{2}}\right).

Hence, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\partial_{z_{i}}G_{\mathbb{H}}(w,z)
is continuous in
(w,z)
away from the diagonal with a
O(1/|z-w|)
singularity.
Since the critical Green function in a general domain
G
is obtained from
G_{\mathbb{H}}
by Equation (
4.1
), this is also true for
G
.
Deriving the resolvent identity (
4.3
) with respect to the second variable, we obtain for
(w,z)\in\Omega\times\Omega\setminus\mathcal{D}(\Omega\times\Omega)
,

\partial_{z_{i}}G^{m}(w,z)=\partial_{z_{i}}G(w,z)+\int_{y\in\Omega}M(y)G^{m}(w,y)\partial_{z_{i}}G(y,z)\mathrm{d}y.

(4.33)

By applying the dominated convergence theorem in Equation (
4.33
), we obtain that
\partial_{z_{i}}G^{m}(w,z)
is continuous in
(w,z)
away from the diagonal, with a
O(1/|z-w|)
singularity.

(iii) Differentiating the resolvent identity with respect to the first variable, we obtain

\partial_{w_{j}}\partial_{z_{i}}G^{m}(w,z)=\partial_{w_{j}}\partial_{z_{i}}G(w,z)+\int_{y\in\Omega}M(y)\partial_{w_{j}}G^{m}(w,y)\partial_{z_{i}}G(y,z)\mathrm{d}y.

(4.34)

Similarly as before, an explicit computation shows that
\partial_{w_{j}}\partial_{z_{i}}G(w,z)
is continuous and bounded away from the diagonal. By symmetry of
G^{m}
and (ii),
\partial_{w_{j}}G^{m}(w,y)
is continuous and bounded away from the diagonal with a
O(1/|w-y|))
singularity. Moreover, as we already proved above,
\partial_{z_{i}}G(y,z)
is continuous and bounded away from the diagonal with a
O(1/|z-y|))
singularity.
Hence, the integrand in the right-hand side of Equation (
4.34
) remains integrable for
(w,z)
away from the diagonal and the dominated convergence theorem applies.
∎

4.2
Overview of proof of Theorem
1.19

In this section we give an overview of what remains to be proved for Theorem
1.19
. We start by rephrasing Theorem
1.14
and Proposition
1.16
as follows. Let
\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}
denote the expectation corresponding to the law of the limiting height function
\phi
, which is already known to exist as soon as
\Omega
is bounded and simply connected and
\alpha
derives from a log-convex potential which is (
C^{1}
) on
\bar{\Omega}
, by
[
6
]
.
For a pair of function
(F_{0},F_{1})
and
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we call

v_{(F_{0},F_{1})}^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}):=\det_{i\neq j}\Big[F_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(z_{i},z_{j})\Big]

Corollary 4.2
.

Assume that
\Omega
is bounded with a straight boundary, and that
\alpha
derives from a log-convex potential which is smooth (
C^{1}
) on
\bar{\Omega}
.
Then,

•

\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
has a solution
(F_{0},F_{1})
(provided by Theorem
1.14
)

•

Then for
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{i})}_{z_{i}}\phi(z_{i})]=v_{(F_{0},F_{1})}^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})

(4.35)

Here, as before,
\partial^{(0)}
and
\partial^{(1)}
refer to the Wirtinger derivatives
\partial_{z}
and
\bar{\partial}_{z}
respectively. Of course, the limiting height function
\phi
is rough, so the Wirtinger derivatives of
\phi
do not make literal sense, and these are understood in the weak sense.
Concretely, the meaning of (
4.35
) is that for any smooth, compactly supported test function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
one can write

\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}[(\phi,f)^{n}]=\int\ldots\int f(z_{1})\ldots f(z_{n})V_{(F_{0},F_{1})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\mathrm{d}A(z_{1})\ldots\mathrm{d}A(z_{n})

(4.36)

where
dA(z)
denotes integration with respect to the area (Lebesgue) measure, and

V_{(F_{0},F_{1})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\int_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{n}}v_{(F_{0},F_{1})}^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}.

Here,
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}
are arbitrary paths that are disjoint, connecting
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
respectively to the straight edge portion of
\partial\Omega
.
The identification with the sine-Gordon field of Conjecture
1.4
proceeds in four steps:

•

First, we prove a
conformal change of coordinate
formula which, together with results from
[
6
]
implies that Corollary
4.2
holds for general domains

•

Next, we check that the determinant in Corollary
4.2
can be written in terms of
fermionic expressions
.
This involves performing a gauge change to transform the boundary conditions of the boundary value problem.
These fermionic expressions match the sine-Gordon field via the Coleman transform.

•

We finally explain how to check in concrete cases our conditions on the domain
\Omega
and the vector field
\alpha
.
Together with the Coleman transform of
[
32
]
, this proves in particular that in the case of the unit disc, the limiting field that we identify is indeed the sine-Gordon model.

Step 1.

We verify that Corollary
4.2
extends to more general domains with the conformal covariance rule described in
[
6
]
.
This is an important step in the identification of the limiting height function as the sine-Gordon field for the following reason: on the one hand, our Theorem
1.14
requires the boundary of the domain to have a straight portion. On the other hand, the Coleman correspondence (on which we rely for this identification) is established in
[
32
]
only in the case where the domain
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
is the unit disc. The result below will therefore allow us to compare these two results and eventually conclude that in the unit disc, the limiting moments of the height function match those of the sine-Gordon model.

This change of domain relies on the following change of coordinates formula.
Let
T:\Omega\to T\Omega
be a conformal isomorphism.
Denote by

T\alpha(w)=\overline{\psi^{\prime}(w)}\alpha(\psi(w)).

(4.37)

the change of drift vector under the conformal covariance rule of
[
6
]
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\Omega
be any simply connected domain.
Let
T:\Omega\to T\Omega
be a conformal isomorphism and let
\psi=T^{-1}
.
For
(F_{0},F_{1})
, let

TF_{s}(w_{1},w_{2})=\psi^{\prime}(w_{2})F_{s}(\psi(w_{1}),\psi(w_{2}));\quad s\in\{0,1\}.

Then,
(F_{0},F_{1})
solves
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
if and only if
(TF_{0},TF_{1})
solves
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(T\Omega,T\alpha)
.
When that is the case, for all
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\in T\Omega
which are pairwise disjoint,

V_{(F_{0},F_{1})}(\psi(w_{1}),\ldots,\psi(w_{k}))=V_{(TF_{0},TF_{1})}(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}).

The proof of this Proposition is given in Section
4.3
.

Remark 4.4
.

Note that the identity (
4.37
) matches exactly (1.23) in the known conformal covariance of the limiting near-critical dimer height function (Theorem 1.7 of
[
6
]
).

Recall that by Theorem 1.7 in
[
6
]
, if
T:\Omega\to T\Omega
is a conformal isomorphism of bounded simply connected domains with inverse
T^{-1}
, and
T
extends analytically to a neighbourhood of
\Omega
(which we will henceforth denote as ”smooth”),
then the height function
\phi^{\Omega,\alpha}
with law
\mathbb{P}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}
and the height function
\phi^{T\Omega,T\alpha}
with law
\mathbb{P}_{(T\Omega,T\alpha)}
are related by

\phi^{\Omega,\alpha}\circ\psi=\phi^{T\Omega,T\alpha}

(4.38)

Together, Corollary
4.2
, Proposition
4.3
and (
4.38
) directly imply the following

Corollary 4.5
.

Assume that
\Omega
is the image by a smooth conformal transformation of a bounded domain with a straight boundary, and that
\alpha
derives from a log-convex potential which is smooth (
C^{1}
) on
\bar{\Omega}
.

•

\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
has a solution
(F_{0},F_{1})
(provided by Theorem
1.14
and the conformal change of coordinate)

•

For
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{i})}_{z_{i}}\phi(z_{i})]=v_{(F_{0},F_{1})}^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})

(4.39)

Step 2.

The next step gives a fermionic representation for the correlation function
v^{(\mathbf{s})}_{(F_{0},F_{1})}
.
In order to do this, we check that they coincide with the correlation functions
\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{SG(\rho|\Omega)}
of the mixed Wirtinger derivatives of the sine-Gordon introduced in Equation (
1.40
).
Recall the definition of the correlations of the mixed Wirtinger derivatives of the sine-Gordon introduced in Equation (
1.40
).

\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho)}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{j})}_{z_{j}}\phi(z_{j})\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho|\Omega)}.

(4.40)

The proof is based on a change of gauge compared to the expression for
(F_{0},F_{1})
appearing in Corollary
4.5
and some rather surprising exact identities.
We first introduce a modified electromagnetic field

\tilde{\alpha}(x)=\alpha(x)e^{\mathbf{i}\arg g^{\prime}(x)}.

(4.41)

where
g:\Omega\to\mathbb{H}
is a conformal isomorphism, and
\arg g^{\prime}
is any determination of the argument.
The significance of this modified vector field (and how it arises naturally from Conjecture (
1.4
)) will be explained in Section
C.1
.
Define the change of gauge

\tilde{F}_{0}(w,z)=\sqrt{\frac{g^{\prime}(w)}{g^{\prime}(z)}}F_{0}(w,z);\quad\tilde{F}_{1}(w,z)=\sqrt{\frac{\overline{g^{\prime}}(w)}{g^{\prime}(z)}}F_{1}(w,z).

(4.42)

We define a new boundary value problem,
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
:

\begin{cases}\bar{\partial}_{w}F_{0}(w,z)=\frac{\tilde{\alpha}(w)}{2}F_{1}(w,z)+\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\delta_{w}(z)\quad;\quad\partial_{w}F_{1}(w,z)=\frac{\overline{\tilde{\alpha}}(w)}{2}F_{0}(w,z)&\text{ on }\Omega^{2}\\
\tilde{F}_{1}(w,z)=-\tau(w)\tilde{F}_{0}(w,z);&\text{ if }w\in\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

(4.43)

where as before
\tau(w)
is the tangent vector at
w\in\partial\Omega
such that
\Omega
lies locally to the left of
\tau(w)
.
Note that only the boundary conditions changes compared to
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
.
Again, the first two lines can be conveniently rewritten in terms of the Dirac operator

\not{\partial}_{\tilde{\alpha}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{i}\tilde{F}_{1}&\mathbf{i}\overline{\tilde{F}_{0}}\\
-\mathbf{i}\tilde{F}_{0}&-\mathbf{i}\overline{\tilde{F}_{1}}\end{pmatrix}=I\text{ on }\Omega^{2}

Lemma 4.6
.

(F_{0},F_{1})
satisfies
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
if and only if
(\tilde{F}_{0},\tilde{F}_{1})
satisfies
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
.
Moreover,

\det_{i\neq j}[F^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})]=\det_{i\neq j}[\tilde{F}^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})].

(4.44)

This lemma will be proved in Section
4.4
.

As a summary of the first two steps, we have already proved that

v_{(F_{0},F_{1})}^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})=\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{i})}_{z_{i}}\phi(z_{i})]=\det_{i\neq j}\Big[F_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(z_{i},z_{j})\Big]

where
(F_{0},F_{1})
satisfy
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
.
By Lemma
4.6
, we find that this is equivalent to

v^{(\mathbf{s})}_{(F_{0},F_{1})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\det_{i\neq j}[\tilde{F}^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})]

(4.45)

with
(\tilde{F}_{0},\tilde{F}_{1})
the unique solution to
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
.
It only remains to connect this expression with the fermionic expression for correlations of the sine-Gordon obtained in
[
32
]
to conclude: this is done in Section
4.6
.

We will as a result be able to make a connection to the sine-Gordon model, and more precisely to its Coleman form, i.e., to its expression as a Thirring model, as we now explain. In this model the “spins” are fermionic variables
(\psi(z))_{z\in\Omega}
where for each
z
,
\psi(z)=(\psi_{1}(z),\psi_{2}(z))
is a vector of size two, whose formal density is given by

\exp\Big[-\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{d}x\ \big(\bar{\psi}\not{\partial}\psi+\tilde{\alpha}\bar{\psi}_{1}\psi_{1}+\overline{\tilde{\alpha}}\bar{\psi}_{2}\psi_{2}\big)\Big].

(4.46)

See
[
5
]
for a rigorous construction (this is a particular case of the so-called
Thirring model
). We call this field the
Coleman form of the sine-Gordon with electromagnetic field
\tilde{\alpha}
(
\mathrm{CSG}(\tilde{\alpha})
for short).

Theorem 4.7
.

Assume that
\Omega
is the image by a smooth conformal transformation of a bounded domain with a straight boundary, and that
\alpha
derives from a log-convex potential which is smooth (
C^{1}
) on
\bar{\Omega}
.

•

\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
has a solution (provided by Theorem
1.14
, the conformal change of coordinate and the above gauge change)

•

If the solution to
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
is unique, the correlation kernels
v^{(\mathbf{s})}
of holomorphic and antiholomorphic derivatives of the height function
(\Omega,\alpha)

agree with the Majorana fermionic correlations of the above field:
for
\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, letting
\lambda=-1/(4\sqrt{\pi})
,

v^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{i})}_{z_{i}}\phi(z_{i})]=\lambda^{n}\det\left(\Big\langle\psi^{s_{i}}(z_{i}),\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\Big\rangle\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}

(4.47)

where
\psi^{0}
stands for
\psi
, and
\psi^{1}
for
\psi^{*}
, defined as
\psi=\psi_{1}+\mathbf{i}\psi_{2}
and
\psi^{*}=\psi_{1}-\mathbf{i}\psi_{2}
.
Alternatively,
\Big\langle\psi^{s_{i}}(z_{i}),\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\Big\rangle
is the unique solution of the boundary value problem
\mathrm{BV}_{3}(\Omega,\theta)
, with
\theta=\tilde{\alpha}/(2\mathbf{i})
defined in (
4.66
).

•

If furthermore,
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
and
\theta=\tilde{\alpha}/(2\mathbf{i})
is real-valued and in
C^{\infty}(\Omega)
, then

v^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{i})}_{z_{i}}\phi(z_{i})]=\lambda^{n}\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{j})}\phi(z_{j}))\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(-\frac{4\theta}{\pi}|\Omega)}

Step 3.

The above result Theorem
4.7
only applies under some assumption on
\Omega
and
\alpha
which may look hard to check in practice.
We provide an explicit criterion for the uniqueness of the boundary value problem, and explain how this can be applied in practice.
Following a similar argument as that found in
[
3
]
, we observe that the solutions to the boundary value problem
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
(or equivalently
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
by Lemma
4.6
) are unique.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\Omega
be an arbitrary (not necessarily bounded) simply connected domain, and
\tilde{\alpha}
be a
C^{1}
vector field on
\bar{\Omega}
.
Assume that
\Im(\tilde{\alpha})\leq 0
and that the support of
\Im(\alpha)
is not empty.
Then if
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
has a solution, it is unique.

This lemma will be proved in Section
4.5
.
This criterion may appear arbitrary, and we believe that uniqueness in the boundary value problem holds under more general conditions.
However, this will be enough to check the assumption of Theorem
4.7
in many cases in combination with Proposition
4.3
, Lemma
4.6
, and the fact that when
\Omega
is the half-plane,
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha
.
In particular, we can prove Theorem
1.19
.

Proof of Theorem
1.19
.

Let
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
,
T:\Omega\to\mathbb{H}
with inverse
g
and
V_{\mathbb{H}},V=V_{\mathbb{H}}\circ g,\alpha_{\mathbb{H}}=\nabla V_{\mathbb{H}},\alpha=\nabla V
be as in the statement of the theorem.
Note that

\alpha(z)=\nabla V_{\mathbb{H}}\circ g=\overline{g^{\prime}(z)}V(g(z)).

(4.48)

We check the assumptions of Theorem
4.7
.
First of all,
\alpha
derives from the log-convex potential
V
.

In the half-plane, uniqueness holds for
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\mathbb{H},\tilde{\alpha})
by Lemma
4.8
since
\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbb{H}}=\alpha_{\mathbb{H}}
has non-positive imaginary part by our assumptions on
V_{\mathbb{H}}
.
By Lemma
4.6
, uniqueness also holds for
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\mathbb{H},\alpha_{\mathbb{H}})
since
\tilde{\alpha}_{\mathbb{H}}=\alpha_{\mathbb{H}}
.
By Proposition
4.3
, uniqueness also holds for
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
.

Finally, we check that
\tilde{\alpha}
is imaginary-valued. By definition of
\tilde{\alpha}
and Equation (
4.48
),

\tilde{\alpha}(z)=e^{\mathbf{i}\arg g^{\prime}(z)}\alpha(z)=e^{\mathbf{i}\arg g^{\prime}(z)}\overline{g^{\prime}(z)}\alpha_{\mathbb{H}}(g(z))=|g^{\prime}(z)|\alpha_{\mathbb{H}}(g(z)),

where
\alpha_{\mathbb{H}}(g(z))
is pure imaginary. This concludes the proof under the assumption that
\alpha
satisfies (
1.44
).

Let us briefly explain what happens under the assumptions of Remark
1.20
instead, i.e., when
V_{\mathbb{D}}(z)=f(\Im(g(z))
, where
f
is as in Theorem
1.19
, and
\alpha(z)=\nabla V_{\mathbb{D}}(z),z\in\mathbb{D}
. While
\alpha
derives from a potential by definition, there are several that we need to check:

•

\tilde{\alpha}
is purely imaginary, with negative imaginary part.

•

the potential
V_{\mathbb{D}}
is log-convex.

In fact these two aspects are closely related to one another, and we briefly explain how to verify this. We start with the verification of the first point. Let
\tilde{f}(w)=f(\Im(w)),w\in\mathbb{H}
. Then
V_{\mathbb{D}}(z)=\tilde{f}\circ g
. Thus another expression for
\alpha
is
\alpha=2\bar{\partial}V_{\mathbb{D}}
. Applying the chain rule for Wirtinger derivatives, we find, letting
w=g(z)
:

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}V_{\mathbb{D}}(z)

\displaystyle=\partial\tilde{f}(w)\cdot\bar{\partial}g(z)+\bar{\partial}\tilde{f}(w)\cdot\bar{\partial}\bar{g}(z)

\displaystyle=2\nabla\tilde{f}(w)\overline{g^{\prime}(z)}

(since
g
is holomorphic, the first term in the right hand side of the first equality is zero). Now,
\tilde{f}
depends only on the imaginary part of
w
so its gradient is purely imaginary, in fact given by
\mathbf{i}f^{\prime}(\Im(w))
. We deduce that

\alpha(z)=\mathbf{i}f^{\prime}(\Im(w))\overline{g^{\prime}(z)}.

(4.49)

Therefore,

\tilde{\alpha}(z)=e^{\mathbf{i}\arg g^{\prime}(z)}\alpha(z)=\mathbf{i}|g^{\prime}(z)|f^{\prime}(\Im(w)).

In particular,
\tilde{\alpha}
is purely imaginary, with negative imaginary part, as desired in the first point.

For the second part, we need to check if
\Delta V_{\mathbb{D}}+|\nabla V_{\mathbb{D}}|^{2}\geq 0
. We already computed
\nabla V_{\mathbb{D}}
so let us compute

\displaystyle\Delta V_{\mathbb{D}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4}\partial\bar{\partial}V_{\mathbb{D}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\partial\Big(\mathbf{i}f^{\prime}(\Im(g(z)))\overline{g^{\prime}(z)}\Big)

where we used (
4.49
). Since
\bar{g}^{\prime}
is antiholomorphic, we have

\partial\Big(\mathbf{i}f^{\prime}(\Im(g(z)))\overline{g^{\prime}(z)}\Big)=\mathbf{i}\overline{g^{\prime}(z)}\partial\Big(f^{\prime}(\Im(g(z)))\Big)

The above holmorphic derivative can be computed exactly in the same manner as above; in fact, since
f^{\prime}
is also a real valued function we can simply take the conjugate in (
4.49
) and replace
f
by
f^{\prime}
. We obtain:

\Delta V_{\mathbb{D}}(z)=|g^{\prime}(z)|^{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\Im g(z))

(4.50)

so that altogether

\Delta V_{\mathbb{D}}(z)+|\nabla V_{\mathbb{D}}(z)|^{2}=|g^{\prime}(z)|^{2}\Big(f^{\prime\prime}+(f^{\prime})^{2}\Big)(\Im g(z))\geq 0

since we assumed that
f
is log-convex (i.e.,
(e^{f})^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
). This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.19
also in this case.
∎

4.3
Change of coordinate formula

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Recall that
T:\Omega\to T\Omega
is a conformal isomorphism and that we define the functions
TF_{0},TF_{1}
in
T\Omega
by

TF_{s}(w_{1},w_{2})=\psi^{\prime}(w_{2})F_{s}(\psi(w_{1}),\psi(w_{2}));\quad s\in\{0,1\}.

Let us fix
s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\in\{0,1\}
. For
1\leq i\leq k
, let
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}=T(\gamma_{i})
denote the paths in
T\Omega
obtained by mapping
\gamma_{i}
through
T
. Then by change of variable
w_{i}=\psi(z_{i})
, one immediately has

\int_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{k}}\det_{i\neq j}\Big[F_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(z_{i},z_{j})\Big]\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}=\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{1}}\cdots\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{k}}\det_{i\neq j}\Big[F_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(\psi(w_{i}),\psi(w_{j}))\Big]\prod_{i=1}^{k}\psi^{\prime}(w_{i})^{(s_{i})}\mathrm{d}w_{i}^{(s_{i})}

Using the multilinearity of the determinant (columnwise) we can rewrite this multiple integral as

\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{1}}\cdots\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{k}}\det_{i\neq j}\Big[\psi^{\prime}(w_{j})^{(s_{j})}F_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(\psi(w_{i}),\psi(w_{j}))\Big]\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{d}w_{i}^{(s_{i})}=\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{1}}\cdots\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{k}}\det_{i\neq j}\Big[TF_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(w_{i},w_{j})\Big]\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{d}w_{i}^{(s_{i})}

When summing over the signs
s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\in\{0,1\}
we get a function of
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}
which has the right formal expression.

It remains to verify that
TF_{0},TF_{1}
solves
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(T\Omega,T\alpha)
.
It is straightforward to check that
TF_{s}
has the correct diagonal behavior, that is the second equation of
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(T\Omega,T\alpha)
: it follows from the diagonal behavior of
F_{s}
, the definition of
TF_{s}
and the analycity of
T
.
Now, we verify that
TF_{0},TF_{1}
satisfy the first equation of
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(T\Omega,T\alpha)
. Consider for instance
TF_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{w}TF_{0}(w,z)

\displaystyle=\bar{\partial}_{w}[\psi^{\prime}(z)F_{0}(\psi(w),\psi(z)]

\displaystyle=\psi^{\prime}(z)\bar{\psi}^{\prime}(w)\bar{\partial}_{w}F_{0}(\psi(w),\psi(z))

\displaystyle=\psi^{\prime}(z)\bar{\psi}^{\prime}(w)\left[\frac{\alpha(w)}{2}F_{1}(\psi(w),\psi(z))+\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\delta_{\psi(w)}(\psi(z))\right]

\displaystyle=\psi^{\prime}(w)\frac{\bar{\alpha}(w)}{2}TF_{1}(w,z)+\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}|\psi^{\prime}(w)|^{2}\delta_{\psi(w)}(\psi(z))

\displaystyle=\frac{T\alpha(w)}{2}TF_{1}(w,z)+\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}|\psi^{\prime}(w)|^{2}\delta_{\psi(w)}(\psi(z))

\displaystyle=\frac{T\alpha(w)}{2}TF_{1}(w,z)+\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\delta_{w}(z),

where at the last line we use that
|\psi^{\prime}(w)|^{2}\delta_{\psi(w)}(\psi(z))=\delta_{w}(z)
due to the change of variable formula, since these equations are understood in the weak sense.
The other equation is checked similarly.

To conclude the proof of Proposition
4.3
, we only need to check the boundary conditions, that is the third equation of
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(T\Omega,T\alpha)
. We simply write, for
w\in\partial\Omega
,

TF_{1}(w,z)=\psi^{\prime}(z)F_{1}(\psi(w),\psi(z))=-\psi^{\prime}(z)F_{0}(\psi(w),\psi(z))=-TF_{0}(w,z).\qed

4.4
Gauge change in boundary value problem (Lemma
4.6
)

Proof of Lemma
4.6
.

First of all, it is straightforward to check that the gauge change does not change the diagonal behavior, thus
(F_{0},F_{1})
satisfies the second equation of
\mathrm{BV}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
if and only if
(\tilde{F}_{0},\tilde{F}_{1})
satisfies the second equation of
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
.

We now check that
(F_{0},F_{1})
satisfies (
1.31
) in
\Omega
if and only if
(\tilde{F}_{0},\tilde{F}_{1})
satisfies the same equation in
\Omega
with
\alpha
replaced by
\tilde{\alpha}
. For instance, since
g^{\prime}
is analytic (so that
\bar{\partial}_{w}g^{\prime}(w)=0
),

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{w}\tilde{F}_{0}(w,z)

\displaystyle=\bar{\partial}_{w}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{g^{\prime}(w)}{g^{\prime}(z)}}F_{0}(w,z)\right\}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{g^{\prime}(w)}{g^{\prime}(z)}}\bar{\partial}_{w}F_{0}(w,z)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{g^{\prime}(w)}{g^{\prime}(z)}}\left(\frac{\alpha(w)}{2}F_{1}(w,z)+\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\delta_{w}(z)\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{g^{\prime}(w)}{\bar{g}^{\prime}(w)}}\frac{\alpha(w)}{2}\tilde{F}_{1}(w,z)+\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\delta_{w}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\alpha}(w)}{2}\tilde{F}_{1}(w,z)+\frac{\mathbf{i}}{2}\delta_{w}(z).

In the last line, we used the fact that

\frac{g^{\prime}(w)}{\bar{g}^{\prime}(w)}=e^{2\mathbf{i}\arg g^{\prime}(w)}

as well as the definition of
\tilde{\alpha}(w)
in (
4.41
). The other three equations can be checked the same way.

To check the boundary conditions, we simply observe that by definition of
\tilde{F}_{1},\tilde{F}_{0}
, and using the boundary conditions satisfied by
F_{1},F_{0}
(see again Proposition
1.16
), we have
for
w\in\partial\Omega,z\in\Omega
:

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{1}(w,z)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime}(w)}{g^{\prime}(z)}}\tilde{F}_{0}(w,z)=-\tau(w)\tilde{F}_{0}(w,z),

using the fact that
g^{-1}:\mathbb{H}\to\Omega
gives a parameterization of the boundary
\partial\Omega
(thus
1/g^{\prime}(w)
is proportional to
\tau(w)
).
(Although this is not needed in the following, one can also observe that in the other variable
z
, the boundary condition reads

\tilde{F}_{1}(w,z)=-\bar{\tau}(z)\overline{\tilde{F}}_{0}(w,z);\ z\in\partial\Omega.

(4.51)

Thus
(F_{0},F_{1})
satisfies BV
{}_{1}(\Omega,\alpha)
if and only if
(\tilde{F}_{0},\tilde{F}_{1})
satisfies BV
{}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
.

For the next item, the starting point is the basic observation that for a matrix
K
and a nonzero complex valued function
f
,

\det[K(x_{i},x_{j})]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=\det\left[\frac{c(x_{i})}{c(x_{j})}K(x_{i},x_{j})\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}.

(4.52)

(This follows immediately from the multilinearity of the determinant which implies that in the right hand side we pick up a term
c(x_{i})
for each line and another ter
1/c(x_{j})
for each column, so the determinant is unchanged overall).

Here the transformation we apply to
F
is not obviously of this form. However, note that if
s\in\{0,1\}
then we can write compactly

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{s}(w,z)=\sqrt{\frac{g^{\prime}(w)^{(s)}}{g^{\prime}(z)}}F_{s}(w,z).

(4.53)

Thus, if

s_{i},s_{j}
are given then

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})

\displaystyle=\left\{\sqrt{\frac{g^{\prime}(z_{i})^{(s_{i}+s_{j})}}{g^{\prime}(z_{j})}}\right\}^{(s_{j})}F_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(z_{i},z_{j})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{g^{\prime}(z_{i})^{(s_{i})}}{g^{\prime}(z_{j})^{(s_{j})}}}F_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{j})}(z_{i},z_{j})

(consider separately the case
s_{j}=0
and
s_{j}=1
to check the last identity). We can thus apply (
4.52
) to conclude with the proof of Lemma
4.6
.
∎

4.5
Uniqueness of boundary value problem (Lemma
4.8
)

Proof.

Assume that
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
has two solutions
(\tilde{F}_{0},\tilde{F}_{1})
and
(\tilde{G}_{0},\tilde{G}_{1})
.
Let
z\in\Omega
be fixed, and denote
u_{i}(w)=\tilde{F}_{i}(w,z)-\tilde{G}_{i}(w,z)
for
i=0,1
.
We prove that
u_{0}=u_{1}=0
.
First note that
u_{0},u_{1}
satisfy
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
without the Dirac term, thus is smooth by elliptic regularity theory.
By definition of
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
,
u_{0}(w)=-\tau u_{1}(w)
for
w\in\partial\Omega
and, for
w\in\Omega
,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&2\bar{\partial}\\
2\partial&\overline{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u_{0}\\
u_{1}\end{pmatrix}=0.

(4.54)

First note that

\displaystyle 4\Im(\partial(u_{0}\bar{u}_{1}))

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\mathbf{i}}\Big(\partial(u_{0}\bar{u}_{1})-\bar{\partial}(\bar{u}_{0}u_{1})\Big).

Furthermore,

\displaystyle 2\partial(u_{0}\bar{u}_{1})

\displaystyle=2\bar{u}_{1}\partial u_{0}+2u_{0}\partial\bar{u}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\bar{\alpha}u_{1}\bar{u}_{1}-\bar{\alpha}\bar{u}_{0}u_{0}

\displaystyle=-\bar{\alpha}(|u_{0}|^{2}+|u_{1}|^{2}).

Likewise,

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}(\bar{u}_{0}u_{1})

\displaystyle=-\alpha(|u_{0}|^{2}+|u_{1}|^{2}),

so that

\displaystyle 4\Im(\partial(u_{0}\bar{u}_{1}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}}\Big(-\bar{\alpha}+\alpha\Big)(|u_{0}|^{2}+|u_{1}|^{2})

(4.55)

\displaystyle=2\Im(\alpha)(|u_{0}|^{2}+|u_{1}|^{2}).

(4.56)

Now, by Green’s theorem, if
f
is a
C^{1}
function on
\bar{\Omega}
,

\int_{\Omega}\partial f(w)\mathrm{d}A(w)=\frac{1}{2\mathbf{i}}\int_{\partial\Omega}f(w)\mathrm{d}\bar{w}
.
We therefore have:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\Im(\alpha)(|u_{0}|^{2}+|u_{1}|^{2})\mathrm{d}A(w)

\displaystyle=\Im\left(2\int_{\Omega}\partial(u_{0}\bar{u}_{1})\mathrm{d}A(w)\right)

\displaystyle=\Im\left(\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}}\int_{\partial\Omega}u_{0}(w)\bar{u}_{1}(w)\mathrm{d}\bar{w}\right)

\displaystyle=\Im\left(\frac{1}{\mathbf{i}}\int_{\partial\Omega}-\tau(w)u_{1}(w)\bar{u}_{1}(w)\mathrm{d}\bar{w}\right)

\displaystyle=\Im\left(\mathbf{i}\int_{0}^{1}\tau(\gamma(s))|u_{1}(\gamma(s))|^{2}\overline{\gamma^{\prime}(s)}\mathrm{d}s\right)\geq 0

(4.57)

where
\gamma(s),s\in[0,1]
is a parametrisation (in the anticlockwise direction) of
\partial\Omega
, and in the last line, we use the fact that
\gamma^{\prime}(s)
is a positive multiple of
\tau(s)
.

Since
\Im(\alpha)<0
in its support, this shows that

u_{0}=u_{1}=0
in the support of
\Im(\alpha)
.
Outside of the support of
\Im(\alpha)
,
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
are holomorphic/anti-holomorphic, and thus also vanish on the complement of the support.
∎

4.6
Identification with sine-Gordon: proof of Theorem
4.7

We start with giving more details about
the main theorem of
[
32
]
(their Theorem (1.1)), which is a bosonisation theorem for the above observables together with further trigonometric functions. Since we only need a subset of their results, we state here for convenience the part which is needed for our purposes. Fix functions
(f_{j})_{1\leq j\leq p}
and
(g_{j^{\prime}})_{1\leq j^{\prime}\leq q}
as in Theorem
1.18
.

Theorem 4.9
.

(Part of Theorem 1.1 and Proposition 6.16 in
[
32
]
,
U=V=A=\emptyset
in their notation). Let
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
be the unit disc and let
\theta:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be a
real-valued
smooth function, i.e.,
\theta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Then the correlations of the derivatives of the sine-Gordon field are given by

\displaystyle\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{p}(2\mathbf{i}\sqrt{\pi}\partial\phi(f_{j}))\prod_{j^{\prime}=1}^{q}(-2\mathbf{i}\sqrt{\pi}\bar{\partial}\phi(f^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}))\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(-\frac{4\theta}{\pi}|\Omega)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\Big\langle\prod_{j}\psi\tilde{\psi}(z_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}}\psi^{*}\tilde{\psi}^{*}(w_{j^{\prime}})\Big\rangle_{\theta}\prod_{j}f(z_{j})\prod_{j^{\prime}}f^{\prime}(w_{j^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}w.

(4.58)

Here,
\psi
and
\tilde{\psi}
are independent and the right hand side may be defined by

\displaystyle\Big\langle\prod_{j}\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\Big\rangle_{\theta}:=\mathrm{Pf}\Big[\langle\psi^{s_{i}}(z_{i})\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\rangle_{\theta}\Big]_{i,j}

(4.59)

where, compared to (
4.9
), we identify
s_{j}=0
with no
∗
and
s_{j}=1
with
∗
, i.e.,
\psi^{s_{j}}=\psi
when
s_{j}=0
, and
\psi^{s_{j}}=\psi^{*}
when
s_{j}=1
.

(In
[
32
]
, (
4.59
) is a property proved in Proposition 6.16 rather than a definition.) Since
\tilde{\psi}
denotes an independent copy of
\psi
, note that an equivalent formulation of (
4.9
) is:

\displaystyle\Big\langle\prod_{j}(2\mathbf{i}^{(s_{j})}\sqrt{\pi}\partial^{(s_{j})}\phi(f_{j}))\Big\rangle_{SG(-\frac{4\theta}{\pi}|\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int\text{Pf}^{2}\Big[\langle\psi^{s_{i}}(z_{i})\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\rangle_{\theta}\Big]_{i,j}\prod_{j}f_{j}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}^{(s_{j})}

\displaystyle=\int\det\Big[\langle\psi^{s_{i}}(z_{i})\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\rangle_{\alpha}\Big]_{i,j}\prod_{j}f_{j}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z_{j}^{(s_{j})}

(4.60)

since fermions anticommute.

The two-point Ising correlators
\langle\psi(z_{i})\psi(z_{j})\rangle,\langle\psi(z_{i})\psi^{*}(z_{j})\rangle
are defined in Definition 6.4 in
[
32
]
and can be conveniently studied in terms of a real-fermion spinor, defined in (6.1.3) as
