f^{[\eta]}(w,z)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\bar{\eta}\langle\psi(w)\psi(z)\rangle_{\theta}+\eta\langle\psi(w)\psi^{*}(z)\rangle_{\theta}\Big)

(they denote this
f^{[\eta]}
simply by
f
).
f^{[\eta]}
is then a massive holomorphic function, in their sense (i.e., satisfying (6.0.1)), which reads:

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{w}f^{[\eta]}(w,z)=-\mathbf{i}\theta(w)\overline{f^{[\eta]}(w,z)}

(4.61)

and satisfies
f^{[\eta]}(w,z)=\bar{\eta}(w-z)^{-1}+o(|w-z|^{-1})
(note that the antiholomorphic correlation thus does not contribute to the singularity near
z=w
) as well as the boundary condition

\displaystyle\overline{f^{[\eta]}}(w,z)=\tau(w)f^{[\eta]}(w,z),w\in\partial\Omega

(4.62)

(see Definition 6.4 in
[
32
]
; note that there are no punctures in our case, i.e., the set
\{a_{1},...,a_{n}\}
is empty). By taking
\eta=1
and
\eta=\mathbf{i}
, this implies the holomorphic and antiholomorphic correlations satisfy the bulk problem, for
z\neq w\in\Omega
,

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{w}\langle\psi(w)\psi(z)\rangle_{\theta}=-\mathbf{i}\theta(w)\overline{\langle\psi(w)\psi^{*}(z)\rangle_{\theta}}\quad;\quad\bar{\partial}_{w}\langle\psi(w)\psi^{*}(z)\rangle_{\theta}=-\mathbf{i}\theta(w)\overline{\langle\psi(w)\psi(z)\rangle_{\theta}},

(4.63)

the singularity

\langle\psi(w)\psi(z)\rangle_{\theta}=2(w-z)^{-1}+o(|w-z|^{-1})\quad;\quad\langle\psi(w)\psi^{*}(z)\rangle_{\theta}=o(|w-z|^{-1}).

(4.64)

and the boundary condition for
w\in\partial\Omega

\overline{\langle\psi(w)\psi(z)\rangle_{\theta}}=\tau(w){\langle\psi(w)\psi^{*}(z)\rangle_{\theta}}.

(4.65)

Using the residue theorem, one sees that the singularity (
4.64
) contributes a Dirac term
4\pi\delta_{w}(z)
to the bulk equation (this is the exact same proof as in Equation (
4.29
)), thus the pair
(H_{0}(w,z),H_{1}(w,z))=(\langle\psi(w)\psi(z)\rangle_{\theta},\langle\psi(w)\psi(z)\rangle_{\theta})
satisfy the boundary value problem which we denote by
\mathrm{BV}_{3}(\Omega,\theta)

\begin{cases}\bar{\partial}_{w}H_{0}(w,z)=-\mathbf{i}\theta(w)\overline{H_{1}(w,z)}+4\pi\delta_{w}(z)\quad;\quad\bar{\partial}_{w}H_{1}(w,z)=-\mathbf{i}\theta(w)\overline{H_{0}(w,z)}&\text{ on }\Omega^{2}\\
\overline{H_{0}(w,z)}=\tau(w)H_{1}(w,z)&\text{ if }w\in\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

(4.66)

All in all, we obtain that

\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho)}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{j})}_{z_{j}}\phi(z_{j})\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(\rho|\Omega)}=(2\sqrt{\pi}\mathbf{i})^{-n}\prod_{j}(-1)^{s_{j}}\det\Big[\langle\psi^{s_{i}}(z_{i})\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\rangle_{\alpha}\Big]

(4.67)

where
(H_{0}(w,z),H_{1}(w,z))=(\langle\psi(w)\psi(z)\rangle_{\theta},\langle\psi(w)\psi(z)\rangle_{\theta})
satisfy the boundary value problem
\mathrm{BV}_{3}(\Omega,\theta)

We finally give a proof of Theorem
4.7
which identifies the holomorphic and antiholomorphic derivative correlation of our limiting height function with those of the sine-Gordon model.

Proof of Theorem
4.7
.

Recall that by Lemma
4.6
, the correlation kernel of the holomorphic and antiholomorphic derivatives (associated with sign
\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
) is given by

v^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\det\left(\tilde{F}^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})\right)_{i\neq j}.

Let

\displaystyle\begin{cases}H_{0}(w,z)&=-8\pi\mathbf{i}\tilde{F}_{0}(w,z)\\
H_{1}(w,z)&=-8\pi\mathbf{i}\overline{\tilde{F}_{1}}(w,z),\end{cases}

(4.68)

The first equation of
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\alpha)
implies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\overline{\partial}_{w}H_{0}(w,z)&=-8\pi\mathbf{i}\overline{\partial}_{w}\tilde{F}_{0}(w,z)=-\frac{\tilde{\alpha}(w)}{2}\overline{H_{1}}(w,z)+4\pi\delta_{z}(w)\\
\overline{\partial}_{w}F_{0,1}(w,z)&=-8\pi\mathbf{i}\overline{\partial}_{w}\overline{\tilde{F}_{1}}(w,z)=-\frac{\tilde{\alpha}(w)}{2}\overline{F_{0,0}}(w,z)\end{cases}

This is exactly the first equation of
\mathrm{BV}_{3}(\Omega,\theta)
if we take
\theta=\tilde{\alpha}/{2\mathbf{i}}
. The boundary values for
(\tilde{F}_{0},\tilde{F}_{1})
in
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
imply

H_{1}(w,z)=-8\pi\mathbf{i}\overline{\tilde{F}_{1}}(w,z)=8\pi\mathbf{i}\bar{\tau}(w)\overline{\tilde{F}_{0}}(w,z)=\bar{\tau}(w)\overline{H_{0}}(w,z);\ w\in\partial\Omega.

We have proved that
(\tilde{F}_{0},\tilde{F}_{1})
satisfies
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
if and only if
(H_{0}(w,z),H_{1}(w,z))
verifies the boundary value problem
\mathrm{BV}_{3}(\Omega,\theta)
for
\theta=\tilde{\alpha}/{2\mathbf{i}}
(the proof of the ”if” part of the statement is exactly the same).
Thus, when
(\tilde{F}_{0},\tilde{F}_{1})
satisfies
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
and the solution to
\mathrm{BV}_{2}(\Omega,\tilde{\alpha})
is unique as in the statement of Theorem
4.7
,

\Big(H_{0}(w,z),H_{1}(w,z)\Big)=\Big(\langle\psi(w)\psi(z)\rangle_{\theta},\langle\psi(w)\psi^{*}(z)\rangle_{\theta}\Big).

(4.69)

It remains to connect the entries of the matrix describing the correlation kernel
v^{(\mathbf{s})}
to those of the sine-Gordon model
\chi^{(\mathbf{s})}
. To this end, let
G_{s}(w,z)=(-1)^{s}\mathbf{i}\tilde{F}_{s}(w,z)
. Then

G^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}=(-1)^{s_{i}+s_{j}}\mathbf{i}^{(s_{j})}\tilde{F}^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}=(-1)^{s_{i}}\mathbf{i}\tilde{F}^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}

so the matrix with entries given by the left hand side differ by a factor
\mathbf{i}
and an additional factor
(-1)
for each line where
s_{i}=1
. Hence

\displaystyle\det\left(\tilde{F}^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})\right)_{i\neq j}

\displaystyle=(-\mathbf{i})^{n}\det\left(G^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})\right)_{i\neq j}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{s_{i}}

(4.70)

Now observe that

G^{(s_{j})}_{s_{i}+s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})=(-8\pi)^{-1}H_{s_{i}+s_{j}}^{(s_{i})}(z_{i},z_{j})=(-8\pi)^{-1}\langle\psi^{s_{i}}(z_{i}),\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\rangle.

The first equation is easily verified for
s_{i}=0
,
s_{j}=0
or
s_{j}=1
by (
4.68
), and follows for
s_{i}=1
and
s_{j}=0,1
by conjugation.
The second equation follows from Equation (
4.69
) in the cases
s_{i}=0
and by conjugation for the cases
s_{i}=1.

Consequently,

\displaystyle v^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{i}/8\pi)^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{s_{i}}\det\Big(\langle\psi^{s_{i}}(z_{i}),\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\rangle\Big)_{i\neq j}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{i}/8\pi)^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{s_{i}}\cdot\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}(2\mathbf{i}^{(s_{j})}\sqrt{\pi}\partial^{(s_{j})}\phi(z_{j}))\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(-\frac{4\theta}{\pi}|\Omega)}

(4.71)

\displaystyle=\left(-4\sqrt{\pi}\right)^{-n}\Big\langle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial^{(s_{j})}\phi(z_{j}))\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(-\frac{4\theta}{\pi}|\Omega)}

(4.72)

where in (
4.71
) we used the relation between the fermionic correlations and the sine-Gordon model (
4.60
). We also used the fact that the function
\theta
does not need to be compactly supported (as mentioned in (
1.41
)). We briefly provide a justification for this last fact below.
Altogether, this proves (
4.72
) and thus concludes the proof of Theorem
1.19
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
be the unit disc and let
\alpha:\bar{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be smooth up to and including the boundary (
\alpha\in C^{\infty}(\bar{\Omega}))
. Fix signs
(s_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and some test functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}
such that
\partial^{(s_{j})}f_{j}
have disjoint support. Then

\displaystyle\Big\langle\prod_{j}(2\mathbf{i}^{(s_{j})}\sqrt{\pi}\partial^{(s_{j})}\phi(f_{j}))\Big\rangle_{\mathrm{SG}(-\frac{4\theta}{\pi}|\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\int\det\Big[\langle\psi^{s_{i}}(z_{i})\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\rangle_{\alpha}\Big]_{i,j}\prod_{j}f_{j}(z_{j})\mathrm{d}z

(4.73)

Proof.

This follows by checking the convergence of the right hand side in (
4.9
). This is already done in Lemma 6.39 in
[
32
]
, namely, their results imply that for
\kappa_{b}>0
fixed, if

\displaystyle\text{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)^{2}|\nabla\alpha_{N}(z)|\leq\kappa_{b},

\displaystyle\text{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)^{2}|\nabla\alpha(z)|\leq\kappa_{b}

(4.74)

and
\alpha_{N}\rightarrow\alpha
pointwise, then the two-point Ising correlators converge. The convergence of the integrals follows from the fact that the
f_{i}
have disjoint support and uniform bounds and the massive holomorphicity (resp. antiholomorphicity) of the fermionic correlators.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

Note that a consequence of the identification (
4.69
) is that
F_{0}(w,z)=-F_{0}(z,w)
and
F_{1}(w,z)=-\bar{F}_{1}(z,w)
, since fermions anticommute and since
\overline{\langle\psi(w),\psi^{*}(z)\rangle}=\langle\psi^{*}(w),\psi(z)\rangle
. This is by no means obvious from the definition of
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
.

4.7
Fredholm regularised determinant formula (proof of Theorem
1.21
)

We include here a few brief reminders concerning such regularised determinants as well as the related notion of
p
-th Schatten class, and refer to the book by Barry Simon
[
38
, Chapter 5]
for more details. The idea is probably more familiar in the context of trace-class operators, so we start with this. Roughly, the idea is the following. Let
H
be a Hilbert space and let
A:H\to H
be a positive, bounded linear operator. The trace of
A
is then the (possibly infinite) series
\Tr(A)=\sum_{n}\langle Ae_{n},e_{n}\rangle
for a choice of orthonormal basis
(e_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of
H
(the convergence and value of the trace does not depend on the choice of orthonormal basis).
Let
K:H\to H
be a bounded operator, not necessarily positive. We say that
K
is
trace-class
if the series
\Tr(|K|)<\infty
, where
|K|=\sqrt{K^{*}K}
. When
K
is trace-class, one can check that the
K^{2},K^{3},\ldots
are all trace class operators, with
|\Tr(K^{n})|\leq\Tr(|K|)^{n}
. This entails that the power series
\sum_{n\geq 1}(-1)^{n}\mu^{n}\Tr(K^{n})/n
has positive radius of convergence. By definition, we set

\det_{1}(I+\mu K)=\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n+1}\frac{\mu^{n}\Tr(K^{n})}{n}\right)

the Fredholm-regularised determinant of
I+\mu K
(for
\mu
small enough). (This generalizes the well-known expression of the log of a determinant
I+A
for a finite-dimensional matrix
A
).

Now let
p\geq 1
be an integer. We say that
K
lies in the Schatten class
S_{p}
of order
p
if
\|K\|_{p}:=\Tr[(\sqrt{K^{*}K})^{p}]^{1/p}<\infty
. In particular,
K\in S_{1}
if and only if
K
is trace-class. Also,
K\in S_{4}
if and only if
\Tr[(K^{*}K)^{2}]<\infty
. The condition
K\in S_{p}
is weaker than
K\in S_{1}
if
p\geq 1
: that is,
S_{1}\subset S_{p}
. However, even if we do not assume that
K
is trace-class but only
K\in S_{p}
we can still make sense of the Fredholm regularized determinant
\det_{p}(I+\mu K)
of order
p
(for
\mu
small), namely via the formula

\det_{p}(I+\mu K)=\exp\Big(\sum_{n\geq p}\frac{(-\mu)^{n+1}}{n}\text{Tr}(K)^{n}\Big).

(4.75)

Recall our formula for the moments of the limiting height field given in Corollary
4.7
, which concretely boils down to (
4.36
). For convenience, this reads

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}[(\phi,f)^{n}]=\int\ldots\int f(z_{1})\ldots f(z_{n})V_{(\Omega,\alpha)}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\mathrm{d}A(z_{1})\ldots\mathrm{d}A(z_{n})

(4.76)

where

\displaystyle V(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=V_{\Omega;\alpha}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\int_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots\int_{\gamma_{n}}v^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}.

(4.77)

and

v^{(\mathbf{s})}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{i}^{-1+2s_{i}}\det\Big((\not{\partial}_{\alpha})_{2-s_{i},1+s_{j}}^{-1}(z_{i},z_{j})\Big)_{i\neq j}.

(4.78)

First, we need a trace formula for the cumulants. By the moments-to-cumulants map (see
[
34
, (3.27)]
)

\displaystyle\Big<\phi(f);...;\phi(f)\Big>_{(\Omega,\alpha)}=\sum_{\pi}(|\pi|-1)!(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\prod_{B\in\pi}\mathbb{E}_{(\Omega,\alpha)}[(\phi,f)^{|B|}].

(4.79)

Since the moments are determinantal, we obtain an expression for the cumulants by expanding the determinant as a sum over permutations; only the cyclic permutations remain. This yields the following expression for the cumulants:

Lemma 4.12
.

The cumulants satisfy

\displaystyle\Big\langle\phi(f);...;\phi(f)\Big\rangle_{(\Omega,\alpha)}

\displaystyle=(n-1)!(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{s_{1},...,s_{n}}\int_{\Omega^{n}}dxf(x_{1})...f(x_{n})\int_{\gamma(x_{1})}...\int_{\gamma(x_{n})}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\langle\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\psi^{s_{j+1}}(z_{j+1})\rangle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}z_{j}^{(s_{j})}.

where
\langle\psi^{s}(z)\psi^{r}(w)\rangle:=\mathbf{i}^{-1+2s}(\not{\partial}_{\alpha})_{2-s,1+r}^{-1}(z,w)
.

Proof.

Insert (
4.76
) into (
4.79
) to get

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1});...;\phi_{\varepsilon}(x_{n})\rangle=\sum_{s_{1},...,s_{n}}\int_{\gamma_{1}}...\int_{\gamma_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}z_{j}^{s_{j}}\sum_{\pi}(|\pi|-1)!(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\prod_{B\in\pi}\det\Big(K^{s_{i},s_{j}}(z_{i},z_{j})\Big)_{i,j\in B}

(4.80)

Expanding the determinant,

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi}(|\pi|-1)!(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\prod_{B\in\pi}\sum_{\sigma_{B}\in S_{B}}\text{sgn}(\sigma_{B})\prod_{j\in B}K^{s_{j},s_{\sigma_{B}(j)}}(x_{j},x_{\sigma_{B}(j)})

(4.81)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\text{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{j=1}^{n}K^{s_{j},s_{\sigma(j)}}(x_{j},x_{\sigma(j)})\sum_{\pi}\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma=\prod_{B\in\pi}\sigma_{B}}(|\pi|-1)!(-1)^{|\pi|-1}

(4.82)

where we used that
\text{sgn}(\prod_{B\in\pi}\sigma_{B})=\prod_{B\in\pi}\text{sgn}(\sigma_{B})
. Now recall that any
\sigma
has a unique cycle decomposition, and the condition that
\sigma=\prod_{B\in\pi}\sigma_{B}
is the condition that each cycle
c
of
\sigma
lies in a block
B
of
\pi
, in the sense that
\mathrm{Im}(c)\subset B
. If
|\pi|=r
and there are
k
cycles in
\sigma
, note that the number of ways to partition
k
distinct objects (the cycles) into
r
subsets (the blocks of
\pi
) is precisely the Stirling number of the second kind,
S(k,r)
. From the combinatorial identity

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{k}S(k,r)(r-1)!(-1)^{r-1}=\delta_{k,1}

(4.83)

(where
\delta
here is the Kronecker delta) we thus deduce:

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\Big\langle\phi_{\varepsilon}(x_{1});...;\phi_{\varepsilon}(x_{n})\Big\rangle_{(\Omega,\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1},...,s_{n}}\int_{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}}\sum_{\sigma}\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\langle\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\psi^{s_{\sigma(j)}}(z_{\sigma(j)})\rangle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}z^{(s_{j})},

where the sum is over all
cyclic
permutations.
In particular,
\text{sgn}(\sigma)=(-1)^{n+1}
. Furthermore, all cyclic permutations contribute the same:
indeed for a given cyclic permutation
\sigma=(i_{1}...i_{n})
, where
i_{j}
are all distinct, consider the permutation
\pi
such that
\pi(i_{j})=j
. Now relabel the variables
s^{\prime}_{\pi(j)}=s_{j},x^{\prime}_{\pi(j)}=x_{j},z^{\prime}_{\pi(j)}=z_{j}
, observe that

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\langle\psi^{s_{j}}(z_{j})\psi^{s_{\sigma(j)}}(z_{\sigma(j)})\rangle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\langle\psi^{s_{\pi^{-1}(j)}}(z_{\pi^{-1}(j)})\psi^{s_{\sigma(\pi^{-1}(j))}}(z_{\sigma(\pi^{-1}(j))})\rangle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\langle\psi^{s^{\prime}_{j}}(z^{\prime}_{j})\psi^{s^{\prime}_{j+1}}(z^{\prime}_{j+1})\rangle

(4.84)

where the first equality holds by commutativity and the second equality holds since
\pi(\sigma(\pi^{-1}(j)))=\pi(\sigma(i_{j}))=\pi(i_{j+1})=j+1
. Hence, each term in the sum over
\sigma
is equal, and since there are
(n-1)!
cyclic permutations we deduce the lemma.
∎

We obtain

\displaystyle\Big<\phi(f);...;\phi(f)\Big>_{(\Omega,\alpha)}=(n-1)!\sum_{s}\int_{\Omega^{n}\times(\prod_{i}\gamma_{i})}\prod_{i}f(x_{i})\prod_{i}\mathbf{i}^{-1+2s_{i}}(\not{\partial}_{\alpha})_{2-s_{i},1+s_{i+1}}^{-1}(z_{i},z_{i+1})\prod_{i}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}.

(4.85)

If we parametrise
z_{i}=\gamma(x_{i})(t_{i})
, so that
\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{(s_{i})}=\dot{\gamma}(x_{i})^{(s_{i})}(t_{i})\mathrm{d}t_{i}
we get

\displaystyle(n-1)!\sum_{s}\int_{\Omega^{n}\times(\times_{i}\gamma_{i})}\prod_{i}f(x_{i})\prod_{i}K_{s_{i},s_{i+1}}((x_{i},t_{i});(x_{i+1},t_{i+1}))\prod_{i}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\mathrm{d}t_{i}

(4.86)

\displaystyle=(n-1)!\Tr((fK)^{n})

(4.87)

where

\displaystyle K_{r,s}((x,t),(y,t^{\prime}))=\mathbf{i}^{-1+2r}(\not{\partial}_{\alpha})^{-1}_{2-r,1+s}(\gamma(x)(t),\gamma(y)(t^{\prime}))\dot{\gamma}(x)^{(r)}(t)

(4.88)

defines an operator
K:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,1])\oplus L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,1]).

(4.89)

Note in the above that we have been somewhat imprecise in how we select the paths so that the operator
K
is truly the same for each
n
. We select these paths as follows, in the case that
\Omega
is the unit disc
\mathbb{D}
, define the paths with endpoint at
x\in\Omega
as the horizontal path
(\sqrt{1-x_{2}^{2}}(1-t)+tx_{1},x_{2}),

t\in[0,1]
. These paths are disjoint whenever the second coordinate
x_{2}
of
x
differs.

Note that

\displaystyle B_{n}=\{(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in\Omega^{n}:\exists i\neq j\text{ with }\Im(x_{i})=\Im(x_{j})\}

(4.90)

has Lebesgue measure zero, or in words, the set of points where there is some pair
(x_{i},x_{j})
on the same horizontal line is measure zero. On
\Omega^{n}\setminus B_{n}
, the
n
paths
\gamma(x_{i})
are disjoint, hence removing
B_{n}
from the integral in (
4.86
) allows (
4.87
).

If instead
\Omega
is an arbitrary
C^{1}
Jordan domain, there is a conformal map
\Phi
from the unit disc to
\Omega
, and so we select the path at
x\in\Omega
as the image of the horizontal path in the disc originating at
\Phi^{-1}(x)
. Since the image of
B_{n}
under
\Phi
is measure zero in
\Omega,
similar to before, we can remove the set
\Phi(B_{n})
from the integral in (
4.86
) which gives (
4.87
).

Proof of Theorem
1.21
.

We first show that
K
lies in the fourth Schatten class
S_{4}=\{L:\text{Tr}[(L^{*}L)^{2}]<\infty\}
(recall the discussion above (
4.75
) and in
[
38
, Chapter 5]
).
In particular, this gives convergence of the series

\displaystyle\sum_{n=4}^{\infty}\frac{\mu^{n}}{n}\text{Tr}[(fK)^{n}]<\infty

(4.91)

for
|\mu|
sufficiently small.

From the definition of
K
in (
4.88
), the discussion in section 4.1, (
4.53
), the definition of
F_{0},F_{1}
in terms of
\kappa,\kappa^{*}
(
1.27
) and the near-diagonal estimates for
\kappa,\kappa^{*}
(
4.1
) we have the estimate

\displaystyle|K^{r,r^{\prime}}((x,s),(y,t))|\leq\frac{C_{\Omega}|\dot{\gamma}(x)^{r}(s)|}{|\gamma(x)(s)-\gamma(y)(t)|}\leq\frac{C}{|\gamma(x)(s)-\gamma(y)(t)|}

(4.92)

where we used that
\Omega
is a
C^{1}
Jordan domain for the boundedness of
\dot{\gamma}.
Recall above that we defined the paths in
\Omega
by fixing a conformal map
\Phi
from the unit disc to
\Omega
, then the path originating from
z\in\Omega
was defined as the image of the horizontal line segment in the disc from
\Phi^{-1}(z)
to the boundary.
We want to show that

\displaystyle\textnormal{Tr}[(K^{*}K)^{2}]<\infty

(4.93)

where

\displaystyle\text{Tr}[(K^{*}K)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\{\pm\}}\int_{\Omega\times[0,1]}[K^{*}K\cdot K^{*}K]^{\xi,\xi}(y,y)\mathrm{d}y

(4.94)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s,u,r_{1},r_{2}\in\{\pm\}}\int_{(\Omega\times[0,1])^{4}}\overline{K^{r_{1},s}(\mathbf{y_{1}};\mathbf{x})}K^{r_{1},u}(\mathbf{y_{1}};\mathbf{z})\overline{K^{r_{2},u}(\mathbf{y_{2}};\mathbf{z})}K^{r_{2},s}(\mathbf{y_{2}};\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y_{1}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y_{2}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

(4.95)

where
\mathbf{y}_{1}=(y,t)
,
y\in\Omega,t\in[0,1]
, etc. Observe that it is sufficient that the kernel
K^{*}K(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is square-integrable on
(\Omega\times[0,1])^{2}
,

\displaystyle\int_{(\Omega\times[0,1])^{2}}|K^{*}K^{r,r^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}<\infty.

(4.96)

Hence, using (
4.92
) and Fubini, (
4.93
) can be deduced by showing the function on the right hand side of

\displaystyle|K^{*}K^{r,r^{\prime}}((x,s),(y,t))|\leq\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{C^{2}}{|\gamma(x)(s)-\gamma(z)(\tau)||\gamma(y)(t)-\gamma(z)(\tau)|}\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}z

(4.97)

is square-integrable.

Now, we use the conformal map
\Phi:\mathbb{D}\rightarrow\Omega
and our assumption that
\partial\Omega
is Dini-smooth which implies (see
[
35
, Theorem 3.5]
) that
\Phi^{\prime}
extends continuously to
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
and satisfies
\Phi^{\prime}(z)\neq 0
for all
z\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}
. We see

\displaystyle|\Phi^{-1}(u)-\Phi^{-1}(v)|=|\int_{u}^{v}(\Phi^{-1})^{\prime}(z)dz|\leq\sup|(\Phi^{-1})^{\prime}(z)|\text{len(path $u\rightarrow v$)}|\leq C^{\prime}|u-v|

(4.98)

for
u,v\in\Omega
, where we used that
|(\Phi^{-1})^{\prime}(z)|=|1/\Phi^{\prime}(\Phi(z))|
is bounded in the last inequality. Hence

\displaystyle|z-w|/C^{\prime}\leq|\Phi(z)-\Phi(w)|

(4.99)

for
z,w\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}
. Make the change of variable
z=\Phi(\xi)
in the integral in (
4.97
), we see

\displaystyle|K^{*}K^{r,r^{\prime}}((x,s),(y,t))|

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\int_{\overline{\mathbb{D}}}\mathrm{d}\xi|\Phi^{\prime}(\xi)|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\tau\frac{1}{|\gamma(x)(s)-\gamma(\Phi(\xi))(\tau)||\gamma(y)(t)-\gamma(\Phi(\xi))(\tau)|}

(4.100)

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}C^{\prime 2}C^{\prime\prime 2}\int_{\overline{\mathbb{D}}}\mathrm{d}\xi\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\tau\frac{1}{|a-\tilde{\gamma}(\xi)(\tau)||b-\tilde{\gamma}(\xi)(\tau)|}

(4.101)

where in the previous inequality, we set
a=\Phi^{-1}(\gamma(x)(s)),b=\Phi^{-1}(\gamma(y)(t))
,
\tilde{\gamma}
is the horizontal path in
\mathbb{D}
such that
\Phi(\tilde{\gamma}(\xi)(\tau))=\gamma(\Phi(\xi))(\tau)
, and we used the bounds
|\Phi^{\prime}|\leq C^{\prime\prime}
and (
4.99
). Use Fubini on the integral (
4.101
) then observe that for a fixed
\tau
,
\tilde{\gamma}(\xi)(\tau),\xi\in\mathbb{D}
, is just a horizontal shift of the points in the set
\mathbb{D},
i.e.
\tilde{\gamma}(\xi)(\tau)=\xi+f(\tau)
for some function
f
. Using the well-known bound

\displaystyle\int_{K}\mathrm{d}z\frac{1}{|z-x|}\frac{1}{|z-y|}\leq C(1+|\log|x-y||)

(4.102)

for any compact set
K
, we deduce

\displaystyle|K^{*}K^{r,r^{\prime}}((x,s),(y,t))|\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime}(1+|\log|a-b||)\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(1+|\log|\gamma(x)(s)-\gamma(y)(t)||)

(4.103)

where we used (
4.98
) again. Inserting this bound into the left hand side of (
4.96
), using Fubini to first integrate over either
x
or
y
, we see the resulting bound is finite since both
\log
and
\log^{2}
are integrable.

Hence we have proved
(
4.93
).
∎

Appendix A
Control of the non-massive discrete Green function and its derivatives for nearby points.

In this appendix, we recall classical bounds on the behaviour of the discrete non-massive Green function and its discrete derivatives near the diagonal.
These bounds can be found in
[
21
]
(square lattice case), and could probably be extracted from the proof of
[
26
]
in the isoradial case.
We provide proofs for completeness.

Recall that when
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L=\Omega\cap B
with
B=(\beta^{*},\eta)
, we denote by
\varphi
the reflection along
L
.
Recall that the discrete Green function
G_{\#}
can be extended to
\Gamma_{\varphi}=\Gamma\cup(\Gamma^{\infty}\cap B)
by the discrete Schwarz principle.
This is also true for the continuous Green function, which can be extended by the continuous Schwarz principle to
\Omega_{\varphi}=\Omega\cup B
.

Lemma A.1
.

Uniformly for
x_{1}
in compact subsets of
\Omega
,
x_{2}\in\Gamma
,

G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2})=O\left(1+|\log|x_{1}-x_{2}||\right).

If
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary near
\beta^{*}
(say on the ball
B=B(\beta^{*},\eta)
, so that
L=\partial\Omega\cap B
is straight, recall that
\Gamma_{\varphi}=\Gamma\cup\varphi(\Gamma\cap B)
and
\Omega_{\varphi}=\Omega\cup B
, where
\varphi
is the reflection across the straight line
L
defined in Section
2.3
.
Recall that in this case,
G_{\#}
is naturally extended to
\Gamma_{\varphi}
.
Then, uniformly for
x_{1}
in compact subsets of
\Omega_{\varphi}
,
x_{2}\in\Gamma_{\varphi}
,

G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2})=O\left(1+|\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|\wedge|\varphi(x_{1})-x_{2}|)|\right).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega
be a compact subset.
Recall that
G_{\#,\infty}
denotes the non-massive Green function on
\Gamma^{\infty}
, which we introduced in Definition (
2.6
).
For
x_{1},x_{2}\in\Gamma
, let

H(x_{1},\cdot)=G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},\cdot)-G_{\#}(x_{1},\cdot).

(A.1)

By definition of
G_{\#,\infty}
and
G_{\#}
,
H(x_{1},\cdot)
is harmonic in
\Gamma
and coincides with
G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},\cdot)
on
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma
.
On the one hand, Equation (
2.13
) implies that
H(x_{1},\cdot)=O(1)
uniformly for
x_{1}\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{C}
,
x_{2}\in\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma
, since
\dist(\mathcal{C},\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma)>0
.
Hence by the maximum principle this remains true on all
\Gamma
.
On the other hand, Equation (
2.13
) implies that
G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2})=O(1+|\log|x_{1}-x_{2}||)
, uniformly for
x_{1},x_{2}\in\Gamma^{\infty}
.
The result follows.

When
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
, there is a classical improvement of this argument due to Kenyon
[
21
]
(see the proof of his Corollary 19 for the square lattice case).
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\subset\Omega_{\varphi}
be a compact subset.
Recall that
\Gamma^{\infty}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
is symmetric along
L
, and that
\varphi
denotes the symmetry along
L
.

Since
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus B(\beta^{*},\eta)
is a compact subset of
\Omega
, we can apply the first point to obtain that
G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2})=O(1+|\log|x_{1}-x_{2}||)
uniformly for
x_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus B(\beta^{*},\eta)
and
x_{2}\in\Gamma
.
Since
G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2})=G_{\#}(x_{1},\varphi(x_{2}))
for
x_{2}\in B(\beta^{*},\eta)
, we obtain that
G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2})=O(1+|\log(|x_{1}-x_{2}|\wedge|x_{1}-\varphi(x_{2})||)
uniformly for
x_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus B(\beta^{*},\eta)
and
x_{2}\in\Gamma\cup B(\beta^{*},\eta)
.

For
x_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
,
x_{2}\in\Gamma
, let

\tilde{H}(x_{1},x_{2})=G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2})-G_{\#,\infty}(\varphi(x_{1}),x_{2})-G_{\#}(x_{1},x_{2}).

(A.2)

By definition of
G_{\#}
and
G_{\#,\infty}
,
\tilde{H}(x_{1},\cdot)
is harmonic in
\Gamma
and coincides with
G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},\cdot)-G_{\#,\infty}(\varphi(x_{1}),\cdot)
on
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma
.
By symmetry of
\Gamma^{\infty}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
and locality of the discrete free Green function (see Remark
2.7
and before),
\tilde{H}
vanishes on
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma\cap L
.
Hence, Equation (
2.13
) implies that
H(x_{1},x_{2})
is
O(1)
uniformly for
x_{1}\in\Gamma\cap\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
and
x_{2}\in\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma
since
\dist(\tilde{\mathcal{C}},\partial\Omega\setminus L)>0
.
By the maximum principle this remains true for all
x_{2}\in\Gamma
.
On the other hand, Equation (
2.13
) implies that
G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2})=O(1+\log|x_{1}-x_{2}|)
uniformly for
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
which concludes the proof.
∎

The same argument can be applied to bound the derivative of the discrete function with Dirichlet boundary conditions. We first need a bound on the derivative of the discrete free Green function, which is a direct consequence of the asymptotic (
2.13
):

Lemma A.2
.

Uniformly in
x_{1},w_{2},\varepsilon
and the isoradial graph
\Gamma^{\infty}
,

G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2}^{+})-G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2}^{-})=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|}\right)

Remark A.3
.

Note that this is much better than what we could obtain using Lemma
2.12
with
r=|x_{1}-w_{2}|/2
and Equation (
2.13
):

G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2}^{+})-G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},x_{2}^{-})=O\left(\frac{\varepsilon\log(|x_{1}-w_{2}|)}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|}\right)

We are now in position to bound the derivative of the discrete non-massive Green function.

Lemma A.4
.

Uniformly for
x_{1},w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega
,

(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(x_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|}\right).

If
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
, uniformly for
x_{1},w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega_{\varphi}
,

(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(x_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{1}-w_{2}|\wedge|\varphi(x_{1})-w_{2}|}\right).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega
be a compact subset. Taking the discrete derivative of Equation (
A.1
), we obtain that

(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(x_{1},w_{2})=(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},w_{2})-(\partial_{2})_{\#}H(x_{1},w_{2}).

where
H(x_{1},\cdot):\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
is harmonic in
\Gamma
and coincides with
G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},\cdot)
on
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma
.
As before, by Equation (
2.13
),
H(x_{1},x_{2})
is uniformly bounded for
x_{1}\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{C},x_{2}\in\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma
so by the maximum principle, it is uniformly bounded for
x_{1}\in\mathcal{C}\cap\Gamma
,
x_{2}\in\Gamma
.
By Lemma
2.12
, uniformly for
x_{1}\in\mathcal{C}
,
w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}

(\partial_{2})_{\#}H(x_{1},w_{2})=O(1).

Together with Lemma
A.2
, this proves the lemma.

Assume now that
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
and
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\subset\Omega_{\varphi}
is a compact subset.
Let also
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{D}}\subset\mathcal{D}\subset\Omega_{\varphi}
be a compact subset.

If
x_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
, we apply the same improvement as in Lemma
A.1
.
Taking the discrete derivative of Equation (
A.2
), we obtain for all
x_{1},w_{2}\in\Omega_{\varphi}
,

(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(x_{1},w_{2})=(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#,\infty}(x_{1},w_{2})-(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#,\infty}(\varphi(x_{1}),w_{2})-(\partial_{2})_{\#}\tilde{H}(x_{1},w_{2}).

By symmetry of
\Gamma^{\infty}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
and locality of the discrete free Green function (see Section
2.4
),
\tilde{H}
vanishes on
L
.
Together with Equation (
2.13
), it implies that
\tilde{H}
is uniformly bounded for
x_{1}\in\Gamma\cap\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
,
x_{2}\in\partial_{\mathrm{out}}\Gamma
.
By the maximum principle,
\tilde{H}
is uniformly bounded for
x_{1}\in\Gamma\cap\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
,
x_{2}\in\Gamma
.
Since
\tilde{H}(x_{1},\cdot)
vanishes on
L
, it can be extended to
\Gamma_{\varphi}
by the discrete Schwarz reflection principle.
The extended function is uniformly bounded for
x_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
,
x_{2}\in\Gamma_{\varphi}
.
Hence by Lemma
2.12
,
(\partial_{2})_{\#}\tilde{H}(x_{1},w_{2})
is uniformly bounded for
x_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta),w_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
.
Together with Lemma
A.2
, this concludes the proof when
x_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta),x_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
.

There is still a small technical detail to fix, that is when
x_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus B(\beta^{*},\eta)
,
x_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\cap B(\beta^{*},\eta)
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus B(\beta^{*},\eta)\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}}\subset\mathcal{C}\subset\Omega
be a compact subset.
If
x_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus B(\beta^{*},\eta)\subset\mathcal{C},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
, the result holds by the first point.
If
x_{1}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus B(\beta^{*},\eta),w_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\mathcal{C}
, the result holds by the second point and the maximum principle since
x_{1}\to G_{\#}(x_{1},w_{2})
is harmonic in
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus B(\beta^{*},\eta)
and
\dist(\partial(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus B(\beta^{*},\eta)),\tilde{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\mathcal{C})>0
.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega
,

(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(w_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}}\right).

If
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
, uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}
in compact subsets of
\Omega_{\varphi}
,

(\partial_{1})_{\#}(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(w_{1},w_{2})=O\left(\frac{1}{|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}\wedge|\varphi(w_{1})-w_{2}|^{2}}\right).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{D}}\subset\mathcal{D}\subset\Omega
be two compact subsets,
d=\dist(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}^{c})>0
.
Let
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
.
The function
x_{1}\to(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(x_{1},w_{2})
is harmonic on
B(w_{1},d/2\wedge|x_{1}-w_{2}|/2)
, and it is
O(1/|w_{1}-w_{2}|)
uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
,
x_{1}\in B(w_{1},d/2\wedge|x_{1}-w_{2}|/2)
, by Lemma
A.4
.
Applying Lemma
2.12
(which works in particular when
m=0
) to the ball
B(w_{1},r+R)
with
r=d/4\wedge|x_{1}-w_{2}|/4,R=2r
gives the lemma.

If
\Omega
and
\Gamma
have a straight boundary
L
, let
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{D}}\subset\mathcal{D}\subset\Omega_{\varphi}
be compact subsets. Let
w_{1},w_{2}
and

d=\dist(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}^{c})
.
The function
x_{1}\to(\partial_{2})_{\#}G_{\#}(x_{1},w_{2})
can be extended by the discrete Schwarz principle to
\Gamma_{\varphi}
.
The extended function is harmonic on
B(w_{1},d/2\wedge|w_{1}-w_{2}|/2\wedge|\varphi(w_{1})-w_{2}|/2)
and it is
O(1/(|w_{1}-w_{2}|\wedge|\varphi(w_{1})-w_{2}|)
uniformly for
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
and
x_{1}\in B(w_{1},d/2\wedge|w_{1}-w_{2}|/2\wedge|\varphi(w_{1})-w_{2}|/2)
by Lemma
A.4
.
Applying Lemma
2.12
(in the
m=0
case is just Corollary 2.9 in
[
12
]
) to the ball
B(w_{1},r+R)
with
r=d/4\wedge|w_{1}-w_{2}|/4\wedge|\varphi(w_{1})-w_{2}|/4,R=2r
gives the lemma.
∎

Appendix B
Elementary technical results on functions of two variables

In this appendix, we prove an elementary result on functions of two variables.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}
be a compact set of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Let
C_{1}>0
and
\phi,\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be two continuous increasing functions such that
\phi(0)=\psi(0)=0
.
Let
f:\mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function satisfying

\displaystyle\forall(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{C}_{1},|f(x_{1},x_{2})|\leq C_{1}

\displaystyle\forall(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{C}_{1},|f(x_{1},x_{2})-f(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})|\leq C_{1}\phi(|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|)

\displaystyle\forall(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{C}_{1},|f(x_{1},x_{2})-f(x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime})|\leq C_{1}\psi(|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|).

Then, for all compact subset
\mathcal{C}_{2}\subset\mathring{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
there exists
C_{2}>0
such that

\forall(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{2},|f(x_{1},x_{2})-f(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})|\leq C_{2}\phi(|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|+\psi(|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|)).

Proof.

Let
d=\dist(\mathcal{C}^{2},\mathcal{C}_{1}^{c})
.
For all
(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
such that
|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|\geq d/2
, then

|f(x_{1},x_{2})-f(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})|\leq 2C_{1}\leq\frac{2C_{1}}{\phi(d/2)}\phi(|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|)

For all
(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
such that
|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|\leq d/2
,
(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
so

\displaystyle|f(x_{1},x_{2})-f(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq|f(x_{1},x_{2})-f(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})|+|f(x_{1},x_{2})-f(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\phi(|x_{1}-x_{1}^{\prime}|)+C_{1}\psi(|x_{2}-x_{2}^{\prime}|).

Hence the lemma holds with
C_{2}=\max(C_{1},2C_{1}/\phi(d/2))
.
∎

Appendix C
Heuristic for identifying the limiting height field with the sine-Gordon model

C.1
Coleman’s correspondence and sine-Gordon model with external field

The goal of this subsection is to give a heuristic argument that the correlations describing the limiting height function in Theorem
1.14
can be identified with the sine-Gordon model via Coleman’s transform, for a generic domain. Note that we only prove this in the case of unit disc, using the construction of the correlation functions the sine-Gordon field given in
[
32
]
.

Under
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{SG}(\alpha)}
,
\varphi
has formal density

\displaystyle\exp\Big[-\int_{\Omega}2\partial\varphi(x)\bar{\partial}\varphi(x)+z_{0}\big\langle e^{-\mathbf{i}\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi},\tilde{\alpha}(x)\big\rangle\ \mathrm{d}A(x)\Big],

(C.1)

which we rewrite when
z_{0}=-2
as

\displaystyle\exp\Big[-2\int_{\Omega}\partial\varphi(x)\bar{\partial}\varphi(x)-\int_{\Omega}\tilde{\alpha}\ e^{\mathbf{i}\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi}+\overline{\tilde{\alpha}}\ e^{-\mathbf{i}\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi}\ \mathrm{d}A(x)\Big]

(C.2)

In
[
15
]
, Coleman observed the formal equivalence of the sine-Gordon field with the massive Thirring model. We recall from (
4.46
) that in this model the “spins” are fermionic variables
(\psi(z))_{z\in\Omega}
where for each
z
,
\psi(z)=(\psi_{1}(z),\psi_{2}(z))
is a vector of size two, whose formal density is given by

\exp\Big[-\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{d}A(x)\ \big(\bar{\psi}\not{\partial}\psi+\tilde{\alpha}\bar{\psi}_{1}\psi_{1}+\overline{\tilde{\alpha}}\bar{\psi}_{2}\psi_{2}\big)\Big].

(C.3)

Once again we refer to
[
5
]
for a rigorous construction. Note the surprising feature that in this correspondence, the nonlinear potential
\cos(\sqrt{4\pi\varphi)}
is transformed into the above quadratic form, which boils down to simply adding a mass term to the field and is thus in a certain sense ‘trivial’.

To explain his idea, Coleman started from the observation that the massless GFF and the massless free fermion “measure” are equivalent in the sense that the following observables have the same correlations:

\displaystyle\!:\!e^{\mathbf{i}\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi}\!:\!\ \leftrightarrow A\!:\!\bar{\psi}_{1}\psi_{1}\!:\!

(C.4)

\displaystyle\!:\!e^{-\mathbf{i}\sqrt{4\pi}\varphi}\!:\!\ \leftrightarrow A\!:\!\bar{\psi}_{2}\psi_{2}\!:\!

(C.5)

\displaystyle-\mathbf{i}\partial\varphi\leftrightarrow B\!:\!\bar{\psi}_{2}\psi_{1}\!:\!

(C.6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}\bar{\partial}\varphi\leftrightarrow B\!:\!\bar{\psi}_{1}\psi_{2}\!:\!

(C.7)

where
A=4\pi e^{-\gamma/2}
(with
\gamma
being the Euler–Mascheroni constant) and
B=\sqrt{\pi}
. (In this massless case, this boils down to some simple Cauchy–Vandermonde determinant identities; see
[
4
, (2.69)]
). In the classical case of the sine-Gordon model,
\tilde{\alpha}
is a constant and real-valued. If we replace each term in (
C.2
) by its value under this dictionary in this case, this
suggests
that the sine-Gordon model with real, constant electromagnetic field should indeed be equivalent to the Fermionic measure given by (
4.46
) still with
\tilde{\alpha}
a real constant. Coleman’s original idea to verify this involved an asymptotic expansion of both measures when
z_{0}
(or equivalently
\alpha
) tends to 0. This has since then been proven rigorously under various assumptions on
\Omega,\alpha
at or below the free fermion point (the Coleman correspondence is in fact more general than what we state here). For a more detailed discussion of this, at least in the full plane, we invite the reader to consult
[
4
]
.

Going back to the case of interest where
\tilde{\alpha}
is not constant, we can still use the same equivalence. In that case we find that the law of
\varphi
under
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{SG}(\alpha)}
(see (
C.1
))
is equivalent to the
complex-massive
Dirac field with formal density (
4.46
).

Remark C.1
.

The relation (
C.7
) has an extra minus sign compared to
[
4
]
stemming from a minor sign error in equation (2.11) of Lemma 2.2 in that paper.
This has been amended in the arXiv version of that paper.

Remark C.2
.

As an aside, in the case that
\alpha(x)=\alpha
is constant, but still complex, observe that
\not{\partial}_{\alpha}\not{\partial}_{-\bar{\alpha}}=(\Delta-|\alpha|^{2})I
, hence the associated Green function is

\displaystyle\not{\partial}_{\alpha}^{-1}(w,z)=\not{\partial}_{-\bar{\alpha}}(\Delta-|\alpha|^{2})^{-1}(w,z)I.

(C.8)

Furthermore, in the case of the full plane, we have
(\Delta-|\alpha|^{2})^{-1}(w,z)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{0}(|\alpha||w-z|)
, so

\displaystyle\not{\partial}_{\alpha}^{-1}(w,z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\alpha}\ K_{0}(|\alpha||w-z|)&-2\bar{\partial}_{w}K_{0}(|\alpha||w-z|)\\
-2\partial_{w}K_{0}(|\alpha||w-z|)&\alpha\ K_{0}(|\alpha||w-z|)\end{pmatrix}.

(C.9)

We thus obtain an explicit expression for the associated Green function in this case.

Using Coleman’s correspondence as heuristic, namely (
C.6
) and (
C.7
) above, it is natural to expect that the derivatives of the sine-Gordon model with electromagnetic field have correlations given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{SG}(\alpha)}\Big[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{i}^{-1+2s_{i}}\partial^{(s_{i})}\varphi(x_{i})\Big]

\displaystyle=B^{n}\Big\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\!:\!\bar{\psi}_{2-s_{i}}(x_{i})\psi_{1+s_{i}}(x_{i})\!:\!\Big\rangle.

(C.10)

Using standard computations for Grassmann integrals (see, e.g., Lemma A.2 in
[
4
]
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{SG}(\alpha)}\Big[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{i}^{-1+2s_{i}}\partial^{s_{i}}\varphi(x_{i})\Big]

\displaystyle=B^{n}\det\Big((\not{\partial}_{\alpha})_{2-s_{i},1+s_{j}}^{-1}(x_{i},x_{j})\Big)_{i\neq j},

(C.11)

when the
x_{i}\in\Omega
are pairwise distinct.

C.2
Effect of centering and gauge change

Here we explain how Conjecture (
1.4
) can be rephrased in terms of Coleman’s transform. It was conjectured in
[
6
]
that the limiting height function field
h
has a law given by

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{SG}(\alpha)}(\mathrm{d}h)\propto\exp\left[z_{0}\int_{\Omega}\langle e^{-\mathbf{i}\sqrt{\beta}h(x)},\mathbf{i}\alpha(x)\rangle\mathrm{d}x\right]\mathbb{P}^{\mathrm{GFF}\#}(\mathrm{d}h),

(C.12)

where
\beta=4\pi
, the constant
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
was not identified and might be lattice dependent, and
\mathbb{P}^{\mathrm{GFF}\#}(\mathrm{d}h)
denotes the law of the Gaussian free field on
\Omega
, normalised in such a way that
\mathbb{E}^{\mathrm{GFF}\#}(h(x)h(y))=(-2\pi)^{-1}\log|x-y|+O(1)
as
|y-x|\to 0
. The law
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{mSG}}
defined (
C.12
) is what we call the
sine-Gordon model with external field
. The external field in question refers to the vector field
\alpha(x)
which acts like an external magnetic field, tilting the direction of
e^{\mathbf{i}\sqrt{4\pi}h}
.

To explain how Theorem
1.14
is consistent with this conjecture, it is important to also specify the boundary conditions of the field in
\mathbb{P}^{\mathrm{GFF}\#}
, which are only implicit in
[
6
]
, and which (explicitly) are given by the so-called winding boundary conditions (see, e.g., (2.7) in
[
7
]
). Thus, when
\Omega
is a smooth domain with boundary parametrized by a simple smooth curve
\gamma:[a,b]\to\partial\Omega
which winds anticlockwise along
\partial\Omega
, and
x=\gamma(a)\in\partial D
is a reference point (corresponding to the limit of the removed corner in the Temperleyan construction) we can define a function on the boundary by

u(\gamma(t))=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}u_{0}(\gamma(t));\text{ where }u_{0}(\gamma(t))=W_{\mathrm{int}}(\gamma([a,t])),

the
intrinsic winding
of the boundary between
x
and
\gamma(t)
(see Section 2.1 in
[
7
]
). The constant
(1/\sqrt{4\pi})
in the expression for
u
above (in front of
u_{0}
) is significant, and is not present in
[
7
]
. This reflects the fact that the Gaussian free field in
[
7
]
differs from the one here by a factor of
\sqrt{2\pi}
(owing to different normalisations of the Green function). The extra factor of
\sqrt{2}
is
1/\chi
for
\chi=1/\sqrt{2}
, the imaginary geometry constant associated with
\kappa=2
: using notations of
[
7
]
, the limit of the uncentered height function is
(1/\chi)h^{\mathrm{GFF}}+u_{0}=(1/\chi)(h^{\mathrm{GFF}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}u_{0}))
, where
h^{\mathrm{GFF}}
is a Dirichlet Gaussian free field normalised according to the conventions of
[
7
]
.

It is elementary to check that the function
u
is invariant under orientation preserving reparametrization of
\gamma
and thus depends only on
\Omega
and
x
. The harmonic extension of this function to
\Omega
is then denoted by
u_{(\Omega,x)}
. Thus, if
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
and
x=1
,
u_{(\mathbb{D},1)}(z)=(4\pi)^{-1/2}\arg(z)
, where
\arg
is the determination of the argument which has a jump of
-2\pi
when crossing the positive real line from bottom to top. On the other hand, when
\Omega=\mathbb{H}
is the upper half-plane and
x=0
, say, then
u_{(\mathbb{H},0)}(z)=0
. To isolate the effect of the boundary conditions we thus write

h=\phi+u

where
h
has the law
\mathbb{P}^{\mathrm{GFF}\#}
,
u=u_{(\Omega,x)}
, and
\phi
is a Gaussian free field with Dirichlet boundary conditions (and with covariance normalised similar to above, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}(\tilde{h}(x)\tilde{h}(y))=(-2\pi)^{-1}\log|x-y|+O(1)
). We will write
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{\mathrm{GFF}\#}
for the law of
\tilde{h}
.

Now observe that, since
\langle z,w\rangle=\Re(z\bar{w})
, we can write

\displaystyle\langle e^{-\mathbf{i}\sqrt{\beta}h(x)},\mathbf{i}\alpha(x)\rangle=\Re\left(e^{-\mathbf{i}\sqrt{\beta}(\phi(x)+u(x))}\overline{\mathbf{i}\alpha}(x)\right)=\langle e^{-\mathbf{i}\sqrt{\beta}\phi(x)},\mathbf{i}\tilde{\alpha}(x)\rangle,

where

\tilde{\alpha}(x)=\alpha(x)e^{\mathbf{i}\sqrt{\beta}u(x)}

(C.13)

is a new vector field which corresponds to the electromagnetic field of
\varphi
.
Indeed, under
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{SG}(\alpha)}
, since
\phi=h-u
we have that
\phi
has law given by

\exp\left[z_{0}\int_{\Omega}\langle e^{-\mathbf{i}\sqrt{\beta}\phi},\mathbf{i}\tilde{\alpha}(x)\rangle\mathrm{d}x\right]\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{\mathrm{GFF}}\#}(\mathrm{d}\phi)

Since
u
is a deterministic function, the correlations of the centered height function
h-\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{SG}(\alpha)}(h)
under
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{SG}(\alpha)}
are identical to those of the centered field
\phi-\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{SG}(\alpha)}(\phi)
.
